WOMEN'S GHAT.

The King's Visit to Berlin.—The King will pay a i State visit to the German Emperor at Berlin, next i April, but his Majesty has no intention of visiting the Russian Court this year. Thevisit to Berlin when the King will be accompanied by the Queen, is intended as the compliment to the visit of the Kaiser, when he came to London in 1891 to pay his State visit to Queen Victoria. That was a little more than 3 years after he had become Emperor of Germany, and a similar period will have elapsed between thelKing's State visit to Berlin, and his Majesty's accessibn to the Throne. This is quite according to the custom by which the return calls between sovereigns are regulated by the most exacting measure of courtesy.. The Royal visit to Ireland will follow immediately upon their Majesties return from the,continent, and it is hoped that the King and Queen will be able to attend the Punchestown races in the middle of April. Wintering in Egypt.—Princess Henry of Battenberg is wintering in Egypt, mainly on account of Prince Leopold,her second son, who is very delicate. She has her daughter Princess Ens with her ; and also Princess Beatnice of Saxe • Coburg. The Royal.party have beens spending Christmas at Cairo, occupying the Empres Eugenies suite of apartments at the Ghezirch Palace Hotel. A visit to Upper Egypt has been arranged, and the Khedive has placed his yacht at the service of Princess Henry for the journey up the Nile. This tour extending perhaps as far as the Soudan is of course the great attraction of Egypt, which is becoming more and more popular with the wealthy classes as a winter. resort. A Ducal Privilege.—The Earl Marshal, amongst other isereditaryprivileges, is entitled to an escort of cavalry for any special occasion, and when the Duke of Norfolk married Lady Flora Hastings, his Grace made a formal claim for the escort. His right to it was at once acknowledged, and the Duke was satisfied with this acknola ledgment He did not have the soldiers, nor will they be present at the wedding of the Duke with Miss Maxwell, which will be quietly solemnised on February 10th at Everingham. This is a picturesque village not far from York, where Lord Herries has his principal residence in England. The wedding will unite two families engaged in strife in olden times, as both the 'Towards and the Herries played their parts in the wars between England and Scotland. A Blind Chauffeur.—The grant of a licence to a blind I man as a motor car driver,is not very reassuring to foot- ; passengers. In some districts around London many families with children are removing fromthe much fre- quented main roads, on account of the motor car danger, and, in the locality of this blind driver, this movement will doubtless be accelerated. It is often the case that the loss of one faculty is partly compensated for by ; the greater acuteness of the others, but it scarcely ' seems possible that a blind person could drive a motor car with safety. There was however a well known sportsman who was quite blind, but regularly went out shooting. He could direct his aim by the noise which the birds made in their flight, but in the end he acci- tentally caught the trigger in. his clothing, and shot himself dead.

No one would have "13."—The first number" one" was much sought after in the enumeration of motor cars under the new Act, but nobody would have " 13." In one country this number fell to the lot of a lady motorist, but she flatly declined to accept this unlucky number, and another had to be found for her. The next applicant, a chauffeur made no difficulty about it, but the next day he came back and said that his master's wife and daughter wanted another number as they would never be comfortable with the number "13.' After that, the authorities did not try it again with car owners, and it is now cancelled in that country. The After Christmas Revival.—There is usually a rush to attend the winter sales at the outset for fear that the best bargains may be cleared off, and there has been no lack of excitement and gaiety at the New Year clearance sales. Particularly noticeable has been the ready sale of the white, ivory, and cream satins, for dance and dinner dresses. These have been on offer at anything from 2s. 6d.a yard, whilst exquisite mousseline vaiours have been selling at Gs. or is. a yard. Bargains in tailor-made suits, cycling skirts, and motoring coats, have not been difficult to secure, but the greatest crushes have been in those departments offering up-to- date coats, mantles, boleros, and travelling cloaks. Soon we shall be on the look out for new spring fashions, but the New Year's sales offer an excellent opportunity for renewing winter necessities especially as the better half of winter with its lengthening days, and return of sunshine, is more searching in discover- ing any shabby signs of wear in our outfit. Dressing and Tea Gowns.—Lengths of ordinary flannel, Molleton, lambswool, Pyrenean wool, combinations of silk and wool such.as Zenana cloth, and flanelle de sole, are to be had very cheaply at the sales now on, and the home worker would do well to secure these for either a dressing gown, or tea gown, the Zenana, cloth or flannelle de sole being particularly suitable for the latter garment. Of flannel that is 36 inches in width, from eight to ten yards, according to the height and and size of the prospective wearer, should be bought, but if a double width fabric is chosen half the length only will be needed. It must be remembered that the essential qualities of a dressing gown are—comfort, warmth and ease. It should too be light in weight. A Modish Trimming.—Grapes are much in request again. They adorn our gowns, our headgear, and our coiffure. This particular fruit appears under different disguises, and would seem to be manufactured in fabrics of every sort and kind. Carried out in soft silk, padded to resemble the fruit exactly in size and shape, and made in deep purple and some in pale green, they have quite the colouring and appearance of the fruit of the vine. In raised jet paillettes, with the vine leaves worked in jet, and the tendrils carried out in black chenille these bunches of grapes are most effective, -while another and very escellent idea is to have them in 41111 gold or silver. Twists of chiffon or tulle, and a bunch of grapes are appearing in the fashionable woman's hair, for all evening festivities. Earrings.—Yet another attempt to revive the heavy long earring has been made, but without success. It drags the lobe of the ear out of shape, is inconvenient, and always catching in things. Those women however who possess fine solitaires, or pear-shaped pendent pearls,will be in the van of fashion, if they don them. The Princess of Wales is never seen without ornaments of thisdescription, and to her Royal Highness, as to the majority of women, they are most becoming. A Useful Recipe.—A excellent way in which to serve the remains of a plum pudding is as follows :—Cut the pudding into fingers. and after soaking them in brandy or rum for about fifteen minutes, these should be placed crosswise in a buttered mould. When the shape is full, a custard, consisting of four eggs, to a pint of milk, and very slightly sweetened should be poured in, The mixture must then be covered with buttered paper, and steamed for two hours. The pudding should then be turned out, and sent to table with some thick *ream.

MADGE'.

Four men were blown to pieces by an explosion at the dynamite works near Hayle' in Cornwall, on Tues- day. Much damage was done to the towns of Hayle, Penzance, and St. Ives by the shock of the explosion.

HOW SMALLPDX IS SPREAD.

Failing to Notify Cases at Warrington,

A WARNING.

At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, before Mr. T. Birchall and other magistrates, John McKenzie, a labourer residing at 150, Longford-street, was sum- moned for failing to notify a case of smallpox to the Medical Officer of Health on December 20th.

_ Mr. Stanley Wilson, deputy town clerk, prosecuted and said the proceedings were taken under the Infectious Diseases Notification Act which required, under a penalty of 40s, the head of the family as soon as he became aware that a person in his house was suffering from an infections disease to at once notify the medical officer. On December 24th, Inspector Stephens, acting oa the instructions of the medical officer, visited the defendant's house and saw Mrs. McKenzie. He re- quested to see the daughter Alice who had been attend- ing one of the schools in the town at which cases of small-pox had occurred. The child had been absent from school for some days, and in consequence inquiries were 'made. After some slight opposition from defendant's wife, the Inspector went into the kitchen and saw the child who was eleven years of age. On examining her he found pimples on the face and arms and called the mother's attention te them. The medical officer subsequently visited the house and certi- fied that the girl was suffering from a mild attack of smallpox. She was thereupon removed to the sanatorium. Although a rash had been noticed no steps had been taken to call in a medical man for four days. The proceedings were not taken with the object of inflicting a heavy, penalty. The only desire of the Health Committee was to lay before the public and the heads of families the obligations under which they were placed by the Act; and to emphasise the importance of early notification. It was perfectly useless for the Health Committee to go to so much trouble and spend a large sum of money with the object of preventing the spread of disease if people were allowed to neglect their duty in the matter. Dr. Gornall gave evidence and said that the girl was suffering from a mild attack of smallpox, in his opinion of some days duration. He had evsry reason to believe that defendant knew it was smallpox. Defendant addressed the magistrates and asked them if they thought any malt with common sense would harbour a case of smallpox in his house. He did not know the child had smallpox and even at prdsent he doubted whether she had. If he had thought there was any need for a doctor he would have called one ion. Defendant's wife gave evidence and said the girl had to be pointed out to the official who removed her to the sanatorium. Another child was in the house and he asked "which he had to take."

James Hardman, labourer, 84, Chorley-street, was then charged with a similar offence.

Mi.. Wilson said the defendant's wife was admitted to the sanatorium on December 27th, suffering from a marked attack of smallpox. It was admitted that the rash was noticed on the 24th but the case was not notified until three days afterwards. Defendant was related to the occupants of 46, Chorlev street, and in- formation the authorities had received led them to the coaclusion that the members of defendant's family had been in the habit of visiting thehouse fromwhich seven patients had been removed to the sanatorium. Defend- ant lived in a small house with three bedrooms and there were thirteen occupants. The case, therefore, exemplified the importance of immediate notification. Defendant-stated that he called in Dr. Peacocke on the 27th. Until that date he was not aware that his wife was suffering from smallpox, in fact Dr. Peacocke kad visited the house on the previous Thursday for vaccination•purposes. Mr. Wilson intimated that he did not wish the defendants to be punished severely and he was pre- pared to withdraw the cases if the magistrates approved of that course.

Mr. Birchall said the bench were willing that the cases should be withdrawn but: they wanted to impress upon the public that failure to notify itfectious diseases was a most serious matter. If any other eases were brought before them they would be severely dealt with.

ALIVE AFTER 3,300 -VOLTS THROUGH THE BRAIN.

A man employed by the Westinghouse Electric Com- pany has survived the terrible experience of having 3,300 volts of electricity shot through his brain—just twice,the amount used "for executing American mur- derers. He was attending to some live wires ona plat- form, and, on stepping back, be slipped, but to save himself grasped hold of the main wires. The unfortunate man's body straightened up, and, as he stiffened like a corpse, his head came near enough to still other wires to form a complete circuit. Onlookers saw a stream of blue flame 18 inches long and 6 inches wide roaring out of his head with the noise of an electric car. This flame melted the iron pipe above, and the molten metal dropped white and hissing on the back of his head. A workman:bravely launched himself against the burning man, thereby breaking the circuit. Experts afterwards stated that no less than 3,300 volts had passed through the man's body. He is now on the way to recovery, and says his experience on the wire was that of a terrible nightmare

A WHOPPER !--It, is surely unnecessary to specify the place of origin of the following veracious incident. An inhabitant of a certain town (we are told) made his living by hauling timber, and used rawhide harness on his team of horses. Rawhide,we all know,expands when wet and contracts on drying. Well. this man was haul,' ing an exceptionally big load one day when a thunder- storm overtook him just as he reached a long hill lead- ing to his home. He was walking beside his team that he might pay close attention to them, and didnot notice what the rain was doing for his rawhide harness until the top of the long hill was reached, when, to his utter amazement, the waggon stood at the bottom of the hill, the rawhide tugs being fairly stretched into fiddle strings. He was puzzled for a moment, hot just then the sun came out, and a happy idea found lodgment in his fertile brain. He unhitched the team,,,fastened the tugs securely around a big tree, and went in to dinner. When he returned the sun had caused the rawhide to contract sufficiently to bring the waggon up that steep hill with a momentum that smashed both fcont wheels when it came in contact with the tree around which the tugshad been fastened. That kind of elastic rawhide is evidently the most suitable material for stringing the long-bow.

The Examiner Saturday, January 9, 1904.

learnt, all the carnage anti manner or a Loniton man in those dreadful backwoods, or wherever it is the diamond mines are."

"1 call him simply fascinating," said the American widow, who was scarcely in Lady Little- wood's set, but who had been invited to stay at Bedington Hall because it was known that she was rolling in money, and the impecunious Algy Redfern was doing his best to secure her for his own by dangling before her eyes the ducal coronet to which he was heir presumptive. "In any case she is rolling in diamonds," remarked the Honourable Maude Edgerley, who had had a long and sufficiently hopeless flirtation with Algy herself before the American had dawned on the scenes. "She is simply covered in ' them, as the upholsterers say. What she cannot wear in her ears and her hair, or on her neck and her arma and her fingers, she has sewn all over her gown, and her maid has to sit up half the night•unsewing them. Mrs. Merton has discovered a remarkably smart way of taking care of them. She carries them in a chamois-lined pocket by day—at least, such of them as she does not put on for breakfast —and sleeps with it under her pillow by night." This was confided to Bexley during dinner by the Honourable Maude, who had promptly decided on traasferring her affection to Sir Geoffrey's heir in default' of the deserting Algy, and had begun the running in very fine style, making it difficult for anyone else to get in a word with him edgewise.

Bexley owned that he had noticed the American's diamonds—as, indeed, who would not ?—it being difficult at any time of the day to see anything else while the widow was by, at least until the eyes grew accustomed to the glare, and became aware that there was a lady behind them. The conversation had turned on diamonds naturally at dinner, since Bexley was an expert, and he not only promised to shew those which he had brought back with him from Kimberley, but talked learnedly about the difference, between Brazilian and South African diamonds, of the various methods of cutting and polishing them, and referred especially to Mrs. Merton's jewels as examples of American setting, which he considered displayed the stones so much better than English or French methods.

"I have often heard my father speak of the Littlewood diamonds," he continued, turning to his grandmother, who made a point of wearing as few jewels as possible while the American lady was by. "They are, many of them, unique, are they not, and of historical interest? That clasp I recognise as one I know by repute." "Yes, this belonged to Marie d'Este, and was ,given by her to your ancestress. I only keep out a few for ordinary wear. The real family diamonds do not see the light except at Court or on very great occasions."

"Such as an election dinner," laughed Sir Geoffrey. :,"She always appears in her war paint for the Conservative candidate." ;

"You must wear; them all the ball to-morrow," exclaimed Miss Edgerley, feeling now a personal interest in them. "No occasion could be so im- portant as the return of the long"-lost heir, could it ? "

"Oh, have I come in for festivities after all," exclaimed Bexley, pith a grimace, "when I thought I was going to sneak quietly home without anyone knowing of it ? " "It is only a dinner and ball," replied Lady Littlewood. "Our own friends, not a county affair. It was impos'sible to postpone it. I think I will keep the diamonds for your home-coming festivities, which we must arrange for later on." "We will persuade Lady Littlewood to give us a private view, then," suggested Miss Edgerley. "I have never seen the half of them, but those I haire seen would make the American woman cut her throat from envy." "It is best to keep them out of sight, then," replied Bexley, laughing, as the ladies prepared to leave the room. "It would be a bad omen for ray home-coming to have a suicide in the house."

FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

There are no novelties to be noted at this season of the year for the winter fashions ass now definitely settled, and it is yet too early to enquire into early spring modes. Still in spite of the lack of anything fresh there are plenty of beautiful and charming garments of all sorts to delight the eye and lure the unwary into undreamt of and possibly unnecessary ex- travagances. Blouses are this year exception- ally charming and are to be obtained ready made so beautifully tucked and trimmed or inserted with lace that it is hardly worth while making them at home. The girl of medium height and build can very easily be fitted with a ready made blouse. It is only those who are tall and stout who must not invest in any blouse which has not been cut and fitted for them personally. . There are some very pretty

A LACE COLLAR.

There are some pretty collar bands of real lace made with a deep vandyke .of lace with the points downwards which will be found useful by anyone with a thin neck as they are in- tended for wear with evening toilettes and are fastened here and there with pretty little brillig,nt brooches and pins. The accompany- ing sketch is made of one of these pretty and useful novelties, by which it will be seea that the ugly part of a thin neck where the collar bone is apt to prove unpleasantly visible is softened and concealed by the points of lace. No blouse or bodice is complete nowadays Without a pretty little collar or band of lace, and there are some wonderfully good imita- tions of real lace to be had at very small cost. The demand for real lace is, I believe, on the increase, but the majority of girls cannot afford such a luxury as it is too costly an item for the ordinary dress allowance to embrace. The dainty bows of white or cream tulle whick proved so becoming last season are still fancied as a finish to an afternoon toilette, and when skilfully adjusted and held in place by little ornamental pins look particularly nice. There are some in net with large chenille spots in colours, but these are hardly so chic as those in plain tulle.

THE SECRET OF THE BUHL CABINET.

BY L. lIIGGIN, Author of "A Cornish Maid," (;ousin jer;l

"Lyona Grimwood. Spinstei, &c.

There was excitement in the village of Old Bedington: at the blacksmith's shop at the corner, where the road to the Hall branched off from the highway to Smalcombe, at least six people, not customers, were hanging about at the doors of the cottages perched on the high banks of what was called by courtesy "the street," women stood looking up the road for the chance of a passing glimpse of the carriage which was known to have gone to Smalcombe railway station to Ineet Sir Geoffrey Littlewood's heir just returned from foreign parts, where .he had, to use a Scotch expression, "gone a-missing " for some ten or fifteen years. It had been said by the Rector of Old Bedington, who was what the residents of that enlightened village called "an outlander," that these sunken roads—for they were all some fifteen feet below the level of the fields surrounding them—had once been Oh the same level, but that the mud carried off on the boots of succeeding passengers had reduced them to their present condition, resembling river beds rather than country lanes. However that might be, the cottagers on the higher level had the inestimable advantage of looking down on the mere traveller along their route, and, as we all know, there is an immense satisfaction in being able to look down on anything. An "outlander " in the parlance of Old Bedington was anyone who had not beep born and bred in the place, and was not, therefore, connected by blood with the Skitralls and Spanswicks and Vivashes, who generally peopled the countryside. One of the most noted outlanders had come as a lad forty- eight years ago to work on the new railway then being constructed; he had settled in the village when his work ended, and had married an Old Bedington woman, but he was "only an outlander" after all, and his opinion was not worth having. So that when old Miles had remarked that the bowing and smiling young gentleman who had just driven by the blacksmith's in the squire's carriage, was like none of the "family," a contrary opinion was instantly set up by the Old Bedington people, and those who had not been fortunate enough to obtain even a passing glance at the new-corner, were told that he was "the very spit" of his grandfather, and anyone would recognise him im- mediately. Old Bedington was a little sore about the coming of Sir Geoffrey Littlewood's heir. It had looked confidently for a general holiday with a dinner at the White Hart, a- deputation of tenants, and the celebrated Old Bedington band in its new uniform. to meet the train at Smalcombe—probably the horses to be taken out and the heir drawn up to the Hall door by enthusiastic labourers on the estate— and after all he had arrived just like any of the other visitors who were staying at Bedington Hall, and there was no talk of a holiday, or even a dinner so far. _ _ _

Old Miles had suggested bonfires as a likely means of attracting Sir Geoffrey's attention and of shewing their delight, but though Miles did work on the estate, it was only as a mole-catcher; and, being an outlander, there were those who said he had a method of preserving his moles so that he was always able to impale more than he actually caught and had a right to be paid for. The moles stuck up on the hedges certainly had a knack of becoming very rapidly dried up and decayed, but Miles kept his own counsel, and if he had a method of exchanging old moles for new ones it was only to be expected of a man not bred in the country, nor related to any Old Bedingtonite. The truth of the matter was that the advent of Bexley Littlewood had taken the Hall as much by surprise as the village. Sir Geoffrey had been unfortunate in his sons. The eldest had been killed in the hunting-field within a few months of his marriage, and his son had been born and brought up in his grandfather's house, only to die in his turn from a chill caught in training for the boat-race, when at Oxford, some twelve months ago. The second son had been somewhat of a ne'er- do-weel, and had been shipped off years ago to the Colonies, where he had married. He died a violent death from an accident, and it was his son, Bexley, who had been searched for and at last found, to the great relief of his grandfather, whose one fear was to see both the title and estate pass to distant relatives at his death. For the last two months uncertainty had ceased. Bexley was found and was coming home from the diamond fields in South Africa, and Bedington Hall was quite prepared to kill the fatted calf and rejoice over him. But while they had understood that he was waiting to wind up affairs and realise his gains at Kimberley, an unexpected telegram informed Sir Geoffrey that he had actually landed in England and would arrive at Smalcombe station by the 7.15 train on the evening when our story opens.

Neither Sir Geoffrey nor Lady Littlewood would have chosen to have the house filled with ordinary three day visitors When their grandson was to arrive, but they could not well ask them to shorten their visits. So it came to pass that the heir arrived only just in time to dress for dinner, and his reception had to be shorn of much of its impor- tance. Sir Geoffrey met him on the steps of the entrance. hall, visibly nervous, and somewhat' excited, had taken him into his own library and made him welcome to the home of his ancestors, and his grandmother had shortly afterwards rustled in, resplendent in ruby velvet and old point lace, and just enough of diamond ornaments to set off her dress ,without any appearance of displaying her • jewels. "Now, my boy, I am afraid you must go up to your room and make ready for dinner," said the old man. "I am sorry we have the house full of company, but they will all clear out on Friday and then we will have a little quiet time to become acquainted. ". Bexley had kissed his grandmother's hand with a quaint, somewhat old-fashioned courtesy which had charmed her. She drew him to her and looked in his face for some likeness to the son who had been all the dearer to her because he was always in disgrace. "You are like your mother's family, I sup- pose? " she said. " "Yon must tell me all about her later on." Then she had kissed his forehead and sent him off to dress with a sigh. •

In the drawing-room before -dinner he was intro- duced to the guests and received an effusive welcome from one and all, and his health was drunk with full honours before the ladies left the dinner-table. In all this Bexley bore himself with just the right mixture of modesty and pride in his ancestry which was to be expected from a young man suddenly transferred from the rough life at the diamond mines to one of the stateliest of English mansions.

His dress was perfect; lle explained that he had just snatched time in London to provide himself with the garments of civilisation, and there was nothing of the colonial nor of the backwoodsman in his manner or conversd ion. , To be sure he used a good many more or less unknown expressions, im- ported no doubt from the diamond fields, but they had rather the effect of being purposely' introduced into his conversation than of slipping out in- advertently. . Sir Geoffrey experienced a feeling of intense -relief Which was fully shared by his wife. They had been so terribly afraid that this unknown grand- son would appear on the scene in the character of a rough diamond himself! They had intended to have him all to themselves at the beginning, and not to present him to-the outer world until they had had some opportunity of smoothing off the angles with which they expected him to be plenti- fully furnished. It even transpired in the•course of dinner that he was a Conservative in politics, which its more than Sir Geoffrey had dared to hope; and the old .man's spirits rose a§ it became more and more evident that his hitherto nknown grandson and heir was able to hold his own among the somewhat supercilious guests. Only Lady Littlewood sighed as she watched him, and felt, in some undefined way, that he would never take the place of her scapegrace in her heart ; he was far too much at his ease, too self- reliant. One of the charms of her own Bexley had been his transparent simplicity. "Nobody's enemy but. his own," people had said of him. However, the concensus of opinion was entirely favourable. ..

"He is simply charming, dear Lady Littlewood," remarked Lady Angela Brooklyn, who was certainly an authority on such subjects. "So distinguished in appearance and so thoroughly up to date. He rust be extraordinarily clever to have kept up, or

===== 0002601_19040109_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
111.

The next day was spent by the squire in taking )iis grandson over the estate and introducing him to the tenants and employes. He earned golden Opinions by his genial manner. He spoke of him- self as a working-man who was no stranger to hard labour and scant fare in the days when he had no thought of being his grandfather's heir, or of ever seeing the old home he knew so well from his father's descriptions. The dinner and ball went off capitally. Mr. Littlewood was pronounced to dance divinely, and there was but one opinion among the guests as to the acquisition he would prove to the neighbour- hood. As for Maude Edgerley she was already consoled for the defection of Algy Redfern. Bexley entered into the flirtation which she had initiated on the night of his arrival with all the fervour that could be desired. Already the Little- wood diamonds graced her brow in fancy. No doubt Lady Littlewood would let her wear them when she was once Bexley 's wife, and that desirable contingency seemed to be within quite a measurable distance.

"And that odious #American shall hide her diminished head! Even if Algy ever does succeed to the dukedom, which I doubt, the Redfern jewels are nothing to boast of; they are all quite new like her own."

So soliloquised the young lady in the sanctity of her own room. But, she remembered that Mrs. Merton was leaving Bedington the next day, and she had fully resolved to humiliate her successful rival, by the discomfiture of having to own her own diamonds a mere nothing compared with those which Maude now confidently counted on one day wearing. She had taken Bexley into her confidence, and, much to his apparent amusement, had made Lady Littlewood promise to give a private view of the historical jewels in ,her own boudoir. The place of keeping of these diamonds was a profound secret, not even any of the servants knowing that they were ever kept in the house, and Bexley advised his grandmother that there should be no divulging of the secret on this occasion. Even Maude Edgerley was only to be admitted after the various trays and cases had been laid on the centre table in ..the boudoir.

"It is impossible to be too careful in such a case as this. One never knows what pressure may be brought to bear on the most unlikely people to get at such a valuable secret," he said, and Sir Geoffrey, who was to be of the party, acquiesced. Bexley himself was delighted with the clever arrangements which had for some two hundred years kept all the family treasures so safely. Bedington Hall had been filled by one of its possessors with antique cabinets and bureaux of immense value. Every room had one or two of these, but the "blue parlour," and her ladyship's boudoir which opened out of it, possessed the lion's share. Among a number of these antique cabinets one of .the least remarkable, perhaps, was a beauti- ful buhl chest with massive ormolu enrichments. It opened in quite an ordinary way, though with a beautiful key of antique pattern, and disclosed a silver-lined cavity the full size of the case, apparently a wine cooler. IL was only on moving four of the 'ormolu orna- ments on the outside and touching a hidden spring that the bottom of the chest gradually rose up till it reached the top, when it stopped with a sharp click. Again a manipulation with new keys and the top lifted off, displayinc, tray after tray, fitting perfectly one over the other, in which the great diamonds as well as other priceless jewels lay. A long-drawn sigh of admiration escaped from Bexley. One by one the trays were lifted out °and placed on the table and the chest was closed again before any of the spectators were admitted. The chest was securely screwed on to the floor, and the secret well sunk into a place specially built for it below. To remove the chest was as clearly im- possible as to learn the intricacies of its menu- fac'zure, except to the owners and the possessors Of its secrets.

The Honourable Maude Edgerley was more than satisfied with the effect, produced on the American Widow •by the sight of jewels which made her Own appear the mere possessions of a schoolgirl. To think that Lady Littlewood could have all these lying close at hand and yet never wear more than a clasp or two or a couple of rings was more than her philosophy dreamt of. Worst of all she remembered how ridiculous she had made herself by carrying her "two or three diamonds "—as she now contemptuously called them—about her person, and allowing herself to be drawn into talking of them as if no one else had anything half so fine. And there was that odious girl scarcely concealing her triumph:Ant contempt for her, and looking forward to the day when, as Mrs. Littlewood, .she would probably be allowed to wear them. The pill was altogether too bitter for Mrs. Merton. She complained of sudden illness and retired to her bed, for once omitting to place the pocket contain- ing her jewels under her pillow—an omission which she had some reason to regret since sne never saw them again ! And, after all, they were a nucleus, the beginning of a collection, as she remembered when the first bitterness had passed.

The household was made aware of the loss of the American lady's diamonds by piercing shrieks which rang through the corridor in which her bed- room was situated, about nine o'clock the followine morning, and penetrated even to the servants' quarters far distant. _ _

- In a few minutes host and hostess, guests and servants alike were gathered together in their anxiety to learn the cause of the uproar. "Oh, it's the diamonds, the diamonds!" gasped Mrs. Merton's French. maid. "Gone! Vanished I" and she wrung her hands in quite the orthodox manner even while she kept guard over her mistress's door. Mrs. Merton had not put on quite all that usually met the eye in figure and decora- tion, and until her curled front was adjusted and other little necessary additions made, even the loss of her jewels was not enough to make her appear. Presently, however, she sallied forth and poured her harrowing tale into the ears of the assembled crowd.

She had felt so ill last night when she went to bed that though she had put her jewels in her loose pocket she had omitted to place it under her pillow. Left it on the dressing-table, she thought, and when she remembered to look for it, it was gone. _ _ _

Adele, for her part, was quite certain that it had never been on the table at all, or she must have seen it. She supposed it to be, as usual, under her mistress's pillow till a quarter of an hour ago when Mrs. Merton raised the alarm.

"Oh, it has slipped down somewhere about the bed," suggested Lady Littlewood. But no, the strictest search revealed nothing. • "Where is Bexley?" asked Sir Geoffrey. "We must wire for police at once." _ _ _

Mr. LittlewOod was out. It was understood that Le had gone on his bicycle early. Miles had met him in the park soon after dawn. He had stopped and spoken to the old mole-catcher, and had said lie was off for a spin before anyone Was up, he liked the Enghill morning air so much. • This appeared to Miles a curious taste for any- one who might have been in bed, but the half-crown the young squire gave him was quite in his line. At this stage of the inquiry the Honourable Alande Edgerley shewed alarming symptoms of bvsttfia, and demanded to see Sir Geoffrey and Lady Littlewood alone. She had a curious tale to unfold. It seemed that she had made tender inquiries after the American lady's headache the night before, and had even gone to her room to offer some remedy—to gloat over her discomfiture. as Mrs. Merton had interpreted it. As she was leaving the room she had seen the fatal pocket lying on a chair, and in a fit of mis- chief picked it up and carried it off. She had promptly looked up Bexley and had let him into the secret of this excellent practical joke. "I thought it would be such fun to frighten the odious creature," she sobbed. "But Bexley was quite shocked, and insisted on their being returned at once. My grandfather would never forgive such a joke being played on one of his guests, he said. So he took the pocket and promised to replace it without anyone ever knowing," she finished. "Bexley knows where it is." But then Bexley was gone out, and we may as well mention that he never returned. _ _

It was a long time before the truth dawned on Sir Geoffrey that his pretended grandson was a professional burglar, or, at least, jewel thief; and when it did, there was no room for doubt that the Littlewood diamonds had, fled in company with those of Mrs. Merton. It was with sick hearts and trembling fingers that he and Lady Littlewood shut themselves up in the boudoir to examine the secret chest. Their worst fears were realised when they found the lock shot indeed, but the chest open. Then they remembered that the man who had personated their grandson had locked up the chest, or appeared to do so, at each stage. Yes, every lock was open. There was no longer a shadow of doubt. Sir Geoffrey touched the springs and the inner chest slowly rose to sight; the top of that was also unlocked. Ile scarcely dared to take off the cover, but when he did so both stood Amazed. The Jewels were apparently untouched, but on the top of the first tray lay, an open letter. It ran thus "To Sir Geoffrey and Lady Littlewood,—Yes, when you find this you will know for certain that I am a thief, and you will tremble for your price- less jewels. They are all safe; not one is missing. I have placed this letter here just to show you that they have all been within my reach. I could as easily have carried them off as those of the American widow.

"I came ; I saw. I could have conquered. But I was conquered instead by the fine courtesy, the generous trust of the old English gentleman whose grandson I personated: but not by that alone. Under those yearning eyes, always searching mine for some semblance of the son who had never been anything but a grief to her, with her gentle kisses on my brow, my whole nature revolted from the deed I had come to do. I am a gentleman by birth, but it is long since I have been in the com- pany of my own clais—a good woman I have never known before.

"Chance put the American woman's jewels in my way. I must have some means of getting away, and I want a new start in life. Please tell the lady I have taken her jewels, worth about fifteen thousand pounds, as a loan. If I get on as I mean to do she shall have them, or their worth, again. "To no-one will I ever divulge the secret of the Buhl Cabinet. It will remain a perfectly safe restinc,-Dlace for the Littlewood diamonds." [Tax Exe.l

THE SPORT OF KILLING. Large quantities of foreign partridges and pheasants and red deer are arriving at Liverpool to the order of Mr. William Cross, the well-known naturalist for replenishing the exhausted game preserves in this country. Owing to in-breeding, British deer and birds are stated to be dying out fast, their extinction being also hastened by the practice of landlords letting their shootings to multi-millionaires, whose one idea of " sport' is to kill as Tally head of game as possible.— It is predicted that within the next few. years British deer and partridges and pheasants will be extinct.

EVENTS OF THE WEEK.

CHICAGO FIRE.

The appalling fire at the Iroquois Theatre fias sent a thrill of horror thraughout the whole world, and it is probably the most awful catastrophe of its kind that has ever (yenned. Even now the death role is incom- plete : but it is certain that over six hundred persons, mostly women and children, lost their lives in a few minutes, whilst almost an 'equal number are missing and injured. The disaster occurred all in a minute, and a panic `followed. To begin with, the theatre at this matinee was filled even beyond its seating ,:.apacity, and held some 1,706 peopls. The theatre itself is brand new and only opened on INovember 23rd, and it was supposed to be furnished in every way with all the latast improvements and safeguards against fire. The play was a pantomime, called "Mr. Bltn Beard," which was in the main identical -with the "Blue Beard" played at Drury Lane

THE IROQUOIS THEATRE, T - FIRE.

two years ago, the scenery having been trans- ported wholesale to Chicago. In the second act there was a moonlight chorus, sun.- with the lights turned down, and it was sung this that the flames broke out, probably ,owing to the tusi gof an electric wire. An attempt to let down the fire-proof curtain failed, as it stuck half-way down and only 'served as a " blower," drawing the fire under it into the house in one vast flame. This completed the panic, and the fight for life began. Women and children were burnt to death in their seats, whilst others were trampled to death or jammed in the exits: and the scene was appalling. As a result, 'Chicago is a city of mourning, as many thousands of families have lost some relative I and all some friend or other. Some of the Officials of the theatre have been arrested, Pending the inquest which is being held.

THE CONQUERING TURBINE.

Whether the turbine is yet qualified to -supersede the cylinder on battleships is a Point the Admiralty are p‘Tsistently putting to a practical test, though so far their experi- mental turbine craft have been unlucky. It 18, of course, the ill-fated destroyers Cobra anc:l Viper that are referred to, both of Which met an early and disastrous end. Now we are soon to see a turbine cruiser in our spiadrons in the shape of the Amethyst, Which has been recently launched at Elswick. The Amethyst, the first regular turbine war- ship, has a displacement of 3,000 tons, and is an unarmoured but protected vessel, being provided with a complete armoured deck, but not having any side plating. She is to be fitted with three of Parson's turbine

THE FIRST TURBINE CRUISER, THE AMETHYST. engines, which will be supplied with steam from -the Yarrow water - tube boilers, but it is not expected that any remarkable -speed will be attained, only about twenty- two knots being hoped for on her trials, a Slower speed than that of many cruisers fitted with the older pattern of machinery- The Amethyst must, however, be regarded more -as an experimental craft than anything else, and if she proves a success it is possible that we may see great development in tur- bine warship building. Her armament will not be heavy, but her battery will comprise a good many pieces of comparatively small calibre, consisting, as it will do, of ,twelve 4in., eight 3-pounders, and two Maxim guns, besides a couple of torpedo im. pulse tubes. • The Ainethyst will carry 300 tone of coal, and a crew of 230 officers and men.

CHATSWORTH VISIT.

This week, since Monday, the King and Queen fiave been visiting the Duke and Duchess of Devonshire at Chatsworth, their magnificent seat in Derbyshire, and they organised a great programme of festivities for their Royal guests. It will be remembeted that their Majesties were to have gone to Chatsworth last Spring and great preparations had been made, when an hour or two before he was to have started the King became suddenly ill and had to postpone the visit by telegram at the last minute. Now he is making amends, as great disappointment was felt throughout the neigh- bourhood, which was delighted this week at the fulfilment of the long postponed visit. It is just over sixty years since a Duke and Duchess of Devonshire had the honour of entertaining a reigning Sovereign at Chatsworth, though the King, when Prince of Wales, has been there sveral times. The previous ,occasion -°' late Queen

CHATSWORTH. Victoria and the Prince Consort stayed with the grandfather of the present Duke: The Principal events of this week's programme were shooting and amateur theatricals. The Shooting was over the Bakewell, Ashford and Besley Coverts, and an expedition for lunch was made to Hardwick Hall, the Duke of Devonshire's other Derbyshire seat. The theatricals were organised, as usual, by Mr. Leo Trevor, who wrote a little pantomime on the subject of Cinderella," a clever little piece which plays just over the hour. Mrs. 'William James appeared in the title-role, whilst Miss Muriel Wilson and the Princess 1-lenrY of Pless, two of the prettiest women in the country, played the "ugly sisters" : and Sir Hedworth Williamson was the Prince, Mr. Leo Trevor himself playing the fairy godmother.

Sir George Compton Reade, eighth baronet of an old family, once masters of Shipton Court, Oxfordshire, follows the plough on a little farm in the middle west ° of the United States, living the life of a small farmer. grounds had descended Ivith the tit4e. SIR GEORGE COMPTON READE AND HIS

PRESENT HOME AT DEXTER, MICHIGAN. of• fifty years in the neighbourhood of his present home, whither his father removed when George was a little boy. There he married a farmer's daughter, there his children were born, grew up, and were married, and to all his neighbours he was known as plain George Reade. He is tall and well built, and carries his fifty-eigght years well.

MR. ALGERNON ASRXON.

The new year sees the press without a cen- sor. Not that it has ever had an official censor, as was the case cluing the war in South Africa; but in Mr. Algernon Ashton, whose signature is familiar to every editor, it possessed a self-constituted critic of errors and mistakes, as enthusiastic as he was unre- munerated. But now, after some twenty years of active service, Mr. Ashton has announced his intention to re- sign his volun- tary post; and with the en- trance of 1904 he has decided to forswear his peculiar hobby. So, unless he slips back again owing to some egregious edi- torial error, which might once more stir Ins blood to MR. ALGERNON ASHTON battle and criti- cism, in future his pen will remain listlessly in its tray. Once before, in 1901, he an- nounced the same intention at the commence- ment of the century, and for a whole year he was faithful to his vow. But then he began again in 1902 with renewed energy, with the result that the past year, with its return of cicse upon one hundred letters, has proved almost the heaviest in his record. Mr. Ash- ton's first letter to the Press was addressed to the "Pall Mall Gazette" in 1883, and dealt with the question of Cruickshank's grave. Since then he has written many hundreds, of which many have dealt with the subject of the graves of famous men. Much that he has written has been very interestiqg ; and his letters, so suddenly stopped, will be quite missed, as much by editors as the general public.

ARCHBISHOP BOURNE.

An event of the utmost importance and interest to Roman Catholics in Great Britain was the enthronement of Dr. Bourne as the Archbishop of Westminster, in succession to the late Cardinal Manning. It took place last week at the recently - opened Westminster Cathedral, which is now almost completely decorated. The ceremony was a gorgeous pageant and most impressive, and a great host of Catholics was gathered to do homage to the new Archbishop. By a coincidence it was the anniversary of the Feast of St. Thomas of Canterbury, and great interest was aroused by the presence of the mitre of Thomas A'Becket in a glass prieu-Dieu. The ceremony itself was in three parts—the reception of the Arch- bishop, the enthronement, and the celebration

of Mass, followed by the bestowal of the Papal blessing. But the pallium, the emblem of ecclesiastical authority in England, had been personally conferred upon Dr. Bourne by the Pope during his recent visit to Rome, and thus the necessity of Papal legates was obvi- ated. This pallium is a small Y-shaped stole of lambs' wool blessed at the Church of St. Agnes during the singing of the " Agnus Del." The Archbishop was incensed by the Provost. and then a procession was formed to the altar. And then followed the enthronement in full canonicals, and the homage of the clergy, after which the Archbishop of West- minster celebrated mass. After the Gospt..l he delivered a pastoral address, dealing mainly with the education question.

THE NORTHERN UNION.

FIRST DIVISION.

LEEDS v. BROUGHTON RANGERS.

The return match was played at Leeds, before a good number of spectators. In the first game at Broughton Leeds were defeated by six points, but since that time they have shown such improved form that they are well in the running for the _championship of the League. Oram missed placing an easy penalty goal, and at half- time neither side had scored. The second half was very evenly contested. Fine efforts were made by Jenkins and Llewellyn on one side and Harris on the other, but all• attempts to cross the line were frustrated, and the game ended in a pointless draw.

===== 0002601_19040109_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
SALFORD v. BATLEY

A very one-sided game was played between these clubs, on the Salford ground. Lomas quickly found out the weak spots in the Batley defence, and when- ever he got the slightest chance he was . over their line. In the first half he scored two tries and kicked two goals and another try was scored by Tunney, so that at the interval Salford had a lead of thirteen points. The game in the second half was more of a farce than anything else. Batley although having the wind with them, were even weaker than they were in the first half, and their line was con- tinually being crossed. Lomas scored two more tries and kicked four goals, making his total for the match six goals and four tries, whilst other tries were scored by White, Heath, and Bedford. Final score :—Salford, seven goals and nine tries (41 points) ; Batley, nothing.

===== 0002601_19040109_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
HULL V. OLDHAM.

Oldham, playing at Hull, were the first to score with a penalty goal. Hull were playing brilliantly, but after a prolonged attack were beaten off. Tyson scored single-handed for Oldham, and Hull pressed to the interval without result. Half-time :—Oldham, five points; Hull, nothing. There was some good kicking afterwards, find then the match settled down to scrum- maging at midway. The Hull forwards got possession much more frequently than their opponents, but the backs could not get away with the ball. Oldham got away, and Carpenter was very near scoring, but was sent into touch. Oldham got possession from a scrummage, and Dixon scored, Carpenter failing to improve. After some midfield play Tyson dropped a splendid goal. Hull made a last effort, and some keen play was witnessed in the Oldham 25, but Spottagwoode removed all danger with a tong kick to midway. Just before the close Owen ran in a try on the right wing after a long kick across. Carpenter failed at goal. Result :—Oldham, two goals and three tries (13 points); Hull, nothing.

BRADFORD ‘ . WIDNES.

At Bradford. The ground had been protected from frost. The first half was fairly well contested, the only score being a try by Linton for Bradford. On resuming Bradford went away with a rush, and Lalley, knocking the ball forward, caused a scrummage close to his line. From this Heseltine sent the ball to Dunbavin, who got over. WHY HAVE BILE BEANS SUCCEEDED?

Simply because of their unequalled curative proper- ties. People always prefer the best—you do yourself. A few years ago Chas. Forde's bile beans were stocked only by a few of the leading and most far seeing Eng- lisb.chemists. To-day every medicine vendor of note in the British Isles, and in fact the whole world over, stocks them, and they are the most widely used family medicine. . . .

Their origin and their increase is most interesting. Some years ago Chas. Forde, an eminent Australian scientist, discovered a purely vegetable substance which has the peculiar property of acting on the human system in exactly the same way as nature's animal substance, bile. After much careful study he combined this sub- stance with some eight other vegetable ingredients, and then concentrated the product so obtained until a suit- able dose could be compressed into the space offered by a small bean. This medicine, made up in the form of a "bean for the bile," soon became widely known as "Bile Beans." Their use simply revolutionised the old method of pepsin, bismuth, or mercury closing, and of starving sufferers from liver trouble and indiges- tion by placing them on liquid diet. Bile beans do not give temporary relief only, and leave the patient weakened, like the out-of-date, so-called remedies of fixity or fifty years ago, containing probably aloes, mer- cury, and other harmful drugs. Bile beans, without the slightest discomfort, prompt the liver and digestive organs to act in nature's normal way, leaving those organs strengthened and stimulated. They produce a gentle action on the bowels, curing or preventing constipation, cleansing the stomach, and ridding the system of all impurities. Do not be misled by claims of half a hundred pills in the box, where probably four to six constitute a dose, and the doses cannot be discontinued. One bile bean „is one dose. They can be discontinued after the cure is effected; they are purely vegetable; they do not contain any harmful drugs, and they are the safest family medicine. The fortnightly meeting of the division was held on Saturday evening in the Greenway road schoolroom. After the ordinary business, the newly-elected officers were installed by the Grand Scribe, Bra. George Powell, P. W. P. He had done his duty as an officer, and he had much pleasure in handing him the emblem. Bros. D. Bisbrown, T. Wyte, and others supported the resolution and sp, Ace in com- plimentary terms of Bro. Powell. He had, they said, worked his way in the division from t.cadet, and had now passed through all the impoytant offices. Bro. Powell thanked the brethren for their kind remarks, and said that the work had been a pleasure to him. It had been educational, and had made him abetter temper- ance advocate.

===== 0002601_19040109_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOD AND EMPIRE.

DO THE COLONIES DEMAND A SACRIFICE?

WHAT COULD THEY GIVE IN RETURN P

The Price of Empire. THE world was startled when the Colonial Secretary went down to Birmingham (May 15th), and, without any previous warning, initiated his scheme of Preferential Tariffs involving a tax on food. His reason for doing this, we gathered, was that he thinks the British Empire will not hold together unless the people of Great Britain are pre- pared to tax their food for the benefit of the Colonies. Said lie:

We should set ourselves a great example of community of interests, and, above all, that com- munity of sacrifice on which alone the Empire can permanently rest. . . . And first among those means is the offer of preferential tariffs.

According to the Colonial Secretary, the "sacrifice " has to be made entirely by the people of this country. As for "preferential tariffs," the Colonies are prepared to c.dmit British goods, but only on condition that adequate protection is afforded to Cclonial products. On our side, Mr. Chamberlain told us bluntly: If you are to give a preference to the Colonies . . . you must put a tax on food.

He urged us to do this not so much because it could be beneficial to our trade, but as a means of keeping the Empire together.

Mr. Chamberiain'.s BArgain.

MR. CHAMBEKLAIN lISCCI this argument in the House of Commons on :`,lay 28th, when he said that unless we were prepared to establish a Fiscal Union, as the necessary beginning of a federal union, the Empire would fall into disjointed atoms. On • June 26th, at the Constitutional Club, he went even further. He told us that he had asked the Colonies if they were ready for Imperial federation, and they had replied, "No." He had asked them if they were prepared to contribute towards Imperial defence, but they would not make any promises. At last he asked if they were agreeable to preferential trading, and the reply he got was to the effect that although the Colonies were not prepared to let British goods go into their countries free, they would be very glad to reduce their duties upon our goods, providing we would give their produce a preference in our market.

Colonial Loyalty. Jr is always best to state an opponent's argument fairly and squarely. Mr, Chamber- lain's reason for wishing to tax our food is because he thinks we can only retain the Colonies by ties of trade interest. Reduced to plain words, it amounts to this: "The chief bond between the Mother Country and the Colonies is a commercial one, and it can only be maintained by Preference." Many who have watched the events of the last few years will not be willing to accept such an interpre- tation of Colonial loyalty without a protest. We think Mr. Chamberlain is entirely mis- taken, and our opinion is enforced by some recent words of the Canadian Premier. Sir Wilfrid Laurier, speaking of preferential tariffs. said:

If such arrangements would not be satisfactory to them, on my part, I do not want to have such an arrangement. If we are to have a Preference, it must be with a firm conviction that such an arrangement would be mutually profitable.

In the face of that speech, Mr. Chamberlain's fears of the Empire falling into disjointed atoms vanish.

Real Preferential Trading. LET us carefully consider what real pre- ferential trading means. The only kind of pre- ferential trading possible is one by which the Colonies can, without danger to themselves, give us some advantage in their market in return for an equal advantage in ours, which shall not Inflict any penalty upon us. Canada, Mr. Chamberlain's favourite example, provides a good illustration of the difficulties from a Colonial point of view. What can Canada give us in return for a tax upon food ? So far as the Empire is concerned, the Colonial Secretary has told us (May lah) : "Canada is, of all our Colonies, the most backward in contributing to common liefence." On these grounds, therefore, it may be said that Canada owes us something to start with. As a matter of business, we do not ket,p the largest Navy in the world going for the protection of our own and the Colonial trade without great expense. Canada tried to give us something in return in 1897, when she granted a preference to British goods. Canada was then putting a duty on all manufactured goods that she received, and by degrees she gave us a preference by reducing the duty on British goods by one-third. But the remaining duty has been more than sufficient to shut out British goods, and the preference has been almost worthless. According to the memo- randum, prepared by the Colonial Office in 1902:

A comparison, either between _total values, or

rates of growth, of the Canadian imports, subject to the lower and higher tariffs respectively, or of imports derived from the United Kingdom and foreign countries, fails to disclose any marked effect of the preferential tariff in stimulating trade between the British Empire and Canada.

Until Canada is prepared to allow British goods to enter her borders under such con- ditions as will enable us to compete with her own, no preference can be of any real advantage to us. This Canada will not grant, and there- fore, so far as she is concerned, the whole scheme of mutually advantageous preferential trading breaks down.

===== 0002601_19040109_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
A Scheme that Failed.

WHAT applies to Canada applies with still greater force to our other self-governing Colonies. So long as they keep up duties against our goods in order to protect their own, all hope of arranging a mutual scheme of preferential trading falls to the ground. See- ing this, Mr. Chamberlain asks us to make a sacrifice and tax our food for the benefit of the Colonies. We are to give everything and get nothing.

A LETTER was posted at a village post-office that had no postage stamp on it, but in place of the stamp had the tollowing written on one corner of the envelope :—" Mr. Postmaster, don't charge no postage on this; the stamp wouldn't stick, so I tole the thing up." EVEN as she looked the angry light faded from her eyes, and the cold, cutting words that but a moment before trembled on her tongue were never uttered. Instead, her heavenly orbs took on the, soft, dreamy look of love, and she bowed her head on his manly shoulder. She had noticed in the upper right-hand corner of his vest reposed two . tickets for the play. __ _ _ _ _

This is without reckoning the young lady reader who skips through the introductory chapters of her novel, searches diligently for the love scene, and then turns to the end to see if the book finishes in the way she desires. A NEW lavina.—Mr. Justice Lowrance recalls a colloquy between a prisoner on his trial for horse-stealing and himself. Judge: "Why did you steal the horse?" Prisoner: "To earn my livin'." Judge: "Bad way of earning your living, isn't it ? " Prisoner: "Must do something." Judge: "So you must. Try six months' hard labour."

A GRADUAL CHANGE.— Farmer (to tramp) "You said you were going to work, and here you are lazily watching those men ploughing, but doing nothing yourself." COULD NOT AFFORD IT.—A creditor called upon a debtor whom he found at dinner, busy carving a turkey. " Now,lsir," said the visitor, "are you going to pay me soon?" "I should only be too glad, my dear sir; but it is impossible; I am cleared out, ruined; I haven't a stiver." "Why, sir, when a man cannot pay his debts, he has no business to be eating a turkey like that." " Alas ! my dear sir," said the debtor, lifting the serviette to his eyes, "I couldn't afford to keep it!" COMPLIMENTARY.—On a certain cross-country line, whose trains have been known to do the journey of ten miles within the hour, a native appeared with a collie. Stationmaster : "Have you a ticket for the dog?" "No," was the reply; "the dog'll run behind." ON a certain farm in the North, considerable damage has been done to the fences by early-morn- ing trespassers in search of mushrooms. The othar day a boy, the son of a ;Socialist in the neighbour- hood, fell into the clutches of tile farmer, who offered to release him it he would promise not to come again • but the youngster was the son of a Socialist, and had "views." Benefits and Burdens.

To sum up, we see that: 1. Colonial loyalty' does not depend upon pre- ferential tariffs.

2. As a business proposal, tariffs would not be profitable to us. 3. The Colonies cannot afford to give us a preference which would allow us to compete with them on equal terms. 4. Mr. Chamberlain asks us to make a sacrifice for the Empire, but that sacrifice is not demanded by the Colonies. While we might consider a sacrifice of luxuries, we have no right to demand it from the poorest, of the poor.

SHOPEREAKING AT RUNCORN. About two hours were occupied at the ,eheshire Quarter Sessions, at Knutsford, on 'Wednesday, in hear- ing a charge against Albert White, alias William Lewis, tailor, for breaking into the butcher's shop of the Run- corn Co-operative Society, and stealing 2s. 6d. 41bs of pork, and a newspaper. The newspaper was really the cause of his undoing. A MARE THAT DIED. Action at the Warrington County Court ,

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday before His Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands, K.C. Jesse Norcott, of Caldwell Farm, Crowley, sued Squire Rat- cliffe farrier and shoeing smith,of Hartley- street,Oldham for £l2 15s. for the keep of a mare. There was a counter claim for £35 for the less of the horse.—Mr. A. Browne represented the plaintiff and Mr. Steel appeared for the defendant.

Mr. Browne in opening the case, said the action was adjourned from the last Court, ,m the application of Mr. Steel, who desired to make a counter-claim. The Icircumstances of the case. as detailed by Mr, Browne, were that on the 30th April, 1901, the defendant wrote Ito the plaintiff asking him to admit to his ley a mare 'in foal, on the understanding that the plain- tiff received the foal as compensation for his services and the cost of keeping the animal. If the mare remained on ,his farm for more than three months it was furtheragreed that the defen- dant should pay 7s. a week. In course of timethe mare foaled and the defendant on going over to the farm was pleased with the appearance of the mare. In con- sequence of the defendant neglecting to take the mare away she became afflicted with mange. The defendant never replied to any letters and when theplaintiff went over to see him, he told plaintiff to get some stuff and dress the mare ready for the nest sale at Crewe. The animal was unfit to be taken for some time, and when she was ready, the defendant still allowed the mare to stop at the farm and did not comply to the plaintiff's request to take her away. The animal, whilst in a field, became afflicted with a disease known as "the staggers" an commenced jumping. Whilst try- ing to leap a recently trimmed hedge, the mare became impaled and a stake stuck in her. The stake was removed and a veterinary surgeon called in, but the animal died the same night. —The plaintiff, in cross-examination, admitted that the animal was working a harrow at the time she had the accident. He did not tell the defendant that she was working a harrow at the time. He was vexed with Broomfield who was driving the mare, for not keeping the animal off the fence when she became afflicted with the staggers. She broke loose from the harrow. —William McAdam Graham, veterinary surgeon, stated that he found the animal suffering from staggers or concussion the brain , lao one could have helped the horse from prancing and falling on the hedge. He stitched up the wounds. —For the defence, Mr. Steel contended that the accident which resulted in the death of the mare did not occur in the way described by the plaintiff. He would call evidence to show that the mare was harrow- ing in the field, when she reared up and fell with her fore legs over a low hedge. Plaintiff was sent for and on arrival struok the mare with a whip. She reared up again and fell on the stakes in the hedge.—Walter Kelly, a farm labourer, formerly employed by the plaintiff stated that he saw the accident to the mare. The mare was drawing a harrow when the mare's feet got fast in the hedge and she fell against'the hedge. The plaintiff came on the scene with a whip in his hand and struck the mare. The mare then got up and fell on a stake in the hedge.—William* Broomfield, also a former employee of the plaintiff, gave evidence to the same effect.—Plis Honour gave judgment for the plaintiff for the amount claimed and dismissed the eounter-claint.

ALLEGED ASSAULT ON A FARM LABOURER.

The next action before the court WSS one in which Walter Kelly, farm labourer, sued Mr. Norcott for £5O damages for alleged assault on October 28th, Mr. Rye- croft, barrister, Manchester (instructed by Mr. Bancroft, of Northwich) appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. Browne defended..

Mr. Ryecroft said that on the date named plaintiff and a man named Spencer were sent to Warrington with two loads of straw. Mr. Norcott wasalso!at Warrington and he look Flick the lighter lurry, Kelly and Spencer having charge of the other vehicle which was laden with manure. They left Warrington about 7.30 and on the way to Crowley called at the Thorn Inn, Appleton where they each had a pint of beer. Theapproach to the farm Was a private road which was full of ruts. Here they stopped the horse and went to the farmyard for some chains.On returning they found that the horses had moved mid the lurry was very near to the ditch. They therefore decided that it would be safest to take the horses out of the lurry and leave the vehicle in the road all night. Kelly was leading one horse to the stables when he met Mr. Norcott who objected to the luny being left in the road and ordered Kelly to bring it to the yard. Kelly said it would be dangerous to remove the vehicle and refused, whereupon Norcott struck him a violent blow in the face, knocking him down. Whilst on the ground, he kicked him once or twice. The following day plaintiff consulted Dr. Love who found him suffering from a cut over the left eye,two fractured ribs, and bruises on the back. Plaintiff bore out this statement and denied that he was drunk. He did not strike the defendant with a pikel ' • neither did he 02 tke following day, threaten to shoot him. •

isSeveral witnesses werelcalled including Miss Britch of the Thorn Inn, who said Kelly and Spencer were sober, when they called at the house. For, the defence it was stated that Kelly and Spencer Fere drunk when they arrived at the farm. Kelly was bleeding and stated that he had fallen down and been trampled upon by the horses. Kelly seized Mr. Norcott by the throat and also wounded him with a pikel and a manure fork. Defendant denied having assaulted Kelly. • In cross-ekamination defendant denied having told a man named• Garner that he had "given Kelly beans."

Mrs. Norcott stated that she-took the- fork from Kelly. His Honour gave 'judgment for the defendant with- out costs. •

COLLIER SEVERELY CENSURED., A St. Helens Youth's Sad Death.

A severe reprimand was administered by Coroner Brighouse on Thursday night at an inquest at Peasley Cross St. Helens. on Edward Balmer, collier, of 127, Eleogant-lane. Balmer worked at the Queen pit of the Lea: Green colliery, and whose drawer was Edward Ashton, aged seventeen and a half years, who lived at 5, Elephant-lane. Ashton had,worked at the colliery since he was thirteen, but ha`d` only been a drawer eight months. Balmer said that on Monday morning there was a " tail " of coal which, after he had "holed" right through lie told deceased to get down with his pick. Balmer then went four or fire yards away to hole for anothershot. Thaccal comedown on deceased, and he was injured internally and died neKt day at the St. Helens Hospital. Balmer said that if the lad hadjworked for the position that he had told him he would not have been hurt.—The Coroner: Then he was doing something that you would not have let him do yourself if you had been there ?—Witness,: Yes. The Coroner then read general rule 39, which stated that" no person shall be employed as a coal or iron- stone getter or allowed to work alone in the face' works until he has had two years' experience in such working under supervision of some skilled workman." Witness admitted that he knew of the rule, and that deceased had not had two years training in working at the coal face.— The Coroner: In fact, he was your drawer.—Witness: Yes.—The Coroner: If the Government Inspector and the manager says you are very much to blame in this matter do you say you are not ?L-Witness: No.—The ' Coroner : Not in the face of that rifle ?—Witness: If he had stopped where he was he would not have met I with the accident.—Edward Smith, fireman, said it was against the rules for Balmer to leave deceased alone in the manner described. He gave Balmer instructions to , get the coal down himself, and he said, he' would do it , whilst the drawer was taking the coal away out of the working place.—The Coroner: Then Balmer did not carry out your specific 1;404. instructions ?—Witness: No.—The jury returned a Jan. 2—Bradford verdict of "Accidental death,"but said that Balmer had not only broken general rule 39, but also disobeyed the " 9—Wigan 16—Leeds specific instructions of the firemen, and he was dewy- " 23—Runcorn big of censuro.—The coroner had Balmer brought in, " 30—Salford and spoke to him of the terribl e consequences to the •b gr e'_ family of deceased.
