THE EXAMINER.

JANUARY 9th 1904.

Mr. CHA3LBERLAIN,as POLITICAL RADIUM. we learn from one of

his journals, is the incarnation of radium in public life, as he " per- sonifies the inexhaustible energies of man." This is a gratifying suggestion that we shall not lose him in the ordinary course of mortality, but that he will continue to radiate activity through the Empire for ever and ever. The Australian Government, as Lord ROSEBERY hints, have their misgivings about the duration of Mr. CHAMBER- LAIN'S influence. They would like to have the benefit of it in Australia now. Lord ROSEBERY interprets Mr. DEA.KIN'S invitation to mean that a Ministry which has not cut a triumphant figure at the recent polls are eager to engage tLie services of our champion agitator. That would be a very good business for Mr. DEAKIN. Mr. GUAM- BERLAIN would be a popular novelty on Australian platforms, and Mr. DEAKIN would enjoy that ex- cellent advertisement which belongs to every enterprising impresario. As for the policy to be expounded, the Australian Premier has no very clear idea of it, and would like to know a good deal more. " We conceive that this distant con- tinent possesses a title to consideration as one of the interested parties in your proposed agreement for Preferential trade." Lord , ROSEBERY quoted this at Edinburgh to shew that the proposed agreement is still rather nebulous to Mr. DEAKIN, although Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is always telling us that it is the pressure of the Colonies which led him to take up the cause of Tariff reform. If that were so, Australia would have a pretty definite notion of what she wants ; but here is Mr. DEAKIN saying to Mr. CHAMBER— LAIN in effect: "It may be all right; still, we don't exactly know what you are driving at. Come over and tell us, and incidentally help us if you can to remain in office." Mr. CHA3fRERLAIN him- self says of Australia "I do not doubt that she will be ready to meet in no grudging spirit all pro- posals for strengthening the bonds between us.' Then the proposals are not of her making or initiation; they are shot out by the inexhaustible radium of Birmingham, and their great advantage in Mr. DEA.Krx's eyes, as in Mr. CHA3tBERLAIN" is that they promise to confer some benefit upon certain deserving politicians. But our radio-active agitator cannot spare any personal rays for Australia at present, and not even a luminous particle from his Tariff Com- mission. The" most impprtant consequence Of radium,"said Sir OLIVER LODGE in his Birmingham lecture, "was the discovery of the mutability of matter, the transmutation of elements, and the liability of mata.rial:atoms to break up and explode.' Mr. CHAMBERLAIN' must have listeaed to this with full appreciation of its bearing on his own

" inexhaustible energies.' For mutability of matter where should we look if not to his speeches ? Who can transmute the elements more sucussfully than the politician at whose magic touch Protection becomes a development of Free Trade ? And if we want to study the liability of material atoms to break up and explode, what better illustration can be found than the condition of the Unionist party? Mr. PIKE PEASE, writing to the "Times," calls upon the Liberal-Unionists to explode for the purpose of blowing up the Dum; of DEVONSHIRE. He is; still President of the Liberal-Unionist Association but, says the "Times," "Mr. PIKE PEAsE will pro- bably have many sympathimrs in his view that Liberal-Unionists should now clear up their position by definitely expressing their full approval of Mr. CHAMBERLMN'S policy and aims." The DUKE is doubtless prepared for this contingency. He can scarcely expect to retain any control over the machinery of the Liberal-Unionist organisation when he has openly proclaimel his hostility to Mr. CHAMBERLAIN. The DUKE can give many points in statesmanship to that politician, but none in the manipulation of a party caucus. He has detached himself no less completely from Mr. BALrouit than from Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, and he must say that they are carrying the bulk of their party with them, and that there is no permanently intermediate position for him. He cannot take up the attitude of one who is neither a supporter of the Government nor an ally of the Opposition. To render the highest service to the cause of Free Trade he is bound to face the necessity of falling into lino with the Liberal leaders.

THE chances of pre-

_ ALARMS AND serving peace in the Far CONTINGENCIES. East grow more slender every day ; but there is time for speculation as to the contingencies involved in a struggle. No subject could be better chosen for the exercise of nervous fancy. Ie the nature of things it does not seem likely that a war would be brief. Both sides have so much at stake that neither would submit easily to any reverse. In the meantime the explosive possibilities of Asia, and their effect upon England, France, Germany, and America, would keep the most active imagina- tion busy. Mr. FREDERICK GREENWOOD has conjured up a picture of the yellow races, intoxicated by Japanese successes, hurling themselves everywhere upon the European in- truders. He thinks this upheaval would compel the powers to combine against Japan, and that the position of England as the ally of Japan would then become untenable. This is a very large assumption. It supposes that all China would b a furnace of ferocity, which could not be extin- guished except by the intervention of the white races on behalf of Russia. This is the sort of nightmare we might expect from the Kaisor,whose historical utterances about the Chinese have not been remarkable for sobriety. Indeed, if war should break out, and if it should go badly ' for Russia in the early stages, the German newspapers will certainly indulge in a scare about the "Yellow Peril." That would be their cue ,from St. Petersburg; but it is an excellent reason why we should not lose our heads. One. effort to frighten us has signally failed already. This was the suggestion that France would be compelled to help Russia if China,or any Chinese supposed to be acting by the direction of their Government, should side with Japan. That notion has been so emphatically repudiated in Paris that we are not likely to hear of it again. Equally baseless is the apprehension that the Powers will have to make common cause with Russia if they are not to be swept out of China by some stupendols outbreak of yellow frenzy. It would suit Russia and her German sympathisers very well to have this generally believed; but why should the rest of the world believe it?

Mr. GREENWOOD has a proposal for making everybody safe. Let Europe and America say to Russia and Japan: "There must be no war. You are quarrelling about Corea. We must put a stop to that. Be it known, therefore, that henceforward Corea is under our protection, and that we shall permit neither of you to get any territorial advan- tage over the other." If this seems rather absurdly peremptory, let us givo the proposal in Mr. GREEN- WOOD'S own words: "Appropriation of Corea by either of the countries named is no better right nor even half so great, as its preservation to its own people, for the peace of all the world. Why, then, may not the European nations—America included, of course—take possession of Corea, declare it under Common protec- tion, forbid Russian encroachment in the north, forbid Japanese invasion in the south, and so give to the contending Powers their indispens- able security against each other? Or if some expansion, some emigration ground is really necessary for Japan (a dubious question how- ever), that might possibly be arranged by the cession of a narrow enclave in the south of Corea, where it would threaten no other nation's peace." All this is just as practical as if it were dictated by some idealist in another plinet. It does not occur to Mr. GREENWOOD that no diplomatist would propose this fantastic scheme to the Euro- pean nations, America included with the smallest expectation of getting it accepted ; or, supposing that difficulty to be overcome, that Russia and Japan would do anything but treat it with polite derision. Imagine the united forces of the Pro- tectorate patrolling Corea so as to keep the Russians out of the north, and the Japanese out of the southern portion, except their " enclave,' kindly conceded to them by general consent! The diplomatists of the neutral Powers may not be able to prevent war between Russia and Japan ; if not, the only practical course open to them is to keep out of it. The upshot of the conflict may raise some very grave questions affecting our own interests ; but for the time cv9 have to maintain neutrality, and in that attitude we are fortunately sustained by the pacific policy of M. DELCASSE,

Warrington Bridge is, after all, to be widened by cantilevers, and Alderman Smethurst has at last carried his point. It is surprising what a complex question this apparently simple improve- ment has developed into, and what a number of difierent sources of opposition there are. The pro- posal before the Town tiouncil on Tuesday was the acme of simplicity, but scarcely any of the speakers could confine themselves to it for a moment. They would not discuss whether or no Warrington bridge should be widened, but instead they dilated upon rosy schemes for develop- ing the south side of the town, building new bridges over the river and generally airing their pet ideas. We have a great deal of sympathy with Councillor Maginn's project, but it was not before the meeting on Tuesday,and with ail his eloquence

the member for St. John's advanced no argumenC against the widening scheme. No matter how many new bridges are thrown over the river at Howley and Arpley the fact still remains that Warrington bridge carries the main thoroughfare between Lancashire and Cheshire, and that it must be widened to b.-) either safe or serviceable. Councillor James Evans obscured the issue almost as much as Councillor Maginn, with his dark hints about level crossings, railway improvements, and approaches from the Latchford side. It was left to Alderman Pierpoint and Coun- cillor Tinnion to deflect the discussion into the proper channel, when the question was soon decided and a majority of 1.5 votes to 10 given in favour of the widening. An important step 2orward has, at last, been taken, but we shall not have much cooliden2e in the scheme being brought to a successful issue until we see the contractor's men actually on the job. There are some men in the Council who have reduced to a fine art the policy of obstruction. In our own opinion, however, the Council has decided wisely, and we cannot see that the widening of the bridge will harmfully effect any future projects. Alderman Smethurst is to be congratulated on the enterprise and persever- ance which have enabled him to lick into practical shape two valuable improvements, viz., Lime- street corner and Warrington bridge.

The Branch Library question also made good progress, and the Council now stands committed to two or three delivery stations, issue centres in the elementary schools, and a reading room at Silver-street. This is a considerable advance toward the goal which Mr. Upson aimed at when he introduced the subject m the Town Council some fe A" months ago &rid he must be ("ratified at the prompt steps which have been taken. There was a practically unanimous feeling in favour of the Library Committee's proposals, the only dissentient being Alderman Sutton who distinguished himself by the expression of remark- ably out-of-date opinions. " Warrington was not yet so large," said the worthy. Alderman," that it was too far from any part of the town for a borrower to go to the Library if he wanted a book." A more conserva- tive notion we never heard, not even from the lips of the chairman of the Conservative Association The necessity for encouraging the love of reading among the working classes is apparently beyond the comprehension of Alderman Sutton. Yet it is difficult to believe that he was really speaking his own mind. There is a perverse strain in the Alderman's composition which occasionally shows unexpectedly,and it must,vve think, have been very near the surface when the Branch Libriiry scheme was introduced. Alderman Sutton should really be warned. There is danger abroad, and a few more antiquated observations such as these quoted, will lead to superannuation being talked of. We are sure none of the Aldermen are desirous of being laid on the shelf just yet. A word to the wise is sufficient!

Fair play for the fairs was demanded in the course of a discussion on the Markets Committee's recommendation to abolish the half-yearly horse and cattle fairs, and the pleasure fairs in Warring- ton. There was a very strong opinion expressed that the horse and cattle fairs, which are an ancient institution, should be preserved. It seems characteristic of Warrington to make no use of its natural advantages. Here we are on the borders of Cheshire, and in the centre of the busiest part of Lancashire and our horse and cattle fairs have dwindled so much that they are not deemed—by the Markets Committee—worth the expense and supervision. Councillor Maginn and others were much inclined to blame the committee for the existing state of affairs. Cer- tainly there has been more apathy than enterprise on, the part of the committee and the question is naturally asked: Why should Warring- ton not have horse and cattle fairs like Crewe, Nantwich, and other places less favourably situ- ated? The suggestion has much to recommend it. Perhaps the Agricultural Society will interest itself in the matter,which should not be beyond its sphere. In the meantime the fate of the fairs hangs in the balance, for, owing to a misunder- standing the whole of the minutes of the com- mittee were referred back—a .anigne occurrence in the Council proceedings.

Alderman Bennett's earnest plea for Warring- ton Dock fell upon deaf ears, or at any rate upon stony hearts. for his motion was- not even seconded. Owing to the lateness of the hour and the protracted discussion on Warrington Bridge many members had had more than enuugh Coun- cil meeting, and about half of them quietly retired when Alderman Bennett rose to move."' that any support which the kjorporation may decide to give to the Manchester Ship Canal Bill be conditional upon an extension of thetime for the construction of Warrington Dock." The remaining members listened with amused tolerance, but they were seemingly not at all impressed with the gravity of the issue or the Alderman's demeanour. Mr. Bennett's plaint was directed against the neglect of the; town's natural resources to which we have previously referred. Had his resolution been somewhat less arbitrary it is possible that he would have had a more sympathetic hearing, for their is no questioning the fact that there must be something radically. wrong when hundreds of thousands of pounds worth of commerce pass our very doors without our particip lting in any way. It is really absurd to think that we have the finest artitical inland waterway in the world filling practically no other useful purpose for Warrington than forming the. borough boundary. What would some towns. give for the opportunities which ‘Varrington, possesses but trtaibles not to, exploit ? Alderman Bennett's dream will, we feel sure, some day be fulfilled.ltmay be that private enterprise will have to step in and make the required deck, but a War- rington Dock there will be in course of time. We hope the Alderman will pursue his theme and enueavour to charge his townsmen with a higher voltage of energy than they at present possess. Warrington has been a port before. There is. greater reason than ever why it should be a port again. This much as least, Alderman I3ennett conclusively proved..

Objection was taken some time ago to the practice of allowing officials of the Warrington Corporation to hold two appointwents in different departments of municipal work. Recent action by committees of the Town Council bears evidence of a desire on the part of the members to put an end to dual appointments. The resignation of Mr. fleas from the position of sanitaiy engineer gave the Sanitary Works Committee the first op- portunity. They have immediately reorganised the department, and Mr. J. J. A. Parfitt is now the manager of the cleansing department and Mr. Richard Wilson,the manager the Longford Depot This arrangement seems preferable to that pre- viously adopted and if it makes for greater effi- ciency the committee will have baen justified in the step they have taken. Since the December meeting of the Council we learn that steps have been taken to abolish another dual appoint- ment. The resignation of Mr. '.3.,ritusdale from the dual position of engineer to the electricity depart- ment and manager of the tramways gave the Tramways Committee their opportunity for abo- lishing the old system and, they have taken advantage of it. The new elactrical engineer will have nothing to do with the tramway traffic. and at an early date a manager is.to be appointed to take sole charge of this work.

Aldernm Bennett does not stand alone in his advocacy of the policy of placing memorial tablets on houses which have had famous or distinguished occupants.ln Warrington he has not received that generous support which is necessary to carry out the scheme thoroughly and up to the present only one tablet has bees placed. In London they do things of this sort much better. On the recommendation of the Historical Records Committee :of the London Council it has been decided to place talbats in three houses in the Metropolis. One is on 36, Devon- shire-street,l Portland-place, which was occupied by Sir John Herschel, anothet on I, Devonshire- terrace, Portland-place, whale Charles Dickens lived for many years, and the Void on 12, Clarges,

' ,• 4 _ • -

street, Picadilly, where Edmund Kean, the actor, once resided. The fixing of tablets of a like description in Warrington was included in the programme of events for celebrating the Diamond Jubilee of the late Queen, but somehow or other this scheme was dropped., It now remains for pri- vate individuals to carry out the project which the Corporation failed to accomplish. There are many interesting houses in the town worthy of a memorial tablet, and it is to be .hoped that they will not be allowed to stand much longer without some permanent mark of recognition.

Helplessly drunk at fourteen! It taxes one§ credibility to believe such an occurrence possible in these days of enlightenment. Yet on Christmas Day, of all days,a boy of that tender age was found lying across the footpath of one of the main streets of Warrington in an ,advanced state of intoxica- tion. His story provides food for serious reflec- tion. Having obtained a bottle from home, he and his companions were able to purchase spirits at public homes and indulge in the disgraceful form of festivity which ended in a prosecution. The publicans were not transgressing the strict letter of the law in serving the lads, and the case only serves to illustrate more forcibly the tempta- tions which beset the young life of the nation. Unfortunately this is not the only Christmas incident of the kind which has been brought to light in this part of the country,for at Manchester on Thursday a boy was dealt with for an exactly similar offence. The recent" Children's Act" was a great step in the right direction, but it would appear that even more stringent measures are needed to protect children from the drink evil.

LOCAL NEWS.

Coming Events. "Redeeming the time" will be the subject of the Rev. Ti. Wilkins Rees' sermon at Bewsey-road 'Wesleyan Church to-morrow (Sunday) morning. The animal festival in connection with Bold-street Wesley Guild will he held in Bold-street Wesleyan Chapel to-morrow (Sunday), when the Rev. Arthur T. .Kinnings, of Southport, will preach. A united rally •will be held in the Chapel on Monday evening.. On Thursday, Friday and Saturday next the War- rington Dramatic Society will give performances of Mr. Haddon Chambers' original modern play" The Idler." It is expected that Sir Gilbert and Lady Oreenall will be present on the opening night. The proceeds will be devoted to the funds of the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children and the Children's League of Pity and it is hoped that a handsome sum will be handed over to these worthy -organizations. Full particulars appear in our adver- tismo• columns. The annual ball in aid of the Infirmary and Dispen- sary will be held at the Lion Hotel on Thursday, February 11th. Mr. Vetter's band has been engaged. ' In connection with the Grammar School Old Boys Union, the second annual dinner will take place at 'the Lion Hotel, on Thursday evening, January 28th. A dance in aid of the funds of the Warrington Musical Society will take place on Tuesday, February 16th next in the Parr Hall.

'Cheshire M.P. to Retire. Mr. 11. J, Tolleinache, 11. P., for the Eddisbury Division, will retire at the next general election. Poor Children's Annual Treat. The Committee have decided, in consequence of the epidemic of infectious diseases, to postpone this annual event. After consultation with Dr. Gornall, it is probable that the treat will take place towards the •end of the month.

Works Accident. John Wild, 50 years of age' living at ill, Long- ford-street, Was admitted to the Warrington Infirmary at noon on Wednesday suffering from shock and injuries to his ribs, the result of an accident sustained whilst following his employment at the Cheshire Lines Waggon Works, Battersby-lane.

Ragged School Y.P.S.C.E. In connection with the Young People's Society of Christian Endeavour a " social " was held on New Year's Day in the schoolroom, Mr. H. Osborne pre- siding. A capita' programme was gone through, con- sisting of songs, piano and flute solos, and recitations and readings. Refreshments were served at the in- terval and the rest of the evening was spent in games. Ancient Urns Unearthed. The Rural District Council tvorkmen in cutting a new road near Kenyon Hall came upon a large burial mound, or harrow, dating back to 500 B.C. An interesting find was a bronze age urn containing' the burnt bones of a human being.. This has .been sent to the Warrington Museum. - Fragments of another similar urn and a quantity of black ash were dis- covered on this ancient Britons' urn field.

Fire In Marittouse-lane. A tire occurred at the workshop of Mr. G-. Smith, joiner and builder, id, Marshhonse-lane, on Thursday evening. Mr. Hughes, the foreman, left the premises apparently secure at 4.20 and about 5.45 some chil- dren saw the place In flames. A telephonic message was sent to the Fire Station, but the brigade did not turn out, as they were soon afterwards informed that the fire had been extinguished by some men.

The Inquiry. The Commissioners appointed to investigate the trading transactions of the Corporation for the past seven years have recommenced- their work. There have been three sittings of the Council during the past week, and considerable progress has been made. We understand that the figures relating to the direct trading of one firm with the Corporation have been got out.

New Bridge Across the Mersey. Terms were on Thursday agreed upon between the various authorities in the matter of the new bridge .across the river Mersey at Fliiton, connecting Lan- cashire with Cheshire. The bridge is to cost £4,500. The Cheshire County Council will pay one half, the Lancashire County Council. a quarter, the Man- chester Committee, and the Flixton Parochial Com- mittee one eighth. The new bridge is expected to create a building boom in the neighbourhood, and considerably increase in value the hundreds .of acres of land owned by the Manchester Corporation. The building of the bridge is to commence forthwith. iA Warrington Fruiterer's Failure. William Edward 80y1in,59 Hardy-street,Warrington, previously at 76, Scott-street, and prior thereto DA 20 Lovely-lane, and carrying on busines§ as a wholesale and retail fruit, rabbit and poultry salesman at a stall in the Warrington market hall, appeared before Mr. Registrar Ridgway at the Bankruptcy Court, -yesterday for his public examinationi in bankruptcy. The gross liabilities were expected to rank at £251 17s. sd. and the assets were expected to produce £67 7. Bd., leaving a deficiency of £lB4 , 9s. 9d. The debtor assigned as the cause of his bankruptcy "Bad Itrade ; want of capital; heavy business expenses in proportion to my turnover; loss owing to goods going bad in consequence of goods being sent to me without my orders; and household and personal expenditure in excess of profits." In his observa- tions the Official Receiver stated that the bankrupt told him he commenced businesEi about May, 1896, without capital. Not having kept sufficient books of account nor ascertained his finan- cial position, he could not file the prescribed deficiency account, but attributed his deficiency to bad debts £l3 18s. 6d., loss by betting £3O, loss in trade owing to goods perishing and overbuying and household and personal expenditure in excess of profit £l4O us. 3d.—The debtor Was closely ques- tioned by the Assistant Official Receiver (Mr. John- son) and also by two creditors. He denied thathe was at the Hayd.ock Park meeting last year, but ad- mitted that he attended the races at Liverpool.—The examination was adjourned to the next court in order that the debtor might furnish some accounts asked for.—Mr.. Steel who represented the debtor, said the account books of the debtor had been placed in the hands of an accountant but he reported that it was impossible to provide a correct and full account.— The Registrar said the debtor could furnish the Official Receiver with a cash account and show the money lost by betting.—The examination was :adjourned.

Licensing Sessions. The borough magistrates on Monday signed the precept, fixing the general annual licensing meeting for Friday, February sth. Black Dike Brass Band. Death of Archdeacon Lewis. 1 Many Warrington Church people will regret to hear that Archdeacon Coldwyer Lewis, rector of Davenham, died at Oswestry on Monday morning. ; Deceased only succeeded to the Davenham living in May. For many years he was chaplain and arch- deacon at Bombay, and latterly he was rector at Aid- ford and chaplain to the Duke of Westminster. He was prominently associated with all the diocesan institutions, was a fine preacher, and held in the highest regard. In Lent of 1902 he delivered an ad- dress to men at the Parish Church, Warrington, on " Work and Wages "and he had also spoken at the Parr Hall in support of the S. P. G. _ _ _ _ _ _

The Druids and the Nurses. At a largely attended annual meeting of the Widows and Orphans Fund in connection with the Order of Druids held on Thursday night, presided over by Mr. William Bibby, it was unanimously decided to con tinue the annual subscription of £5 to the Warrington District Nursing Association. Several members expressed their very high appreciation of the noble work the nurses are doing in our town. It was also mentioned by the Chairman that the annual meeting of the Druids Family Funeral Fund to be held on Friday would also consider the claims of the Nursing Association.

These results are highly satisfactory. " Looking Backward and Forward." This was the title of a double paper which was read at the meeting of the Temperance Mission Lodge of Good Templars on Wednesday. The first half of the paper was taken by Bro. *C. J. Sheath who took his hearers in imagination over the year 1903 and re- viewed what hid been done for thetemperance cause (Ist), in-the United Kingdom, (2nd), locally, (3rd), in the Good Templar order, and (4th),in the Temperance Mission Lodge. Bro. Merlin followed with the second half and created hopes—and fears—for the fu- ture. He deplored the increase of drunkenness among women and said it was a bad outlook for the future' generation of England • when the mothers of the country were addicted to drink. The papers were favourably commented on by Bros. Wtherts and O'Brien and Sister Miss Woods of Earlestown.

I Alien Immigration. I At a meeting of the Fairfield Literary and Debating; Society on Monday evening last, Mr. J. Price intro- duced a paper entitled "Alien Immigration." He handled the subject very skilfully and showed in the clearest possible manner that the present large influx of aliens was very detrimental to the country in many ways. He contended that on arrival into our country- the majority of aliens are in a destitute and impover- ished condition • that among them there are many criminals and others being paupers become, a burden on the local rates. Also, he stated, it tended to overcrowd :the already overcrowded poorer parts of our big - cities and to introduce and spread infectims disease. In the latter portion of his paper Mr. Price argued that immigration of aliens into this country should be placed under State control, 'and finally completed a very interesting paper by suggest- ing certain regulations which ought to be put into force. The President of the Society (Rev. A. S. Cameron) complimented Mr. Price on his paper and told him that there could not really be any opposition to it. A few points were raised however, and after an interesting discussion the resolution Mr. Price pro- posed, i.e., "That unlimited alien immigration is detrimental to the country," was put to the meeting and carried unanimously. Free Church Prayer Meetings. United prayer meetings arranged by the Warring- ton and District Free Chuach Council have been held in Wycliffe Memorial Hall every evening during the past week, and have been fairly-well attended. On Monday evening Mr. C. E. Parker delivered an address. He said those who knew anything of the history of the different Free Churches would realize what a dividing influence such things as church ,rovernmen.t and church doctrine had had in the past, and how those.things had kept the different churches from each other. At the same time he thought they would all realize that those things that had divided the churches were taking the right place in their thoughts, and that the great thing that united them as Christian people, their loyalty tO God, was stupendously higher and greater than the things that divided them, although in their place they might have had their importance.—The Rev. John Yonge also spoke. At Tuesday's meeting the Rev. J. S. Hughos presided, and was supported by the Rev. John Yonge. The Rev. J. S. Hughes spoke at length upon the growth of false doctrine, and contended thut doctrinal preaching had been very largely neglected, and that topical teaching was more to the fore.—The Rev. John Yonge presided on Wednesday evening, and the Rev. W. Rees delivered an address, dealing more particularly with missionary work.—On Thurs- day evening the chair was occupied by the Rev. G. H. Carnson, and the Rev. J. L. Milne, M.A„ 4.D., spoke upon Sabbath observance, parental responsibilities, and the training of the young. Last evening the address was delivered by the Rev. G-. H. Carnson. - An Active Warrington Rechabite. The December issue of the "Juvenile Rechabite " contained a photograph of Bro. William Wright, D.S.J.T. and P.D.C.R., an active and useful worker in the Warrington district, together with the following sketch of his career since he became identified with the temperance cause :—" Bro. W. Wright was born in Warrington in 1859, where he still resides. At a very early age he joined the Band of Hope and has been connected with this work ever-since. He joined " Charity " Tent, No. 11, in February, 1881, aad few years later became an active worker. In 1888 he was elected to' his, present post of Tent Secretary. For many years the tent had been lying dormant, the membership had gone down to 23 and was in low water financially. Bro. Wright however, set to work and in a short time there was over 100 members. 1 Steps were taken to extend the Order by the members of " Charity " Tent being transferred to other parts of the town. Several Adult Tents were thus instituted and still " Charity " Tent prospered and has at present over 160 members. In 1901, Bro. Wright was pre- sented by his Tent with a roll-top desk. In 1900 he. was elected D.G., subsequently passed through the Chairs and is still on the District Executive. He represented his district at Edinboro' and Swansea H.M.C.'s. Meanwhile the,juvenile cause had died out in Warrington. A committee was appointed, and in. 1891 the "John Dimond" Juvenile Tent was insti- tuted. Bro. Wright was appointed and remains a superintendent. Several other Juvenile Tents have since been instituted, the present membership being 415. On September 28th, 1891, Bro. Wright with a few other workers formed the Warrington Juvenile District with three Tents and 61 members. In 1893 he became, D.S.J.T. and there are now 15 Tents and over 700 members. Then our brother is President of his own Church Band of Hope and on the Executive of the Band of Hope Union, being in much request as a capable .speaker at the various meetings."

The Examiner, Saturday, January 9, 1904

I The New Vicar of Christ Church. The Bishop of Chester instituted the Rev. G. C. Briggs, M.A., to the cure of souls in the parish of Christ Church. Latchford on Monday. Last evening in the presence of a large congregation, the rev. gentleman wv inducted to the living by the Von. Archdeacon ; Barber. The late incumbent, the Rev. F. A. Screeton, was present. The usual form of service was gone through and the Archdeacon deliv- ered an address explaining the ceremonies, He also eulogised the work of the late Vicar who, although he had only been with them a short time had gained a warm place in the affections of the parishioners. He asked them to be loyal to the newly-appointed Vicar. Warrington P.S.A. Society. .

There was a good attendance at the annual meeting of the above society which was held in the Co-opera- tive Hall, Sankey-street, on Sunday afternoon under the presidency of Mr. W. Clegg (retiring president). The annual report was presented by the secretary of the society (Mr. E. Stanley. Taylor) and showed that the society was progressing, the membership having steadily increased throughout the year. There was also an increase in the number of prizes distributed, which, of course, signified a ,corresponding improve- ment in the regularity of attendance. There was every indication that the coming year would be suc- cessful. Mr. Clegg retired from the office of president and Mr. E. Wynne Jones was elected. 'Mr. Clegg- was appointed vice-president; Mr. E. Stanley Taylor, secretary; Mr. Warham, assistant secretary; Mr. Thomas Garnett, treasurer; and Mr. B. Wadsworth, organist. A committee of eighteen members was elected.

BANKRUPTCY OF EARLESTOWN BUILDERS.

Before Mr. Registrar Ridgway, at the Warrington Bankruptcy Court yesterday William Stephens and Nicholas Martin Stephens, both residing at and carry- ing on business as builders under the style of W. and N. Stephens, at 24, Sankey-street,Earlestown,appeared for their first public examination in bankruptcy. The gross liabilities were expected to rank at £867 75.9 d. andthe assets were estimated to produce £l5l is., leaving a deficiency of £716 6s. 9d. The causes of failure as alleged by bankrupts were "Losses on contracts and household and personal expenditure in excess of profits." In his observations, the Official Receiver stated that the bankrupts commenced business in July, 1901 with £l5 borrowed capital. The bankrupts stated that they had not kept sufficient books of account and they had not, while in business, ascertained their financial position.—The examination was closed.—Mr. B. A. White, solicitor,appeared for the bankrupts.

50 YEARS' OF SUNDAY SCHOOL WORK.

New Year's Day marked the end of 50 years' Wesleyan Sunday school work in Earlestown, and the Jubilee was celebrated on Friday by a tea and concert About 300 teachers, scholars and friends assembled in the Lecture Hall, and partook of a sumptuous tea,which was prepared and served by the teachers and ladies of the congregation. Afterwards an adjournment was made to the Town Hall, where a miscellaneous concert was held. In the absence of the Rev. F. R. Smith (pastor), Mr. Burgess (day school headmaster) occupied the chair. He was supported on the platform by the Rev. B. Weaver (Manchester), Messrs. Pearson, Warbur- ton, Clare, Bradley, T. Parr, P. Parr, Atkinson, and R. A. Robinson (secretary.) The latter presented a lengthy and interesting report which was passed. The musical portion of the programme opened with a glee by the Jubilee choir of 70 voices, drawn from the church choir and Sunday school. The sections were very well balanced, and the rendering of "Softly falls the shade of evening" reflected great credit on the choir and its conductor, Mr. T. R. Bradley, Miss B. Smith recited with much effect, while Mr. T. Robinson sang " Come back to me," for which he received due award. The Brunswick Quartette Party have been for some time favourites with Earlestown audiences, and their efforts in "On the banks of .Allan water "were highly appreciated. Miss Lily Barr, a singer of no mean repute, was once more in evidence. She sang "For all eternity" in admirable style. Mr. W. Goslin of Leicester,gave the recitation" The surgeon's child," and Master G. Cawley recited" The policeman's story. Mr. F. P. Clare received loud applause, for his render- ing of" To Mary," whilst Miss Olive Roberts was a distinct success with "Killarney." The Brunswick Quartette Party kept the "house" in a merry spirit with the humorous quartette "The fishing story," for which they received an encore. The miniature dialogue "Hubert and Prinde Arthur" was undertaken by Masters Jos. Atkinson and Jas Alderson. The impersonation of the Prince was capably performed, but unfortunately Master Alderson did not appear to advantage. Mrs. C. F. Hopwood sang" Down the vale" in good fashion, but owing to the lengthy programme was unable to res- pond to an encore. The glee party gave two songs entitled "Now by day's retiring lamp "and. "Night, lovely night," which the audience were not slow to appreciate. A very enjoyable evening was brought to cdnclusion witha laughable dialogue " Who would be a bachelor ?" in which Mrs. Goslin, Miss Berry,Messrs. J. H. Gregory, E. Goslin and D. H. Robinson took part.

DISORDERLY BOYS AT RUNCORN.

At the Runcorn Sessions, on Monday, before Sir Richard Brooke and Mr. W. Timmins, Arthur Kirkham (17), George Faulkner (14), Lock-street, Arthur Shon (15), Harry Fish (14), Handley-street, and Joseph Faulkner (14), Cooper-street, were summoned for obstructing the footpath in King-street, Runcorn, on the night of December 23rd. They pleaded guilty.-- P.C. Brayford said he saw the boys wrestling and roll- ing about on the footpath. They were having what they called a rough house. It was the tradesmen'e show night, and there were a good many people in thd -street.—Superintendent Ennion said he had receives numerous complaints about this sort of conduct, and he could not overlook it.—There were previous convictions against Kirkham and Shon,' and they were fined 10s. The others were fined in the costs, 4s. 6d. MERSEY MISSION TO SEAMEN. RUNCORN.

Daring the holidays several social gatherings have been held at the Seamen's Institute, Runcorn. They were continued on Friday evening, when the workers and their friends, numbering upwards of 100, spent a very pleasant evening. Songs were contributed by Miss Edwards and Mr. F. Ellison,the accompanist being Mr. S. Ravenscroft. Refreshments were served. Dancing afterwards took place, Messrs. E. Johnson and F. Wakefield officiating as M.C.'s. On Monday evening about GO members of the three Bible classes held their annual tea party and meeting. Mr. W. Shaw (mission- ary) presided, and an address was given by Mr. Shaw .(lay reader). BOGUS COLLECTOR AT RUNCORN.

At the Cheshire Quarter Sessions at Knutsford, on Wednesday, Fred Moss, alias Arthur Brown, 36, fitter, waslindicted for obtaininglodgings and board:from Mary Ann Hunt, Runcorn, by false pretences ; also small sums of money from Jane Mather and Charles Wood. He ob- tained lodgings on the strength of the statement that he had found employment on the canal at 16s per week. Then he went to various houses pretending that the real collector for an assurance company was ill, that he was the superintendent, and that they had better pay him the money. By this means he obtained three sums belenging to two different companies. The Runcorn police stated that prisoner had fraudulently obtained lodgings at Preston and Widnes, but the people would not prosecute.—Prisoner was sent to goal for six months with hard labour.

ST. HELENS.

SMALLPDX AT T. HELENS.

After enjoying immunity for a considerable time, St. Helens has unfortunately, discovered another smallpox case. On Wednesday afternoon a young man named Sephton was removed to the Old Wint Hospital from 67, Peter-street. ST. HELENS BUTCHER'S DEATH.

A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned at a coroner's inquest held in Liverpool on Wednesday rela- tive to the death of John Hatton, aged thirty, butcher, Kirkland-street, St. Helens, who died after being in a gig accident on October 29 last, near Eccleston Park. —lt was stated that deceased, who was under the influence of drink, accompanied by his ostler) took out a fresh mare for exercise. On returning home the animal shied and then bolted. The vehicle collided with a landau and was overturned, both occupants being thrown out. On being driven home, Hatton received medical attention, but he remained in a semi-conscious condition, and a month later he was removed to the Northern Hospital, Liverpool, where the operation of trepanning was performed. When recovering, he tore the bandages off his head, with the result that septic meningitis set in and he died on Saturday. ANOTHER GTFT BY Sin DAVID GAMBLE. The Mayor (Alderman J. Massey) presided at the monthly meeting of the St. Helens Town Council on Wednesday.—Councillor Martin, in presenting the minutes of the Libraries' Committee, said that they held a special meeting that morning to consider the question of a site for the library which Mr. Carnegie had offered to build, at a cost of £3,000, on the condition that the land was obtained free of charge to the Corporation. Atthat meeting they had the pleasure of receiving the following letter, which created great enthusiasm among the members:—"January sth. Dear Mr. Lacey.— Referring to my interview with your deputy-chairman and Mr. Lancaster about the land for the new library at Sutton, I am willing to give the price that the Glass Company ask for it—viz., £213 15s. providing the Corporation carry out the arrangements with Mr. Carnegie for the building of the library.—Yours very truly, DAVID GAMBLE " (loud applause.) The Library Committee had passed the resolution tendering their thanks to Sir David for his generous gift. The land was 835 yards in extent.

WARRINGTON ANGLERS' ASSOCIATION.

THIRTEENTH ANNUAL MEETING.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Anglers' Association was held at the Norton Arms "'tote', on Thursday evening. In the absence of the esteemed chairman (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle.) Mr. W. A.Proud pre- sided over a large attendance. In a letter regretting his inability to attend, Mr. Whittle said he thought the members had every reason to feel gratified at the work accomplished duringthe past year. (Hear, hear.) Afr.W.A.Pßoup wished all present the compliments of the season, and said he hoped they would have health and strength in the coming year to bring more pike to Warringron. (Applause).

===== 0002601_19040109_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REPORT.

The SECRETARY (Mr. Knight); in the course of his annual report, stated that at the end of 1903 they had registered on their books 488 subscribing members, fully and partly paid up, including 16 jnveniles. The rapid strides they had made numerically might be gathered from the fact that they had now reached a total membership of 658, including 115 ladies and juveniles. In addition there were 65 who had not_paid, and4o who had partly paid their subscrip- tions. In addition they had issued travelling cards to 140 members of affiliated clubs,making a grand total of 798 an increase of 148.Thisincrease was in a great measure due to the committee having reduced the fee of the workingman angler from 7s. 6d. to 6s„ at which figure that fee would be maintained, The higher scaled mem- bers would pay Bs. Gd. per annum. It was the intention of the committee to secure additional waters both local and foreign. In dealing wite their private waters, the past season had not been the success they anticipated. Their waters had been flooded, together with foreign waters by the disastrous floods which had occurred at different periods. Of the 43 prize fish 24 had been caught in Association waters and 19 in open waters Taken on the whole the Bridgewater Canal had yielded better sport throughout the past season than any other, water within some miles of theborough, In regard to stocking operations, a thousand roach had been placed in lhe old Quay Canal,and these seemed to have adapted themselves to that patticular fishery. The river Bollin secured a consignment of chub during the early part of the year, and through the generosity of their chairman (Mr. Lyon Whittle) a handsome lot of trout had been turned into the upper portion of the Bollin. In respect of catches, quite a number of really good specimen trout varying in weight from 1 to 2pbs. had been taken from the river. They had also a number of young trout in their rearing ponds at Heatley, and these would be transferred to the river as soon as the weather per- mitted. Theyalso intended to stock the Ackers Pits, I whilst the Bridgewater Canal had received a consignment of perch during the latter portion of the year. The Com- mittee had furthermore decided to purchase 2,500 roach up to 9 inches long to be placed in the Old Quay Canal and Ackers Pits. The angling contests during the past season were a distinct advance on former years. lathe medal championship contests the first prize (a gold medal) was won for the second time in succession by Mr. S. Thomas. The second shield competition was brought off in one event, the charige being most accept- able to all concerned, and an entry of 25 teams was obtained. The Victoria Angling Society were the winners. During 1904, the Association contests would be fished in the Bridgewater Canal. The members dur- ing the past season had been very successful in the catching of prize specimen fish, 101 having been weighed in, as against 94 in the previous year. It was hoped that during the coming year members would pay their subscriptions more expeditiously than they had in the past. With their increasing liabilities the association would continue the popular fee of 65., believing that it came within the means of every mem- ber. The committee honed that a substantial appreciation of their efforts would be shown, and that they would be able to produce a still/better *financial statement at the end of 1905. (Applause.) Mr. E. PICKA.VANCE, jun., in moving the adoption of the report said it was one of the best they had had the pleasure of listening to, and the manner in which it had been drawn up did the secretary great credit. (Hear. hear,) Mr. F. J. Emson seconded, and the motion was carried

===== 0002601_19040109_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====

THE FISCAL PROBLEM.

How the Iron Trade Benefits by " Dumping."

The following extract, from a report which the United States Consul General at Frankfort has sent to his Government shows how " dumping " may operate to our advantage :—" German papers report that an English firm has been awarded the contract for a large gasometer by the city of Copenhagen, being the lowest bidder-210,930 (53,185 dollars) ; the lowest German bid was £11,250 (54,742 dollars). The curious part is that the English firm intends to use German material, finishing it in England. It will be bought in Germany at export prices, which are about cost, or even less. The papers state that the German manufacturers of gas reservoirs cannot purchase their raw material in Ger- many as cheaply as foreign firms can,, and therefore cannot compete with English manufacturers. They also state that similar conditions exist in other branches of home industries using iron. The producers of iron are called upon to revise their prices in favour of home consumers."

Mr. Hugh Bell, the Middlesbrough ironmaster, writes in the" Times " :—" I say there are no valid grounds for apprehension, and, if there were, the remedy is illusory—in fact worse that the alleged disease. No valid grounds, because a trade of which the raw material (pig iron) amounts to 81 million tons a year and the total ultimate value represents over £150,000,000, need not fear—nay, should welcome cheap imports of 10 per cent. of that value. If the Germans and Americans are hindered from coming here they will not close their works and let their men starve. They will dump their over-production in the markets where we sell £00,000,000 worth of goods a year, and will, to that extent, cripple our export trade and deprive this country of the profit accruing to it by dealing with the £15,000,000 which now comes here. If so, T say the last state is worse than the first.

===== 0002601_19040109_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
The Australian Premier and Preference.

"The Manchester Guardian" of Monday says :—The correspondence between Mr. Chamberlain and the Premier of the Australain Commonwealth which is pub- lished to-day throws an instructive light on the position of the latter. Mr. Deakin makes an offer at last, but it is curiously unlike the" colonial offer "that we heard o f He does not offer a substantial preference; he does not even offer to persuade Australia to grant one; he in- vites Mr. chamberlain to travel to the other end of the earth and do his own persuading for himself; and he offers him—an audience. Empty compliment could scarcely go further. Mr. Chamberlain, one would have thought, hardly needed Mr. Deakin's long cable to be assured that he might address the voters of Melbourne or Sydney without having brickbats thrown at him, or even to know that he would attract great crowds there and be eagerly looked at, as, says Shakespeare, "our rarer monsters are." Nor did he want to be told that he has great powers of persuasion. What he did want to be told was that Mr. Deakin would do the persuading on his behalf, and this, it is now evident, the Australian Premier feels he cannot promise to undertake. Personally he seems, like Mr. Balfour, to find no grave objection to Chamberlainism excepting its unpopularity. He will stand by in an attitude of neutrality or of non-committal benevolence while Mr. Chamberlain does the fighting. And this is the only "colonial offer" he can m9.ke. It will be interesting to see how the Tariff Reform League, which publishes it to the world, will endeavour to disguise its disap- pointment and dress up its unpromising materials into a presentable stage property.

LATEST NEWS.

FAR EAST CRISIS. War Pending. THE RUSSIAN REPLY PRESENTED.

Viscount Hayashi informed a press representative yesterday that hehad received a telegram from Tokio announcing that the Russian reply had been delivered, and giving the terms of the Note. His excellency was unable to express any opinion on it one way or the other, as to do so would indicate the nature of the reply which must b 3 kent secret until definite action was taken by his Government. This might be expected very shortly. He added that the period of suspense is over so far as Japan is concerned. It is now Russia's turn to wait. The Minister saidhe did not believe the various reports of the movements of Japanese ships or troops as they were being kept so secret that it was impossible for them to become known. With reference to the position in Corea, a Japanese diplomatist said Corea has been so secluded and oppressed by a despotic Power that not only do the people count for nothilig, but persons in high authority do not know how to govern. In order to keep Corea from anarchy some Power must have control, and Japanese interests being the greatest, Japan had the first claim to that control. There has bead talk of neutralising Corea, but this would imply that the country is strong enough to maintain neutrality. In a country where anarchy is chronic there can be no ques- tion of neutrality, and it is necessary that the Power having the largest industrial and commercial interest should also have a preponderating political influence.

PROBABLE IMPENDING NAVAL ENGAGEMENT.

Russian Warships Leave Port Arthur.

PORT ARTHUR, THUR.SDA.Y. Several Russian warships left here at midnight to reinforce the Russian cruisers now at sea.

It is stated that the Combined force will then pro- ceed te meet a Japanese squadron of four ironclads which is approaching Corea- Six Japanese ships laden with coal from Japan, des- tined for Port Arthur have cancelled their charter with a Russian firm here. It is understood that the Japanese Governmont require the coal. Lake Baikal is now frozen over. The normal move- ment of troops along the railways in both directions Continues.

The defences of Port Arthur are now complete, and the Russian authorities are confident of their ability to meet any emergency.

ACTION BY THE BRITISH GOVERNMENT.

The Russian Ambassador and the Japanese Minister. paid a visit to the Foreign Office simultaneously yes- terday afternoon, The British Government are making a final and supreme effort in the interest of peace.

WARRINGTON AS A PORT.

TOWN COUNCIL AND THE DOGiC PROJECT.

Alderman Bennett's Chilling Reception.

" Warrington as a port" was the theme upon which Alderman Bennett based a rosy word-picture of what the future might have in store for Warring- ton if the town had the advantage of a dock in which some of the big vessels which now traverse the Manchester Ship Canal to the city could be berthed. The speech was delivered at Tuesday evening's meeting of the Town Council, the alderman, in I accordance with notice of motion, proposing "That any support which the Corporation may , decide to give to the Manchester Ship Canal bill! be conditional upon an extension of the time for 11 the construction of the Warrington Dock." The proposal met with a very unsympathetic re- i ception, many of the members apparently wearied by I the long debates on other subjects,leaving the Council I Chamber. Others seemed to be disposed to treat the matter very flippantly and although Alderman Bennett made a strong appeal for its serious consideration, no one even ventured to second, the motion which there- fore fell to the ground. At the outset Alderman BENNETT observed that the matter was one of greater importance than any sub- ject which had been brought before the Corporation for many months. The question involved was whether Warrington was ever to be a port. The Corporation were now engaged in important negotiations with the company and if they did not avail themselves of the present opportunity of obtaining what he was going to argue for it would probably be lost and would never return. Proceeding to recall a few facts, the alder- man pointed out that in the Act of 1885, under which the canal was constructed, there was a clause binding the company to construct a dock at Warrington within three years of the completion of the canal. The dock was not constructed and in 1894 the Corporation had some protracted negotiations with the company in regard to the question of dredging the river and the provision of a Warrington dock. Finally an agreement was arrived at giving the Corporation power to construct the dock themselves during a period of ten years. The land was to be given by the company and a wharf was to be made at the entrance of the dock. The manage- ment of the dock, which was to be constructed by capital raised through the Corporation, was to be vested in a Warrington Dock Committee consisting of eight members—five representatives of the Corporation and three gentlemen appointed by the company. Although he was a members of the committee which had the matter in hand, he was not aware until the present day that the agreement, which was duly signed on behalf of the Corporation and the company, was not formally ratified and brought into the Act passed in 1896. Instead of the agreement which gave them power to construct the dock, a clause was inserted— At this stage a number of members retired and Alder- man Bennett was commenting upon their treatment of his proposal when The MAYOR remarked: lam afraid that it is all a waste of time. The matter was discussed at the cor-- mittee meeting and you were the only one in favour of it. It is interfering with the carrying on of the negotiations.

dounsillor JAMES EvAxs: May I suggest in all fair- ness that,considering thejimportance of the matter, the resolution be placed first on the Council summons and introduced at the next meeting. It is a pity that Alder- man Bennett should waste his time on the resolution when the Councillors seem determined to leave the room.

The MAYOR: You were on the committee and heard all he had to say so you know all about the matter. Alderman BENNETT : If I leave it until the next meeting it will be too late.

The MAYOR: You know very well the Ship Canal Company would not entertain anything in reference to this:matter. They say they could have let the land to the'Westinghouse Company if iylad not been tied up. Alderman BENNETT: 'There is a higher authority than the Ship Canal Company. The question is so vital to the interests of Warrington that I ask permission to proceed. 'I hesitate to make a bore of myself at any time but there are duties which make one indict oneself on fellow-townsmen sometimes, even to their apparent, annoyance and displeasure. I feel I should be altoget- her failing in my duty if I did not make the proposal before it was too late. _

Alderman SUTTON: lam prepared to listen to what Alderman Bennett has to say, but I think it will be a waste of time. All the questions have been gone into before by the whole Council. Alderman BENNETT : No the matter has not been discussed for many years. The MAYOR: He is like a boiler. If he doesn't let off steam he will burst. (Laughter.) Alderman BENNETT: Tam prepared to 'receive chaff even from the chair, in performing what .I believe to be a duty. This matter, which we used to regard as absolutely vital to the welfare of the town, has not been discussed for ten years. Now we are losing absolutely the last chance of having a dock at Warring- ton. We have sat here till ten o'clock discussing some petty-fogging details as to some man's character— The MAYOR: Stick to the motion. Never mind talking about that. Alderman BENNETT : I will take your advice. Con- tinuing, he said the agreement arrived at with the com- pany, giving the Corporation ten years in which to con- struct the dock, was apparently over-ridden by the Act —he did not know how or why—and a clause was intro- duced extending the time during which the company could construct the dock to within ten years of the opening of- the canal. That time expired on January Ist this year, and rt meant that unless they moved at this stage when the company were bringing a Bill before the House, and endeavoured to get the protection of Parliament they missed what- ever chance they had of having a dock at Warrington. He regarded it as a more serious thing even than the widening of Warrington Bridge. Many towns had gone to the expense of making waterways at an enormous cost and building docks. and yet they were not nearly so favourably situated for the purpose as Warrington. Thanks to the Ship Canal, Warrington had not to make its own waterway, and if thel agreement had been adhered to they would have had the land on which to construct the docks for nothing. The hnd in question was on the right hand side of Wilderspool Causeway, and the only expense the Corporation would have had to incur was the cost of constructing the dock. They had been waiting and troubling their heads about things of infinitely less importance, until ten years had gone by. The land now reverted to the company, and, unless they obtained protection of Parliament, the only possible site which would be available and feasible would be taken from them. Producing a plan, Alder- man Bennett contended that the dock was already half-made.

The MAYOR: On paper. (Laughter). Alderman BENNETT: This is the plan about which we were all rending the air with exultation and talking about what an important town Warrington was going to be. It is extraordinary how soon we have forgotten this; turned our backs upon ourselves; and sat down to sacrifice what we once regarded as the very greatest boon that could possibly have happened to Warrington. Continuing, the speaker said the site of the proposed dock absorbed the whole of the river bend practically as far as St. James Church, and excavations had already been carried out. The area of the land was 22 acres and the width 330 feet and therefore, without paying for the 'site and without making a waterway, they could have had one of the finest docks in the country if they had had the energy and enterprise to construct it within ten years. Dealing with the con- tention that it would not be worth while to have docks, Alderman Bennett pointed out that an additional ad- vantage to Warrington was that the rates of toll were only three-fifths of the mounts charged at Manchester. Another important point was that a dock would bring the canal, which was at present seine distance from the town, to their doors. Being in intimatecommunication with three of the principal railways of thecountry,lit seemed jto him that the site was tan ideal one. They could not expect the Canal Company to be very enthusiastic in their desire to help Warrington in making a dock. Warrington had not been enthusiastic: it had been asleep. Warrington had looked at the whole question through the wrong end of the telescope. They had been absolutely blind to the possibility of huge ocean-going steamers stopping at Warrington, the traffic being in- tercepted, and Warrington becoming a port of impor- tation and embarkation on a very extensive scale, Instancing the]difficulties which had to be contended with at other towns, the Alderman pointed out that the dock at Preston had not been a success. There, in addition to the cost of the dock, the town had to bear the ex- pense of widening the Ribble for ten or fifteen miles. Glasgow one hundred years ago was probably no larger than Warrington. Now it was the second city of the empire with anenormous shipping trade. Yet Glasgow had not a Ship Canal made for it. The Clyde had to be deepened from the city to the sea and docks had to be constructed out of the city's own resources. If the Council really thought his proposal was of so little importance as not to be worth half an hour's discussion he, at any rate, would have relieved his conscience and done his duty. He could see the possibility of War- rington becoming a port that was worthy of comparison with some of the largest ports in the country. There was no earthly reason why it should not be. -it was on the threshold of one!of thelrichest industrial districts,had one of the finest of waterways, and a preferential tariff in regard to tolls. All that was wanting to make it one of the principal ports was a: little enterprise,energy and foresight. The Alderman ironically remarked that we hugged our Crosfield's steam boats and Fairclough's flats to our bosoms and thought'what a grand thing we were doing. If Warrington was only to be a port for that "twopenny-halfpenny kind of traffic' it was not worth considering. This generation would be wholly failing in their duty, and would be quite untrue to the principles which ought to animate Ipublic-spirited men if they lost that opportunity of intercepting some of the ships that wont to Manchester It and seeing that they were berthed at Arpley. If he

carried his motion he would suzgest that the Corpora- tion should appear before Parliament and 'ask for a clause extending the time for the construction ,of the dock. He didinot care whether the dock was to be constructed by Manchester or Warrington, but some day they would want it. In ten or twenty years hence they would probably say 'After all; that poor old. dreamer was right.:

'There was no seconder to the motion,and the meeting concluded.

OLD FOLK'S TREAT. SUCCESSFUL FUNCTIONS AT THE PARR HALL.

The happiness which arises from giving pleasure • to others must have been shared, in the fullest sense of theword, by those who undertook the arrangement of the annual treat to the aged poor which passed off with gratifying success at the Parr Hall on Thursday. The old folk enjoyed•themselves thoroughly and mani- fested the most cordial appreciation of the efforts put. forth by the large band of Willing workers. At noon the old men haddinner, and a splendid repast it was. Roast beef, pork, mutton, vegetables and plum pudding were served in abundance. There was also an allowance of beer, and bread and cheese and the edibles being demolished the guests settled down for a whiff of the fragmant weed. The assembly numbered about 400, being a slight increase upon last year. Many and Varied were the types represeeted.Fine old veteranswho carried their years well and walked with, firm step, men who in the evening of their days were showing signs of feebleness-and infirmity; and others who could nnly just lay claim to being "old folk" found places round the festive board. The age of the oldest guest. was. 87 years. Everything possible -was done to make the old men feel at home, and it was evident from their smiling countenances that the day would stand out as one of the-happiest in, what in-many cases, is an uneventful life. The meal had been prepared by Mr. and Mrs. A. Hare, under the superintendence of Mr. and Mrs. R. Shackleton, who. have- had charge of ' the culinary department for many years. No one need have any doubt as to whether all the guests fared well, when it is stated that the food consumed during the day, including the aged women's tea, included: 5951b5. of beef, mutton, and pork; 20 boiled hams; 301bs. of boiled beef, 500 mince pies and 41 plum puddings. The wants of the-diners were attended to ; with assiduity by a numerous company, those present ' at the midday function including the -Mayor (Alderman , Bolton), Alderman Sutton (president), Alderman 'Smethurst (vice-president), Mr. Peter Chrimes (hon. treasurer), Mr. T. A. Sutton (hon. secretary), Messrs. George Piling, Richard Shackleton, J. J. Nield, Alfred • Jolley,. J. Sampson, G. Legge, Councillors Forshaw and Rogers (members of the committee), several local clergy and.town councillors. Mr. Percy Randall 'played selec- tions on the organ. After dinner several speeches were delivered. Alderman Surrox asked the guests to drink to the health of the King and Queen and other members of the Royal family expressing the hope that our sovereign would continue to reign "happy and' glorious" for many years to come.. •.

Ttie-toast was honoured with great enthusiasm .the old men cheering lustily and singing• a . verse of the. National Anthem.

Alderman. Surros, on behalf of the committee and . subscribers, then wished all present a very happy New Year; and certainly a more prosperous-one than prob- ably most of them had just experienced: He was afraid many of the old men had undergone greater .difficulties and hardships during the past year than they had suffered in many previous years; owing to bad weather, bad trade and the poor harvest. Let them hope that this year they would be more fortunate in the weather and that trade would's°. improve al/ round that workingmen nail particularly old men who might be able to work would find Nil employment throughout the year and thus enable themselves and their families to be happy and comfortable. He wan pleased to tell them that notwithstanding the fact that trade had been so bad and the calls on the public generally for all kinds of charitable- objects had been so numerous, the subscribers to the fund which provided the annual. treat, had again nobly responded to the appears made to them so that he was in hopes that they would again succeed in obtaining sufficient funds to meet all the expenses of that treat. It afforded him pleasure, in one sense, to such a large gathering and to find the old men look- ing so well. 'He asked them to drink to the health of the subscribers and he was sure it was. the hope of al/ the guests that they would live long and never feel any the worse or poorer for contributing to such a worthy object and asSisting the committee to provide that sub- stantial banquet during the festive season. They had been honoured with the presence of the Mayor during the morning but owing to private business he had been unable to stay bmg. He wished him (the speaker) to express his regret and they hop ad to see him later in the day. (Applause.) The toast was heartily drunk. The Rev. W. BRACECA.MP was called upon to speak and wished the guests a very happy and blessed New Year. It was most discouraging to those who had the welfare of the workingmen at heart to know that trade had been so bad lately. They hoped that very shortly trade would improve and that the old men would be in better positions than they had over occupied in the past. But while trade had been bad they were grateful that so many people were interested in their behalf and provided such a splendid dinner. It depended a great deal upoa themselves whether they would have a happy new year. If they did the best they could and remembered that there was One above who loved them he thought. they were likely tolhave a very good new year. If,a fter all,they felt depressed sometimes and things were not so bright with them and they said they would "go tai the bad" they were likely to go down stream.. If they made up their minds to light against difficulties and endeavour to overcome poverty they would be much happier and the year would go on smoothly. He coped the year would be truly happy and that they would be spared to meet again next year. (Applause.) Two humorous songs were given by Mr. T. Whittle and greatly amused the old men who applauded heartily pErER ClinoiEs also evpressedpleasure at meet. ing his old friends again and wished them a happy New- Year. In regard to the finances he hoped they would be able to find sufficient money to pay for the treat without drawing upon the funds in the bank. Alderman &PITON said it was a work of pleasure to the Committee to arrange the treat and they were much indebted to the ladies and other helpers. The proceedings then concluded. There *as another happy gathering in the evening when between five and six hundred aged women were provided with ft substantial tea. The ladies who pre- sided at the tables were :—The Mayoress (Mrs. Bolton) Mesdames Smethurst, Chrinies, Pilling, J. H. Smet- hurst, Neild, Legge, Ferguson, Holford, Randall, Egerton, Bailey, Snoton and Saurbourn, awl the Misses Tort and Richardson, _

After tea Alderman Sutton addressed the assembly. He said that as they had welcomed their husbands in the morning so now they welcomed the aged women and wished them a very happy new year. la hoped their husbands would have plenty of work during the year, for that would make them happier than ever. Theyha,d entertained during the day something like a thousand personstand theycould take it for granted that that was not accomplished without a great amount of labour. For several weeks they had been making preparations and he cordially thanked the ladies and others who had ren- dered very valuable service. lfe expressed regret that the Mayor could not be with them, but they were honoured with the presence of the Mayoress who was much interested in the treat, and was particularly anxious that the old people should enjoy themselves. (Applause). The ladies and all who had assisted were cordially toanked on the proposition of the Rev. W. Bracecamp. A capital entertainment then followed,a large number of the general public witnessing it from the galleries. The whole of the programme was sustained by the Morrion.s (Will, Harry and Fred) who enhanced the high reputation which they have already gained in Warrington. They are accomplished entertainers and kept the audience in a jovial mood throughout the evening. A particularly amusing item was the " marvellous conjuring entertain- ment," the new boy, as usual, giving away all the little secrets which ought only to be known "behind the scenes." Mr. Harry Merri on's ingenious pictorial sketch "Living pictures from the music sellers " was a novel and highly creditable feature, and the concluding trio- in which a very amateur brass lxind was represented, convulsed the audience. Mr. C. Pollitt ably supplied the pianoforte accompaniments and played two solos. Before leaving each guest was the recipient of a mince pie, a packet. of tea, and an orange.

Hawthorn, and satisfac- tory explanations were given by the Chairman and the Secretary. It was explained that the Society had made .some additional profit by the sale of an appro- priation, but only a proportion dould be included in this . year's balance sheet.—The report was unani- mously adopted.--The three directors who retired, namely, Messrs. A. H. Dunn, B. Farrar, and J. P Jones were re-elected. The auditors, Messrs. E. Bradshaw and F. Pearson were also re-elected.—The meeting terininated with an acknowledgment of Mr. Sutton's services in the chair.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

JUVENILE DEPRAVITY.-A LAD OF FOURTEEN HELPLESSLY _DRUNK AT WARRINGTON. _ _ _

A painful story of juvenile depravity was unfolded to the Mayor and other magistrates at the 'Warrington Police Court on Monday when a boy of fourteen named William Stanfield, of Pinners' Brow, appeared in answer to a summons charging him with having been drunk and incapable in Winwick street on Christmas Day.—When the defendant entered the box there' was some laughter in court.—The Chief Constable: lam surprised to see that grown up people—presumably many of them fathers themselves—consider this a laughing matter.— Sergeant Cheney: His own father is laughing there.— The Chief Constable said the case was a very painful one and if there had been any course of dealing with it other than summoning the boy on what was a most dis- graceful and disgusting charge, he would have taken it. On Christmas night P.C. Cooper (36) was on duty in Winwiek street and saw the defendant lying on the pavement in a helpless state of drunkenness. He was so helpless and utterly inert that the officer had to pick him up and carry him to Sankey- street were a cab was obtained and he was taken to the Police Station. Afterwards he was removed home in the vehicle and the matter was reported to him (Mr. Talbot). Inquiries were made by a detective officer and P.C. Cooper as to how defendant got into such a deplor- able state. In their report they stated that the boy left home about seven o'clock on Christmas night, taking with him a bottle, and went to the Hawthorne Inn, Orford-lane. Here he obtained sixpennyworth of whisky and drank a portion as soon as he got outside. He then went for another boy named William Pendle- ton, 10, Winifred-street, and they, in company with two other boys, went to Town's-end and during that time Stanfield- drank the whisky which remained in the bottle. He went into the Golden Ball Inn twice and obtained threepennyworth of whisky in the bottle each time,the four boys each drinking some. Afterwards Pendleton went into the Golden Ball and obtained three pennyworth of brandy, of which all the boys drank. Stanfield said that was all the drink they had. The case continued Mr. Talbot, showed a deplorable state Of juvenile depravity in young boys on Christmas night, that they should spend their pocket money in purchasing this vile stuff to en- deavour to make themselves men by turning them- selves into beasts. The licensed victuallers concerned were not transgressing the letter of the law, and when interviewed they did not remember having seen the boys. It was quite likely they would not at such a busy time.—Questioned as to how he obtained the money, defendant said it was given to him as Christmas boxes.—A fine of 5s was imposed.

Birch- all and Hayward, A BAD CHARACTER.—James Quigley, Naylor-street, was summoned for making use of threats towards his father, John Quigley, on New Year's Day.—Complain- ant stated that when his son entered the house on the date named he wanted to fight witness and threatened to "knock him out of the house."—The Chief Con- stable said prisoner had been before the Court 22 times, mostly for offences against his parents. He robbed them of clothing and practically lived upon them beating and threatening them when they refused to pander to his idleness and drunkenness. He had been imprisoned for assaulting the police, and at the Quarter Sessions he was sent to gaol for six months for throwing a lighted lamp at his father.—Prisoner was bound over and ordered to pay the costs, or in de- fault go to prison for two months. R.S.P.C.A. PROSECUTION D I SM I SSE D.—Michael Toole, Cloth Hall Yard, was summoned for having 011 December 23rd illtreated a horse by work- ing it whilst in an unlit state. George Buckley. Knutsford-rbad, was charged with causing the animal to be worked.—Mr. Browne defended.—P.S. Smith stated that he was on duty in Wilson-Patten-street and met Toole in charge of a horse attached to a lurry laden with sods. The animal was very lame on the off- fore leg and could scarcely put the foot on the ground. On his instructions the horse was taken to the stables and on examining it witness found a cut an inch and a half long and half an inzli wide on the fetlock. The

wound was festering and a large quantity of blood was oozing from it. The defendant Buckley said the horse had been suffering from lameness and he sent Toole out to try it, with orders that should it show any signs of lameness it should at once be brought in.—lnspector Gibson R.S.P.C.A. give evideace.—Mr. Browne said that one of the horse's feet had been shod too tightly. There was no sign of lameness when the animal left the stable, and it had reached its destination with the lead of sods when the sergeant came upon the scene. No wilful cruelty was committed, and he contended the case ought never to have been brought into Court.—Mr. Hewitt, veterinary surgeon, having given evidence, the magis- trates dismissed the case, at the same time expressing the opinion that the Society was justified in bringing it forward.
