TUESDAY.—Before Alderman Sutton (in the chair), and

Hutchinson.

A CAB DAMAGED.—James Cooke, of no fixed resid- ence, was charged with having been drunk and dis- orderly opposite the Royal Court Theatre on Monday night, and having done damage :to a cab door, the property of Mr. Edward Connor, amounting to 18s.—It appeared from the evidence of,Police-constable Sweeney and George Daniels, cab driver, that the prisoner was outside the theatre drunk. de wanted to get into Daniels' cab, but he was, told that it was engaged. He insisted upon getting into the cab and opened, the door, which swung against an electric tram pole and was smashed off. Cooke was then conveyed to the Police Station in the cab.—The Chief Constable observed that the damage had already been made good by someone, but that did not clear the prisoner from the criminal responsibility.—Fined 10s. and costs.

PUBLIC-HOUSE SCENE.—Thomas Warburton, Gorsey- lane, was summoned for having been disorderly at the "King and Queen "Hotel,Fadgate,Lane and refusing to quit when requested to do so on December 30th. He was further charged with breaking a pane of glass, doing damage to the extent of £l.—Mr. Browne prosecuted, and said this was an extremely bad case. Defendant, who had been known to Mr. Clare, the licencee, for a number of years, was served with a glass of beer at the house on the date in question. Subsequently he became most quarrelsome and wanted to fight. As he refused to leave the premises he had to be ejected and on the way out he broke a pane of stained 'glass. He then went to the Police Station and stated that he had been robbed of £9 at the hotel and that there was a lot of gambling going on. There was no truth in the statement.—The Chief Constable said the matter was carefully investi- I gated by Sergeant Williamson and an officer who reported ' that it was a bogus case.—Evidence was given by P.S. Williamson and Mr. Frank Berry.—Defendant expressed regret at what had occurred. He did not say that he had been robbed of the money but he had lost it some- where.—The Chief Constable said defendant had been convicted five times previously and had been fined twice for having been drunk on licensed premises.—The magistrates fined defendant in the costs (23) and ex- pressed the unanimous opinio4 that there was no truth in his statement about the alleged robbery. CRUELTY TO HonsEs.—Septtmus Kay, Southport, MS summoned for cruelty to two horses by working them whilst in an unfit state on December 24th and George Henry Turner,his employer,was charged with causingthem to be worked.—lnspector Gibson, R.S.P.C.A. said the animals were attached to a furniture van. The chain horse was very lame op both fore legs, and the shaft horse was in poor condition, very exhausted,and reel- ing. Kay said he had driven the horses from Southport and it had taken seven and a half hours to come the last ten miles—Mr. A. J. Willett, solicitor, gave evid- ence and said both horses were unfit to work and, in his/opinion, gross cruelty was committed—Turner was fined £1 and costs and Kay was ordered to pay the costs.

WONDERFUL AS A TONIC.

There are many people who, although not ill enough to go to bed, are still far from well. They cannot tell just what is the matter but they are not feeling right. They are run down or tired. Their systems need a tonic. _

To—thousands who suffer in this way the discovery of Miss Lily MacKenzie, of Miter Hall, Newcastle, Staffs, will be very interesting. Miss MacKensie says : "I was thoroughly run down and out of sorts. I used to feel depressed and worried. Everything I did seemed to be a labour to me, and I hardly knew what to do. I was in this condition when a friend recom- mended me to try Dodd's Kidney Pills which, she said, had done her so much good. "I took my friend's advice and, after I had used Dodds Kidney Pills for a, little while, I began to feel my health and strength returning. I kept on with the Pills and was soon restored to health. I believe, if I had not used this remedy, I would have soon fallen into a very serious illness. If I should ever again find myself in that unhappy state I will most certainly take Dodd's Kidney Pills :it once."

A LEAP FROM RUNCORN BRIDGE. Determined Attempt at Suicide.

About four o'clock on Thursday afternoon, Samuel Cooke, aged forty-seven' of Halebank-terrace, Ditton, and employed as a clerk at Gaskell Deacon's chemical works, Widnes, made a determined attempt,to ccm- mit suicide by jumping from Runcorn Bridge into the Manchester Ship Canal. The under side of the bridge is 75 feetabove high water,and Cooke's leap was fully 85 feet. Cooke was seen by several men on the banks of the canal and on the Transporter Bridge works to mount the rails of Runcorn Bridge, and without hesitation, jump off and drop like a stone into the water.--He sank, but after so long a drop he quickly rose to the surface, and he struggled most vigorously, in the meantime Mr. Szubbs, boatbuilder to Messrs. Abel; Mr. Richard Aspey, the ferryman; and Mr. Charles Wood had put off in a boat, and quickly reached the drowning man, and hauled him on board with a boathook. He was landed at Abel's Yard, and carried into a hut, where Police-constable Preston, Mr. W. Booth, and others applied artificial respiration. Detective Mottershead arrived on the scene with the ambulance car and some blankets. Mr. Cooke wa stripped, and placed in the blankets, and attended to by Dr. M`Lennan, who stated he was suffering from shock, but that there were no bones broken. Afterwards Detective Mottershead and Police-constable Preston removed Mr. Cooke to the Cottage Hospital. There is no doubt of Mr. Cooke's motive in making this desperate leap. Fastened to his coat by a long piece of string was a small, wide-necked bottle, such as is used in chemical laboratories, and in this bottle was the following remarkable letter ;—"I feel no fear. I commit my body to the water and my soul to my Maker, as I offered it to Him last night." It appears that on Thursdayafternoon, at his office in Gaskell Deacon's works, Mr. Cooke made his will, and had it witnessed by two office boys. Heaftervvards left the building, and did not return. In his drawer was found this will and a sheet of note paper on which were the words "Runcorn Bridge." It appears that Mre Cooke has been very depressed lately owing to family troubles, and had recently consulted a doctor.

St. John's Presbyterian Church.

The members of the Literary and Social Society connected with St. John's held their annual New Year's Social Meeting in the church schoolroom on Tuesday evening. Mr. D. R. Hodge, vice-president, occupied the chair, and there was an exceptionally large attendance of members. The Chairman, in his remairks, welcomed all present and intimated that the meetings already held during the session had been so successful and well attended that it had been decided. commencing with the new year, to meet weekly in- stead of fortnightly as hitherto. The Society was having a useful and successful career and he cordi- ally invited all connected with the congregation to become members. The evening's programme was opened with instrumental selections by the Misses Chesworth, after which songs were sung by Mrs.P.H. Brown, Miss Chesworth, Mr. J. Hunter, whilst an amusing reading was given by Miss Dewar. After tea had been served various games were entered into with zest by all present and a thoroughly enjoyable evening spent.

THE BRANCH LIBRARY SCEIENIE APPROVED ALDERMAN SUTTON'S ANTIQUATED VIEWS, The minutes of the Museum Committee contained the report on the branch library question as pablished by us lastweek.

Alderman BexxErr, in proposing the adoption of the minutes, explained the proposal to establish branch libranies. It was recommended to establish one or two experimental delivery stations in different parts of the town, and one or two shopkeepers in different parts would be provided, and any borrowercould either select from the books on hand or have any book in the cata- logue on short notice by making application. They would try to plant these centres at suitable distances from the central library and in prominent positions. They also proposed to establish issue centres in the elementary schools so tkat teachers would be enabled to encourage the love of reading amongst those scholars who might be inclined to read. The success of this department would largely depend upon the spirit in which it was administered in the schools, and if children could be got to take an interest in good books it would be an incalculable advantage to them in later years. Having dealt with the estimated cost of the new stations, he pointed out that really the idea would be a sort of counter-attraction for the working classes to the public-houses and other things which were not for their welfare. As an experiment it was proposed to start a reading room at the Silver-street sehool and to make it as bright and as attractive as possible. If the experiments were fourid to be a success ' they might be extended and an extension would be the best justification. He only wished ex-Councillor Upson could have been present to see the adoption of this proposal. (Laughter.) No one could tell what good results might ultimately follow his scheme. Councillor Er.usoN seconded. Alderman SUTTON said that however desirable it might be to give the proposed comforts to the working classes it was at the same time very necessary, and at the present time particularly so, to be careful in res- pect to these small sums. It might be all 'very well to confirm the estimate of what it might cost, but it was always found that these things cost a great deal more to carry out than was represented in the estimates. His opinion wasthat the town of Warrington was not yetiso large that they could not afford to wait some time before they carried out these arrangements. He did not think it was too far from any part of the town for a borrower to go to the Museum if hewanted a book. There had been at various times endeavours to carry out what was now suggested,and the result had been that partieshad gone to these rooms simply to en- joy themselves, and would probably, if they wished to light their pipes, tear a leaf out of a book. Such a reading room had been established by the late Rev. M. Schonberg in John street, and he had told him (the speaker) that he was obliged to give it up because peo- ple went to it simply to amusethemselves. He thought that if people could come from any part of Warrington to see some stupid play or entertainment—(laughter)— they could very well manage to get their books in the same way and to wait a little longer before they in- creased the rates in the way proposed. Councillor STorr said he had had some experience lately among the working classes, an tv d he might s,gest Ithat Alderman Sutton in his old ago, though he wished to say it with all due respect, had got a little out of touch with the desires of the working classes. He thought he could speak front experience. He had rtienl tio'ned the matter ef branch libraries very often during the last 12 months, and his experience was that the working classes were desirous of having these facilities granted to them. (Hear, hear.) He had pointed out to them that they. must be prepared to pay more if they desired to have these facilities, but that did not seem to prevent them desiring to have them. If there was one suggestion more than another of the Museum Com- mittee which he thought should be carried out it was the question of school issue centres. One headmaster who had a large school in his charge in the town saw the danger of the boys and girls being led to read trash, and had himself borne the. expense of purchasing 100 standard works in order that the boys might have the facility of borrow- ing books at the school. He pointed out that a new- agent hadtold him the previous week that the sale of certain books intended for boys (which he named) reached ever a million copies a week. Such books as he named he contended were extremely harmful, and it was essential that they should do all they could to create a love for healthy reading. Still he did not think that any one individual, like the schoolmaster, should be asked or expected to do what he considered wasa public duty. In conclusion he said that though they were limited to a 11d, rate he thought a portion of the expense of the schaol issues should come out of the education rate.

Alderman BENNETT, in a brief reply, said with all due I respect to Alderman Sutton he was quite indisposed to say it was wrong to go to see a" stupid play" or 'even a football match—(laughter)—but still they were not the highest things in life, and it was desirable that everyone should have an opportunity to improve his mind and equip himself for the duties in his station in life. They must not forget those who lived in poor streets, arid they must seek their welfare equally with the others of their townsfolk. The whole thing that they proposed was purely experimental, and he asked that they should not discourage them. The Minutes were then adopted. WARRINGTON FAIRS. PROPOSED ABOLITION—STRONG OPPOSITION.

COUNCILLOR AVAGINN AND THE RAG MARKBT.

The Markets Committee met on December 14th, when itwas reported that the total income from all sources for the past four weeks was 4410 15s. 7d.; correspond- iugperiod last year, £123 7s. lid. ; decrease .217 12s, 4d. It was resolved that the Town Clerk be instructed to apply to the Secretary of State for an order abolishing the two half-yerirly horse and cattle fairs and the plea- sure fairs. _

Alderman HUTCHINSON raved the confirmation of the minutes, and said the committee had, through the Town Clerk, applied to the Secretary of State for an order to abolish the pleasure fairs. The Committee could not interfere with what took place on private property, but they could collect the tolls. Councillor ROGERS seconded the motion.

Alderman SMETH URST approved of the alpolition of the pleasure fairs, but he could not understand why they should ask for an order to abolish the horse and cattle fairs.

The MAYOR: We have no place for them. Alderman SM ETHURST pointed out that the fairs had beenheld from time immemorial.

The MAlroa: Would you spend .£BOO on paving the land for a cattle market?

Alderman SMETHURST : Spend what youcan if you can get a return for it. Alderman BENNETT said the fairs had been in exist- ence for 500 or 600 years, and he contended that unless some very emphatic reason was given they ought not to be done away with. There was a great deal of plea- sure to be derived from the pleasure fairs. There was no special reason why smallpox should be brought into town by "hobby-horses" and swing-boats. (Hear, hear.) The MAYOR: But the fairs have brought smallpox into the town.

Alderman BENNETT : They may have done so once. If there was no pressing reason for the abolition of the fairs they ought not to be in a hurry, and he admitted that there was a great deal about the pleasure fair that was attractive. The horse and cattle fairs ought not to be abolished without some very satisfactory reason being given. Alderman SurroN pointed out that the Council had on more than one oc3aston resolved that the fairs should be stopped, as they were not at all in the interests of the town. (Hear, hear.) The horse and cattle fairs had diminished to Such an extent that it was not advisable to incur expenditure in providing places for them.

UNDER the CLOCK or GOOD BOOTS.—Advt. Poaching at Sankey. At the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednes- day, before Colonel Ridgway and Mr. F. A. Frost, Henry Johnson, Sankey-bridges, was summoned for trespassing in search of rabbits on land in the occupa- tion of Henry Fairclough, at Great Sankey, on December 13th. He pleaded guilty.—Mr. E. A. White prosecuted, and said that on the date named, at 8.30 in the morning two Bewsey keepers were on duty when they saw the defendant and two other men ferreting for rabbits. Johnson and one man were in a field occupied by Mr. Fairdough, and they were seen to place a net over a rabbit hole. Defendant walked towards the keepers, and when they got up he ran away. The keepers shouted that he need not run away as they knew him. The keepers then chased the other men, but they got away. Fourteen nets and a live ferret were found by the keepers at the place where the men had been.—A fine of 10s. and -costs was imposed, and the advocate's fee was also allowed. WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

A THREE HOURS' SITTING.

Important Business.

The Town Council met on Tuesday evening and sat until nine o'clock. Several important matters were brought forward, and schemes which received approval were the proposal of 'the Museum Committee to estab- lish delivery stations and the scheme introduced by Alderman Smethurst for the widening of Warrington Bridge by cantilevers. The Mayor (Alderman Bolton) presided, and there were also present :—Aldermen Fair- dough (deputy mayor), Bennett, Smethurst, Sutton, Pierpoint, Hutchinson, Councillors James Evans, John Evans, S. Davies, T. H. Ashcroft, Stott, Gough,Brough, Ellison, Lord, Pemberton, P. Peacocke, H. Thornton, Forshaw, John :Roberts, Wilson, Rogers, Wilkinson, Tinnion, Burton Maginn, with Messrs. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk), S;Wilson (deputy town clerk), J. Fair- hurst (borough treasurer), T. Longdin (borough sur- veyor), W. S. Haddock (gas engineer), J. Moore Murray (director of education), Dr. J. Gllest Gornall (medical officer lof health), Charles . Madeley (librarian), F. V. L. THE SMALL-PDX OUTBREAK.

Ata meeting of the Health Committee on December and, the Medical Officer reported a serious outbreak of smallpox in the north part of the town,while at a meet- ing" on 'December 16th tie dealt with the steps which had been taken by him to deal with the disease. It was resolved that the Markets Committee be requested to consider the advisability of abolishing the pleasure fairs.

Alderman Surrox said that with reference to the small-pox ontbreak there had been taken into the hos- pital, since the first case occurred, about 70 cases. However, owing to the prompt and energetic steps taken by the Medical Officer of Health, and the inspec- tors and assistants generally, encouraged and supported, he might say, by every member of the Health Com- mittee, the number of cases in the hospital at the pre- Lent time hadlbeen reduced to about 20, and that number included the three near cases brought in within the past week. Therefore, with the continued careful,andactive exertions on the part of the Medical Officer and his staff, the borough would be free from the epidemic in some two or three weeks. He thought he ought not to omit to mention that in successfully preventing the spread of the disease beyond the limits it had reached, they were inagreat measure indebted to the willingness of the employers of labour, in acceding to the request not to allow a workman to come to work who had been residing in a house from which a case of small-pox had been removed without a certificate stating that all danger was passed. There was no doubt that in so complying with the request the employers had very In Lterially aided and assisted the officials in preventing the spread of the disease. He hoped he might have the pleasure that time next month, of announcing the fact that the disease had been entirely done away with, and that they would hear no more about it. (Hear, hear.) He would like also to call attention to the resolution requesting the Markets Committee to consider the advisability of abolishing the pleasure fairs. He did not understand how it was that the fairs had been continued after the decisions which had been more than once given by that Council. He had at various times in the Council con-

tended that they ought to be abolished, and his own opinion was that the fairs held in the district of Town's-end were responsible for the epidemic of smallpox. He did not think the Markets Committee or any other committee should endeavour totincrease their incortte to the detriment of the health of the town and to the detriment of the shopkeepers or ratepayers. He hoped the Markets Committee, when the question of abolishing the fairs came before them, would decide that they should be abolished. Councillor JAMES EVANS seconded. Alderman BENNETT said he wished to associate him- self cordially with everything that the Chairman had said, and he believed that Dr. Gornall and Ms staff had exerted every effort to check the spread of the disease. The case was serious enough as it was, and 70 cases since the outbreak was no little matter, and they might flatter themselves that it was the energy that had been shown by Dr. Gornall and those who assisted him that the outbreak had been kept within the proportion it had. One of the few unwise things done I amongst the many 'wise things which the Medical Officer had permitted was to allow the re-vaccinations to take place in the public schools, and if there was any danger of the disease being carried from place to place it was unwise to allow these people to congregate in the schools since it was possible that they might leave behind them the germs of disease. In spite of all that had been done, the state of the health of the town was even yet not satisfactory, and during the past month their death-rate had been amongst the highest in England. Some people said that diseases werel sent by God, but he thought they were the fault of their own dirty habits, and they should do every- thing that human energy could do to secure immunity. The town as a whole should take the lessen to heart, and put itself into harmony with the latest sanitary laws and try by that means to reduce this lamentable death-rate.

NEW PLANT AND NEW MANAGENENT AT LONGFORD. .

At a meeting of the Sanitary Works Committee held on December 2nd, the Assistant Manager submitted a plan of 'a proposed cast iron storags tank of 3,000 gallons capacity, and it was resolved, that the plan be referred to the Chairman and Assistant Manager for consideration and report. Mr. P. G. Ruddock attended the committee and explained in detail the analysis of the manure made by him in its different stages of manufacture in accordance with the instructions of the committee, when it was resolved, that the tender of Messrs. Thomas Beeley. and Sons to supply a calandria for No 3 evaporator at Longford Depot, and that of Messrs. Manlove, Elliott and Co. to supply a vacuum pump at £3lO be accepted; also that the assistant engineer be authorized to place the orders for the above calafidria and pump at once and request de- livery at the earliest possible date.—At a meeting on December 16th the following resolutions were passed: —That the tendef of Mr. T. W. Ward, of Sheffield, to supply a second-hand boiler at £382 10s., delivered at the Longford Depot, be accepted,and that a cast iron water tank of 5,000 gallons 'capacity be advertised for. It was also decided that Mr.J..T. A. Parfitt be appointed the manager of the Cleansing Department, at a salary of £lsolper annum rising:by £lO per annum to a maximum of £2OO, and thatiMr. Richard Wilson be appointed the , manager of the Longford Depot, at a salary of £l5O per annum, with annual increases of £lO to a maximum of £2oo.—The Town Clerk submitted the draft agreement with Messs. Martin land Company, with respect to Professorr Dunbar's plant to be erected at Longford, and reported that the patentees were prepared to allow the Corporation,to use the patents, appliances andiplant for any quantity of sewage exceeding 500,000 gallons per day for which no charge is to be made, at a fixed annual payment of £350, when it was resolved that the agreement be approved, as well as the annual payment. Councillor JOHN EVANS moved the adoption of the above minutes and explained the need for various new appliances. With regard to the appointments, he pointed out that Mr. Deis having tendered his resigna- tion a short time ago, and that resignation having taken effect on December 24th, the committee decided not to refill his place, but to divide the department and to put the two men who had been work- ing under him in the different departments. The outside portion was now in the hands of Mr. Parfitt, who had previouslyreceived a salary of £135 per annum whilst the Longfordi Depot would be managed by Mr. Wilson, who had previously received a salary of about £l3O per annum. Alluding to Professor Dunbar's ex- periments at Longford, he pointed out that the agree- ment was to enable the Council to secure the benefits offered should the experiments prove a success. Alderman SMETHURST seconded. He said they were anxious to dispense with all smells arising at Longford and, perhaps, they Would thus avoid lawsuits in which they would perhaps spend more money. Councillor PEMBERTON raised an objection to the latter portion of the minutes with reference to the ,experiments at Longford. He did not think that they should be asked to pay anything since no experiments had yet been made; and he and others had voted for them on tin understanding that it would cost the Council noth mg. The MAYOR explained that the agreement was to secure to the Council the option of having sewage up to 500,000 gallons dealt with free should the experiments prove a success. Without such agreement they might be called upon to pay from the first gallon. Councillor GOUGH asked what portion of the town's sewage it, was proposed to send to Longford? The MAYOR: We have no sewers at Longford, T think. These are only experiments to treat sewage; Councillor Gouoir: I underatand that if this were carried out we shonld have to alter the main sewer.

The MAYOR: Oh no. We should probably make a sewage farm somewhere and use this treatment. Councillor JOHN EVANS replied briefly, and the minutes were adopted.

The MAYoFt: The present law requires that all places where horses and cattle are sold shall be flagged and paved.

Alderman HEA.TON alluded to the visiting of the market by "dirty, filthy Jews, with their dirty, filthy clothes," and said they would have to stop the market also if they wished to prevent the risk of spreading disease. There was not a more central place in Lancashire and Cheshire for a horseand cattle fair than Warrington, and he could only say that if the Markets Committee had any energy about them they would have provided suitable places for the fairs long before this. At Crewe, a most out-of-the way place, there were three plans where cattle were sold. We had good horses but they had to be taken to Chester, Crewe, Shrewsbury, or Nantwich for sale.

Councillor ROGERS explained that it was not the intention to abolish the weekly cattle sales. There was no doubt that smallpox had been introduced by some of the small shows. It was not intanded to abolish the shewi altogether. If a respectable circus or a wild- beast show visited Warrington the committee would provide a site. Councillor JAMES EVANS Could not see the force, the wisdom, or the usefulness of the resolution with refer- ence to the abolition of the fairs. The resolution was the outcome of the indifference of the Markets Com- mittee in the past. No provision had been made for the fairs. Orford-lane was a most unsuitable place, and he suggested that instead of the Council accepting the two resolutions about the fairs, that the matter should be referred back to the committee, who should be asked to make the necessary provision. The committee had to face the fact that it was their duty to provide a suitable site. In the markets bundle after bundle of second hand clothes were sold, and who knew where they came from,and who cared? As a mattet of fact disease might be transmitted from town to town in the way he had named. What they should do was to establish a place and make it a profit to the ratepayers. Councillor MaarsN failed to see why they should do away with the horse and cattle fairs. All over the country where horse and cattle fairs were encouraged they had been a source of revenue. (Hear, hear.) And he knew of several places in his own country which made their living out of them. In Warrington they had no money to throw away. He once made an attempt to bring a horse and cattle fair to Warrington, but the members knew how the Markets Committee treated,him. They needed, he contended, a horse and cattle fair more than any other town he knew of in the country. It was the best centre in all Lancashire and Cheshire. It was said they had in Warrington the finest rag market on earth—(laughter)—and they seemed to have a free place for all the rogues, vagabonds, bank- rupts and thieves in England to sell in to the disadvant- age of the shopkeepers who had to keep the town going. (Laughter). Councillor JAMES EVANS : Well done, John! (More laughter.) Councillor MA.GINN, continuing, said if the Corpora- tion would curtail that famous rag market, and make provision for the horse and cattle fairs it would be of greater benefit to the town as well as a greater assist- ance to the ratepayers. (Hear, hear.) Alderman HUTCFIENSON, in reply, said he had been as anxious as anyone that the fairs should be continued, and he was very sorry indeed when he was over-ruled in committee, and it was decided to remove the horse and cattle fairs from Winwick-street. Those fairs were at one time held half-yearly and brought in a consider- able amount of money, and they were ef very little inconvenience to the borough or to the traffic of the borough. However, it was the wish of the committee that the fairs should be removed from the street and a plot of land in Orford lane was selected,but year after year the fairs had dwindled down and the receipts only amounted to £4 half-yearly. A portion of that money had to be paid to the Open Spaces Com- mittee and there was, as a result little benefit derived from the fairs. Nearly everyone who went to Orford- lane said it was not a proper place in which to sell horses and cattle. The committee could not at the present time see their way to spend £3,000 or £4,000 for the purpose of providing a sale yard. The Corporation certainly had an old charter and they could collect tolls from anywhere. The sales at the Norton Arms Hotel and the Seven Stars yard had taken a great deal of interest out of the old cattle and horse fairs. In a short time he hoped the Council would allow his committee £3,000 or £4,000 for the provision of a proper place for the sales, such as they had at Crewe and Wrexham. Alderman PIERPOTNT rose to speak, but was ruled out of order.

The MAYOR. The Chairman has replied. Councillor MAGINN : I know hundreds of towns- (Cries of" Chair," "chair," "chair.") The MA.YOR then put the motion for the confirmation of the minutes to the meeting, but owing to a misunder- standing it was lost. His Worship explained that none of the minutes had been carried. Lunacy. Alderman Dr. John Milson Rhodes, in a statistical communication to the "Poor Law Officers' Journal" of this week, mentions the certified insane in Lanca- shire as increasing from 9,697 in 1893 io 12,311 in 1903. The increase is much greater in workhouses than asylums ; at the same time, there is great varia- t ion in the number of certified lunatics from different parts of the county. Careful examination shovis that the system of dealing with lunatics is not uniform throughout the counties of Lancashire and Cheshire. Speaking ef the percentages for Blackburn, Bolton, and. Bury, the writer points out the unfairness of Black- burn receiving only £5OO odd per annum from the County Council per 100 lunatics, while Bolton receives -S.:000. Dr. Rhodes says he is not alone in holding that the causes 9f the increase in the number of certi- fied insane must be sought for elsewhere than in the ' actual increase of the number of insane. In this con- nection he cites the following reasons given by the Scottish Lunacy Commissioners and practically adopted by the English, for believing the same thing :—(1) the erection of new asylums for pauper lunatics ; (2) the readier means of access to asylums due to increased facilities for travelling; (3) the gradual dying out among the public of feeling of dislike and suspicion towards asylums; (4) the ,greater readiness of the poorer classes to send then- relatives to asylums as pauper lunatics ; (5), the growing unwillingness of the poorer classes to submit to all that is involved in keeping an insane relative at home ; (6) the greater willingness of the authorities to recognise claims to relief on the ground of insanity; (7) the grant in aid • and (8) the widening of medical and Vublic opinion as te the degrees of mental unsoundness which may be certified to be lunacy.

FATAL FALL DOWNSTAIRS AT HALTON. An inquest was held at the Village Hall, Halton, on Monday, on the body of Ellen Pollard, wife of Richard Pollard, of 59, Main-street, Halton. Deceased, who was aged 77, fell from the top of the stairs at her residence on thenight of December 14th, and died on January 3rd from a broken thigh. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death,"and recommended that there might be some guard rail placed at the top of the stairs.

PENKETH.

OLD FOLK'S TREA.T.— The annual treat to the old people of Penketh and district was held in the Wesleyanschool last night(Priday), and a full report of the proceedings will be found in these columns next Saturday.

CINDERALLA.—The second cinderella of the season was held in Sankey schools or* Thursday evening, and proved a universal success. Nearly 100 people assem- bled and dancing was indulged in to music supplied by Mr. A. Starkey, whose programme was very attractive. Messrs A. Hodgkins= and E. H. Coates acted as M.C's. The rooms had, as usual, 'leen charmingly decorated by the ladies of the committee. During the dance, pro- gressive whist was played by the older ladies and gentlemen. Miss Ashurst again catered, and gave the utmost satisfaotion.

ALARM op FlRE.—Consideralke excitement was caused in the village at midnight last Saturday, by the buzzer at the Penketh Tannery sounding the fire alarm. In a very short time the village was alive with people hurrying to find out the whereabouts of the conflagra- tion, as no signs of it could beseenaround. On arrival at the tannery it was found that by some means or other some cotton waste in the store room had become ignited, but this was quickly extinguished by the aid of buckets of water, the engine not being needed. The danger was over before the crowd arrived, and on learning this, the anxiety was quickly dispelled, and much good humoured badinage freely indulged in, one humorist wishing the crowd "A very happy New Year."

CONNTERSAZIONE.—The 21st annual Conversazione in connection with the Penketh Mutual Improvement Association was held on Wednesday night last in the Wesleyan 'schoolroom. There was a record attendance and this year'sifunction,kept up the excellent reputation it has enjoyed for many years pass. The room had been tastefully decorated for the occasion, a coloured por- trait of the King and Queen, occupying a prominent position, while flags, and evergreens were lavishly placed around the walls. The president, Mr. Thomas Gandy, with Mrs.C. E. Parker, and Mrs. Clarke (hon. sec.) had arranged the evening's entertainment, which consisted of a series of "Tableaux Vivants." in the initial half of the programme. These were a very popular feature, and withal a decided success, each participant having done their utmost to correctly impersonate the character taken. The list of pictures was as follows :—(1) "The Spinner" with the song "When Maiden Loves," from the "Yeomen of the Guard" (by permission) was taken by Mrs. Clarke, and her costume of a Puritan Maid was very neat. No. 2 was " A Nun," which was admirably taken by Miss Winnie Ambler; her dress and attitude being very realistic, " Ava Maria " was sweetly sung while the picture was shown, by Miss Daunt. A very pretty scene was No. 3, entitled "The Fairies," the Misses Rutliand Rosmond Parker looking very dainty in their fairy costumes, and going through a dance, which was much applauded. No. 4, "A Gipsy Scene," was shown by Miss J. Johnson (gipsy) and Miss M. Dodds. The military side had not been neglected, for in No. 5 "The last stand" the horrors and dangers of war, were brought vividly to the minds of the audience. The scene showed the wounding and subsequent rescue of a. soldier by his officer, South Africa being the supposed scene of the picturellThe'officer, Mr. T. Gandy looked terribly in earnest, while the get up of the wounded soldier (Mr. Harry Mainley) was very clever. No 6, "The Sculptor" was taken by Mr. James Watson, who was shown in a studio, putting the finishing touch to a bust, which stood on a pedestal before him. No 7,, "The blind Nydia," was a pretty though touching por- trayal, the part being taken by Miss Ambler. No 8, showed a modellof the well knowril soap advertisement "You dirty boy," and created much amusement the old lady being Miss L. Cooke,and the boy Master H. Dodds. No. 9, "When the cat's away" showed a lady's maid (Miss M. Dock's), taking advantage of the mistresses absence, and receiving a bicycle riding lesson. She was aided and abetted in this by a footman (Mr. A. Clarke). The unexpected return of the mistress (Mrs. Clarke), and the guilt of the culprits was splendidly portrayed. No. 10, "Mary Queen of Scots," was a dramatic and thrilling scene, the part of the ill-fated Queen being taken by Miss Winnie Ambler, while the headsman was portrayed by Mr. H. Ambler. The block'and theformid- able axe,made a creepy feeling prevalent in the audience the scene being very realistic. The series closed with a scene of "A sleeping camp," the central figure being that of Mr. W. Upton, whq as a sentry in full marching order, watched over the safety of his sleeping comrades. the alarm, and the turn out to defend the camp being realistically shown in subsequent scenes The pictures were shown more prominently and owed much of their success, to the limelight effects, worked by Mr. James Woodward. The songs" Sous of the Mother Land," and "Tommy Atkins, were rendered by Mr. H. Plumpton, to the scenes of "The last stand" and "On Guard "respectively. Mrs. Parker and Mrs. Dow were responsible for the accompaniment on the pianoforte, while Mr. Parker announced the various scenes, and acted as stage , manager. Another interest- ing portion of the proceedings was the " Sixpenny Competition," for articles, the manufacture of which had not exceeded sixpence. Many very pretty articles had been entered, and the audience voted for the article they considered best, the winner being Miss Florrie Woods, Miss M. Woods and Miss Belle .Mainley being second and third. The refreshments'were, of course, a very important feature, and were thoroughly enjoyed. The remainder of the evening was given up to vocal and other items and games,'the following taking part in the programme. Comic song, Mr. Man Watson, and Messrs. A. 0. Clay, H. 'Mainley, and W Upton and Miss Cookealso contributed items. A comprehensive vote of thanks was proposed by Mr. Birch and seconded by Mr. Midwood, and the meeting closed by the singing of the National Anthem.

SEP SOWERBY'S SUCCESS.

BY AARON WATSON, Author of "Brown Studies," "Waifs and Strays," "For Lust of Gold," Sze. -

Somebody was always going away from Bickers- dyke! The village was huddled up on the hill-side be- tween two "becks," or brooks, which here flowed out of their separate valleys into a little bay, and lost themselves in the uneasy waters of the North Sea. Sometimes a house was blown down in a gale, or was battered into ruinousness by some piece of wreckage hurled at it by a winter storm. Nobody rebuilt or repaired the wrecked or damaged cottages. Where roofs, and joists, and walls fell together into a heap of confusion, there they lay. At intervals of a few years a little brick cottage would be built a mile or so inland, in the midst of a fair space of garden ground; and whilst it was in course of construction a grizzled, weather-beaten, sailor-looking man would hover about it all day, anxious and helpless, but proud; for every brick that was being placed on the top of another was paid for out of his own pocket, and in his sea-chest down at his lodgings in the village there was, carefully packed away at the very bottom, with all his other belongings piled above it, a document which con- veyed to him the freehold of the piece of land on which he stood.

It was one of the Bickersdyke lads, come -flack after a life spent in strange ports and on far seas. They were always skippers, those who came back —men who had made enough money to retire upon. It was worth no other man's while to return; fot there was no industry but the fishing, which was here a more dangerous and a more laborious occu- pation than at any other place along the whole coast of the broadest of English shires. It was seldom, then, that any Bickersdyke mar came back; but somebody was always going away. The village swarmed with children, who, if they were boys, were born seamen and adventurers. They took to roaming as naturally as the young seagull casts itself from the cliffs and finds the support of its wings.

.. _ And Sep Sowerby had determined with himself that his own time had come.

He was neither fisherman nor sailor; being, indeed, the youngest son of the great tradesman of the place. There had beea seven of them, where- fore there fell to him, as may sometimes happen even to lads in remote fishing villages, the unde- sirable, because schoolboy-provoking, name oi Septimus. "Annie," he said to a girl who sat beside him on the cliff-tops, her eyes following the lessening saile of the fishing boats as they glittered red and golden in the light of the westering sun. "Annie," he re- peated, with abrupt decisiveness of tone, "I have made up my mind at last." She turned to him and looked into his face, placing a sun-browned hand on each of his shoulders. "Oh, I knew it would come, Sep. I have been looking for it ever so long. I seem to have been expecting it, and_fearing it, ever since I was a little girl; for everybody goes away from here, and hove could you remain?" "I have remained longer than most, dear. Why, just think of it! lam twenty-four, and even you are eighteen. I have seen all those go who were oi my own school age, and most of those who came after. There will soon be only women and old men at Bickersdyke. But I have tried to stay; you know that, Annie. I have tried even to like the shop, for the old dad's sake. And then-"

"And then what, Sep?" "There was another reason for staying.* Which was?" "Which was that / loved you."

"And is that a worn-out reason?"

"It is far from that, dear; but it has become a reason of another sort. I must go, Annie, if only because of love of you. My heart is too hungry to find happiness here, or to make you happy if I remained here. I must go, dear, and I mean to go at once. You shall hear things about me when I have gone away--" "Sep, it is from you that I wish to hear."

"Oh, you shall hear from me almost every day, and you shall hear about me, too,' he added, with a ring of manly confidence in his voice. The girl rose, with tears in her eyes. She was strikingly fine-featured, and intelligent-looking, and tall.

"I have expected it a long time, Sep," she said. "I have prepared my heart for it. Let us walk along, the cliff path. Perhaps," with an heroically stifled sob, as her heart prophesied of bitterness to come, "this may be for the last time."

Sep Sowerby had been in London two months, and he was almost beaten. In all that vast cauldron of humanity there seemed to be no room for him. Nobody wanted him. There:was nothing for him to do. London had space and occupation for five millions, but not for an additional unit. That was how it struck Sep. He had been a great reader, according to his means. He had not only read everything in Bickersdyke, but every book that was to be begged or borrowed for wiles around; and now there came into his mind a passage that he had never seemed to remember, and the significance of which he had never previously felt. "0 London, stony - hearted stepmother! Thy streets have echoed to the groans of innunaerable hearts."

There was a bill on the hoardings which exactly fitted into the sentiment of his unconscious quota- tion. A dramatist was advertising his play by means of his verses:

"0 cruel lamps of London! If tears your lights could drown, Your victims' eyes would weep them, 0 lights of London town."

He was becoming foolishly lachrymose, he thought —he who had told Annie that she should hear of him —and he thrust his hands into his pockets, shrank his head into his, shoulders, and dashed up Fleet street, heedless of other hurrying wayfarers,

As he was passing the end of Wine Office Court a man came out of that dim entry and lunged heavily against him. He was a young flaxen-haired giant, far gone in drink. The two clung together lest both shoulcl fall. - - -

"Why, It's Dick Christison!" said Sep, holding the other man up. "Who the deuce ''are you that knows me? Sep Sowerby, by jingo! See me to a cab, Sep, that's a good fellow. I'm deuced drunk. I know I'm deuced drunk. When you are in Bohemia you must do as the Bohemians do. Here, hansom, you beggar; hansom!" The driver leaped down and helped Sep to get Christison into the cab.

r ZSepiwould have gone away, but the drunken man seized him by the lapel of his coat.

"Here, that won't do, you know! You come along with me. We're going to have a crack together. Be right as the mail in the morning. Tell him Great Ormond Street. Great Ormond Street, cabby."

And they bowled away to Great Ormond Street, where Christison introduced Sep to some disorderly chambers, with books and papers liberally- bestrew- ing the floor. Then he turned the key in the lock, thrust it into his pocket, pushed Sep into a bed- room, and said:

"You sleep there. Too drunk to tllk to-night. Want talk in the morning. Right as mail—mor—or —fling"; and he fell into a huge armchair, and was immediately sound asleep.

said

"Now you can tell me ' about yourself,." Christison, as they sat at breakfast on the following day, while the sound of the mid-day traffic was in the street.

It seemed to Sep that he must have dreamed that this man was drunk on the previous night: Why, he was now fresh, bright, self-composed; full of life, vigour, good sense, and good humour. They had known each other from Sep's boyhood, Chri ion having the advantage of five years of age and . immense distance of position. This flaxen- haired giant was the wastrel of a family which had an ancient name, a large house, and a small landed estate, a few miles inland from Bickersdyke; and he had disappeared a few years ago in a cloud of ill- repute.

, He pressed Sep in a whole-hearted, most friendly way, to give him the whole of his confidence, and Sep, who was touched by this unexpected kindness, and who was almost sobbing under the memory of Ins late'experiences and approaching despair, told him everything concerning his hopes, and his vanishing. confidence in himself, and even his love for the girl whom he had meant to make happy by his success. "Well, that's all right," said Christison. "It will come all right for Annie vet. you maw depend- We'll see to that. You see, you are just the chap that I want." "But hew?" asked Sep.

"Why, this way. I want somebody to take mr dictation. Not any shorthand fellow, you know. Just somebody who's slick with his pen. I've got a lot to do, and I get a lot •:lone; but, you see, I closet think fast enough for shorthand. This is the way of it, and you might as well know at once. I write for a lot of papers—gossip papers, chiefly— and they say I'm rather a good hand at gossip and scandal. At any rate, they want more of it than I can find time to do, so I'm going to ring you in.' You come here every day, at this time, and you write to my dictation for three hours, and you get three quid a week. How does that strike you?" Sep was for the moment too stunned for thanks. Here was not only something for the needs of a day, but the opening of a career. When he dived into his inner consciousness, and asked himself what he most wanted to do, the answer, was always the same. He wanted to write. And why, he inquired of himself at this moment, as he saw the vista opening before him, should he not write as well as Dick Christison ?

It is the common thought of youth that it can do everything that offers itself to be done. And then, writing seems so very easy! Sep Sowerby had projected himself far into the future before he had finished thanking Christison for his offer; and he had at least this justification—that he had a natural and undeveloped talent for just that kind of work in which this dictation offered him a training.

So the two friends sat down on opposite sides of the table' and Christison stared at nothing for a while. Then he rose up and paced the room, dictating sentences and half-sentences meanwhile, and Sep Sowerby took down all that he said. Christison had done himself injustice when he said that he wrote gossip and scandal. • -

He had really the very finest kind of talent for the lighter varieties of journalism. Among the men who had not yet risen above anonymity there was none who was more in demand than he; and he made a liberal income,which he squandered in many squalid ways. The Bohemia of Warrington and Pendennis has long vanished into the past; but he was determined, even against odds, to maintain the tradition, and, as he said, to do in Bohemia as the Bohemians do.

So, night by night, he stumbled into his chambers in a state of semi-intoxication; and every morning —for he was magnificently endowed in body and brains—he rose up a new man, and in the afternoon dictated to Sep with as much freshness and vivacity as if he had never looked on the wine while it is red.

"This is all going splendidly." he said one day. "Why, you've doubled my income, old chap! I get through more than twice the work that I used to do, and all as easy as winking. tell you what we'll do, Sep, he added. "We'll make it five pounds a week. It's worth that to me, anyhow; and then, as for you—why, Annie will soon come within sight, my boy."

One of Dick Christison's mainstays was the Tray of the World, to which he contributed almost in- numerable columns of notes every week, signing himself "The Prompter." His was the most popular feature of the paper, and might almost be said to be the paper itself. But one day there was no "copy" at the stipulated time. The day of publication came, and still there was no copy. Great consternation in the office in consequence.

But within an hour or two of the first post Sep. Sowerby arrived, and asked to see the editor. _

_ "You haven't got your 6 Prompter '4, copy this week," he said. "That's true; but how do you kndw ? " "Because Christison's on the batter." "But what do you want ? " "To do ' The Prompter.'" _

The editor lay back in his chair and laughed; though, to be sure, this confident young man excepted, there was a mournful lack of comic elements in the situation.

"Look at that," said Sep, taking a parcel of manuscript from his pocket. _

The editor glanced over a few pages, and ex- claimed:

"Why, this is Christison's writing!" "No; it's mine."

The editor sent for the copy of the previous week's contribution.

"Is this your writing too?" "Certainly. You can compare it with what you have in your hand." The editor gave a low, sibilant whistle. He was, as it seemed to him, beginning to see some things in new lights. "You can leave this," he said, "and see me in a day or two."

'When Sep Sowerby bought the Way of the World the following morning he found his whole contribu- tion in it, line f.-r line and word for word, and the evening post brought him a letter from the editor asking for a similar contribution for the next issue, to be sent in early, so that other arrangements might be made if it were not equal to the standard requirements. -

From that day. Sep Sowerby was on the staff of the Way of the World. He had been able not only to fill the place of Dick Christison, but to strike out a new vein. There was a certain rollicking humour in his work, and a most dexterous use of clichés and slang. By this time, too, he knew his London, ai d had the talent for making it appear that he knew far more of it than was actually the case. His work, as was observed by the editor of the Way of the World, who had originality neither of ideas nor of phrases, "palpitated with actuality." As for Christisdn, he wondered what had become of Sep Sowerby, but knew nobody of whom he could inquire. When his copy was sent back after the week during which his contribution had lapsed, he took it all in good part. "It's just what I deserved," he said. "Besides; if Sep Sowerby has gi.en me the slip, I've more than I can do without doing this." •

And so he went on his ;my, heedless, not even looking at the Way qf the World to see in what fashion his former contribution was continued. As for Sep, it was said that no man who had appeared in London journalism for years past had discovered such a knack for his particular kind of work, or had been so much run after for his contri- butions by those editors who are just outside the pale of formal and regular journalism. So, of course, he got "swelled head." He took chambers from a legal friend, in Pump-cOurt, Temple, and furnished in a curiously mixed taste, with abundance of ornamental knick-knacks, and went out into what he had come to regard as society, and made a collection of cards in a tray .on his mantelshelf, and thought less and less of Annie, and said to himself that "a journalist who had got Into the swim as he had done might marry almost anybody that he pleased." He worked like ten men, and took his pleasure like five; until it came about that, writing in his lonely chambers until the morning sunlight filtered In at the windows, he determined, after his floor had become bestrewn with pages of copy, to write a letter that had been long in his mind; and, writing it, he fell forward, his head on his arms, and wrote no more.

Dick Christison was not an angry or revengeful man; but he had heard, after many montks of not caring about the thing. how Sep Soweroy was prospering, and who had taken up his own work on the Way of the World,, and by what means the new man had obtained his introduction.

So he thought that, by way of a morning's amuse- ment, he would walk along to Pump-court, and give Sep a bit of his mind. "Who would have thought that the man would have been such a scoundrel ?" he muttered to nimself, rather In atone of astonishment than vexa- tion, as he turned out of Clare Market into St. Clement's-inn, and thus made his way to the Strand.

There was consternation at Pumn-court, Temple. The laundress could not obtain admittance to Sep Sowerby's chambers, and the door had obviously been locked from the inside, for the key remained. "I think there must be something wrong, sir," she said to Christison.

Dick put his back to the door, and exerted all his strength; but he might as well have attempted to overthrow one of the walls of Newgate.

"You wait here," he said, ."BAW I will fetch locksmith."

As he was coming back he met a medical friend, and dragged him along. "Syncope," said the medical friend, as he ex- amined the figor .vhich was bent over Sep Sowerby's desk.

"You had better fetch a policeman," said Chris- tisOn to the horrified laundress.

Then he took up the letter which Sep had been writing when he died. It began, "Dear Annie," and then it went on to speak of the mistake they had made. They must have believed that they loved each other because they were so much together. "I am now beginning to look at life with other and saner eyes -" And there the letter came to an end. "The cur!" muttered Christison, as he crumpled the letter in his palm. The sounds of the heavy steps of the policeman, mingling with the mournful garrulity of the laun- dress's voice, were on the stairs. "Poor girl! Poor girl!" said Christison. "He was a brute, but she shall keep her faith in him. This letter, at least, shall never reach her eyes, or tear her heart. To have been saved from him is some- thing to wank lirou cot, out sue 41134g0 914 &mpg bim to tkp eud."

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Baptist Church, Colborne-straet.

At this church on Sunday evening the Pastor (Rev. H. Dunnico) preached a sermon to young people, taking for his text the church motto for the year, 'Hold that fast which thou hast, that no man take thy crown" (Rev. iii.ll). Religions liberty was mentioned as being one of the things which should be held fast, it having been purchased by the blood of the martyrs. Indivi- duality and 'independence ,of character should be developed and kept. The preacher emphasized the im- portance of thinking for one's self instead of accepting other men's views, and of having one's own convictions and standing by them.

===== 0002601_19040109_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
St. Peter's Church.

The annual tea p rty arranged by the members of the Mother's Meeting was held' on Monday night, when about 120 sat down to a good substantial repast. The tables were presided over by Mesdames Percival, Barn- forth, Lingley, Adams, Taylor, Burdett, Heacock, and Cowdell, and the Misses Young, Adams Percival, and others. After the tables were cleared various games were indulged in, interspersed with songs by Miss Adams, Mrs Yeomans, and Mr. Sumner, which were much appreciated. During the evening parcels of clothing kindly given by the Warrington and South Lancashire Needlework Guilds and Mrs. Comber, we.:e distributed by Mrs. Percival. The Vicar spoke for a few minutes, congratulating the members of the class upon their regular attendance during the year. The party dispersed at 10.30, all having spent a most enjoyable evening. The piano was presided over by Mrs. Adams and Mr. F. Webster.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. .

- At the meeting of this society on Sunday the chair was occupied by Mr. George Wallington, who, at the outset, wished the members "A Happy New Year ! " The address was delivered by Mr. J. A. Yonge, M.A., of Wakefield. He drew some useful lessons from the parables recorded in the 16th chapter off St. Luite.Refer- ring to the work of the P.S.A. movement, he said it was the duty of all connected with it to help and rescue those who were either wilful transgressors or who were crushed and beaten by circumstances. Unless the P.S.A. Society was an aggressive one in this respect it wasnot worth its salt. If life was to be beneficial it must be sacrificial. If they were willing to make heroic sacri- fices then they would become powerful for good. Let them put their hearts into the work and become mission- aries for the society and they wouldlknowsomething of the supreme joy of those who served Him.—Phe Nev. John fonge announced that three new members had been enrolled, bringing up the membership to 532. The Savings Club had now 76 members and the sum of £24 Gs. 6d. had been withdrawn during the Christmas liOliaays. He trusted that in the coming year they would see a great revival of P.S.A. work. Mr. A. Griffiths very efficiently sane "Th 9 Star of Bethlehem." Service for Young People.

Mr. J. A. Yonge, M.A. (Principal of the Northern Congregational School, Silcoates, Wakefield) and eldest son of the Rev. John Yonge of this town, conducted a service for young people in Wycliffe Church, Bewsey- street,on Sunday morning. There was a large congrega- tion. Suitable hymns were sung and an address was delivered by Mr. Yonge, based on the words "1 go to my Father' (John 16c. 28v.) He asked his hearers never to forget their final goal and to always remember their God to whom they went. He alluded to what the late Mr. Glacbtone called "The inestimable blessing of unremitting industry" and pointed out that the egreat value of work was not in what it eamed but in the education of body, mind and soul which attended it. At the close, bibles were distributed to 30 members of the pastor's catechumen class. The Rev. John Yonge, in distributing the bibles, stated that he commenced the class in 1881 and since that year 662 young people, on reaching the age of 15 years, had passed through the class. Some of these former members were scattered in nearly every part of the habitable earth. He trusted they would always carry with them the influence of the class and the remembrance of its purpose.

===== 0002601_19040109_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
A Welcome and a Farewell.

At the communion service held at Wycliffe Congrega- tional Church on Sunday evening, a hearty welcome was extended to the Rev. Harry Wall, late of Nottingham College. who has accepted the call to be the assistant minister at Wycliffe Church, with the special oversight of Cockhedge Mission. The Pastor welcomed Mr. Wall on behalf of the members and expressed the hope that his labours would be fruitful. At the close, an affec- tionate farewell was taken of the Rev. A. A. Dignum, who is returning to his work in the mission field at Salem, India. The pastor in bidding him farewell, referred to Mr. Dignum's early associa- tion with Wycliffe Church and Sunday school and to his ordination at Wycliffe in 1882, prior to his departure for India as a missionary of the London Missionary Society. Since that time, Mr. Dignum had been in a special degree, Wycliffe's representative in the foreign mission field,—The congregation joined in singing "God be with you till we meet again."—The Rev. A.A. Dignum addressed the gathering and stated that he received a shock at the Mission House, when the Secretary described him as a veteran of the mission field. He had hitherto looked upon himself as a young worker, but he supposed that when one had given 21 years of service he must have left his youth behind. He remembered with gratefulness and joy his early associations with Wycliffe and although he had been greatly distressed during his furlough by the death of his wife and the prospect of leaving his children behind in England, still he went out again to the mission field with a hopefulness for the success of his work in India.—At the usual service which preceded the Communion, the Pastor (Rev. John Yonge)preached on the 'Wycliffe Motto for 1904—" In His favour is life" (Psalm 30, sv). The usefulness and practical in- fluence of the motto he said depended upon its being constantly displayed. Still it needed more than being kept in sight, for it would be of no avail if it was re- garded as a scripture text, which must as a matter of course lie good and true- There was a possible danger in their familiarity even with scripture texts, and, as the old proverb had it, " familiarity breeds contempt." IWhat had that motto to• say for practical use in this life? It was a plain statement of a spirit ual and truth- ful fact of the relations existing between man and God. They must takel God as their standard in life; a task which was not so impossible as it might eeem. Patience and perseverance would lead them to success.

PRISON GATE MISSION WORK.

The Police Court and Prison Gate Mission of the Liverpool Diocesan Church of England Temperance Society is now one of the largest agencies of its kind in the United Kingdom. Its area of operations is CO extensive with the Diocese of Liverpool, and includes the city of Liverpool, the boroughs of Bootle, South- port, St. Helens, Wigan, Warrington and Widnes together with the Petty Sessional Courts in thecourrtry districts and the Recorder's and Assize Courts. It also has Agents who meet prisoners on discharge every morning at Walton prison from both sides of the river Mersey. Prisoners are also occasionally met at Preston and Man- chester gaol gates. Three of its missionaries are also Agents of discharged Prisoners' Aid Societies in their respective districts namely, the South-West Lancashire and Wirral, the Preston and Mid-Lancashire, and the Manchester and Salford Hundred. During the month of December these missionaries made 261. visits to the police courts conversed with 1,165 persons in the courts and cells and visited 647 of them subsequently in their homes. Sixty women, girls and boys were sent back to friends, taken to refuges or provided with employment, 63 persons were attended to in the courts at the request of the Magistrates, and 64 at the desire of the court officials and the police. In 146 instances persons visited the offices and homes of the missionaries to be assistsd to work &c. In 64 instances second hand clothing, was provided to enable the recipients to ob- tain work. At the Mission room near Walton Prison breakfast, advice, help &c, were given to 712 persons on discharge, 25 tickets for work at the Society's Firewood and Mat Factory, Bootle were given to men, 55 pessons were visited in their abodes from the prisons 175 per- sons signed the pledge of total abstinence, 45 meetings were addressed. There were also 46 non-court capes attended to, persons wholad not been in a police court or prison but for whom friends and relations sought , iihe assistance of the missionaries. There are many othe-c items of work.

The Murderous Malaria Mosquito.

Sir,—ln the otherwise clear and succinct account of my lecture on Mosquitoes and Malaria, published in your last issue, there it one slip of such importance that I crave your permission to make further explana- tion, since it may be due to lack of precision on my part. I am reported to have said that the blood-parasites -which cause-malaria 'were introduced into the blood by mosquitoes who obtained them in their food from puddles and swampy districts," whereas I hoped I had made it clear that our present knowledge only enables us to say that the 'mosquito transfprs the parasite from one hitinaii.being to another. Malaria is due to minute animal organism dwelling as parasites in the red compuscles of the blood. Certain of them increase in size and finally break up into numerous spores (a form of young) whioh attach them selves to other corpuscles and ultimately become full- -sized parasites. In this way the parasites multiply in the patient's blood, and when they have become suffi.- ciently numerous the act of breaking ap into spores causes the outbreaks of fever with which dwellers in the tropics and elsewhere are only too familiar. Certain other members, however, instead of breaking up into spores, assume forms known as gametocytes, and when a mosquito bites a person suffering from malaria it is practically certain to draw into its body along with the blood a large number -of these gametocytes. lathe mosquito's body the gametocytes pass through I complicated changes which flually result in the produe- tion of a prodigious number of young parasites, called in this stage." blasts." Now if this particular mosquito should bite a person previously free from malaria,these "blasts" are carried down the insect's trunk while it is biting along with the irritating fluid which it injects, and get into the unfortunate victim's blood. Here they attach themselv es to the red corpuscles, increase in size, and many of them ultimately break up into spores which attack other corpuscleras we have already seen. Some of them, however, instead of breaking up into spores become gametocytes (as did their predecessors) which may be down up by some other blood-thirsty mosquito, to go thrbugh the same complicated changes and to have their numerous progeny transferred to yet another person—and so the disease is spread:- • This is, in brief, the life-history-of theparasite as we understand it at present, and it. seems to be a. complete statement, for no stage in the transformations is wanting: Experiment, too,seems to prove that malarial fever is never developed except as the result of the bite -of a mosquito belonging to the genus Anopheles which has previously bitten a per- son suffering from the disease, and the parasite which produces the fever is only known to occur in the bodies • of mosquitoes, and in the blood of malarial patients. The question of the origin of the .parasite and its habits is still a problem for biologists, and likely to remain so. It will therefore be evident that there are no grounds for stating that the mosquito obtains the parasite in its food from puddles anti swamps (wherein it feeds only in the larval stage) and my desire to cor- rect this impression must be my excuse for troubling you.—l am, dear sir, faithfully yours, E. E. LOWE. Plymouth, January sth, 1904.

Warrington and the Drink Question.

Sir.—The very sad case reported in the public press of a boy drunk at 14, furnishes food for reflection and makes one feel disposed to ask what is being done to minimise this terrible evil in our town. On Christmas Eve I chanced to walk towards the centre of the town just as the pitblic• houses were being closed and the frightful number of drunken men and women met with -will not readily be forgotten. In about six minutes' walk T. counted more than 50 staggering along. Some were being held up on either side by more sober corn- ; panions ; others.were unable to get along at all. One young woman about 20 years of age was drunk • screaming and situ-ring, with a baby in her, arms. We are told that the publicans are not respon- sible for the drunkenness that exists, but I saw several men leave public-houses staggering drunk,carryialg large ...stone bottles of beer. Twoof them were so drunk that

theyletthem fall and they were broken and the contents ,spilled. Hearing , a crash further on some called out "There's another bottle gone." I may say this is ,no new thing in Warrington, as I have more than once -seen the same thing happen afterclosing time on Sunday afternoons. A friend remarked to me on the number of • young girls from 17 to 20 years of age who were under' the influence of drink. Is there any wonder that I hear in one inebriate home in Lancaster for women, I during the past year out of 14 inmates no fewer than 5 were from;Warrington ? I ,firmly believe from what ' 'I saw that there were in the streets of the Borough ; more than 200 drunk and yet so far as I know not more I -than one or two cases were dealt with by the police. I People are asking why is this? Ts it to keep down the statistics of drunkenness] or is it as sdine think,that the magnificent building on which so many ,thousands have been spent, is too far away. to be of any service? Any how, the fact remains that a fearful amount of drunken- ness is allowed to go on unchecked. By and by we shall be todid that drunkenness is decreasing while just the oppositers the case,especially with regard to women. The question then is who is respimsible? The publicans have rezently been taken to task by Justice Grantham because of the awful amount of crime brought before him as the direct result of drink. While one is ready -to admit there are many who try to conduct a difficult business in a satisfactory manner, yet it is equally ,certain there-are those who do not seem to care as long as they are alle to do a good business. This, of course, .may arise from the fact that under the tied house system they aro compelled to do so much business, and .competition being so keen owing to the excessive number of houses they are obliged to sell as much as possible 'in order to get a living. The police also should be called upon to do their Juty without fear or favour. Drunkenness is an offence against the law and should be dealt -with as such. It is all very well' to prosecute small boys for playing a game of pitch and toss,but why not the men and women who by their foul language and disorderly conduct while under the influeoce of drink, make it im- possible for respectable people to pass along our streets with any degree of pleasure. My impression is that if -this were done we should soon see a very desirable change, but so' long as they are allowed to break the law with impunity,so long will the evil continue and grow. Men one may ask what are the clergy and ministers of the churches doing with regard to this matter? Do they realise the tremendous responsibility that rests upon them.? There are homes within a atone!s threw of nearly every church in Warrington whieh axe a shame and disgrace to civilization and they are made so by drink. With the exception of the vicar of iSt Paul's, who is nothing if not outspoken, I do not know of any clergyman or minister who during -the festive season protested against this unchristian method of observing a Chris- tian festival. Again with the exception of Wycliffe Church—all honour to them for it—l am not aware that any church in the town has en Adult Society where men and women anxious to reform can be brought in and helped to do so, and be also a means of preventing others from falling. If I might suggest a text for a Sunday's discourse it would be Proverbs 24c., 11-12 v. We should then be able to judge as to what our Chris- tian leaders think and feel as to their responsibilities in this matter. There are other sides to this question which I should have -liked to have touched upon. These shall, however, be deferred till another time, and by your kind permission I shall be pleased to bring them before your readers.—Yours faithfully. OBSERVER.

Councillor Maginn and Warrington Bridge

Sir.—The Town Council passed a resolution at their monthly meeting on Tuesday to increase the width of Warrington Bridge to 51 feet by the erection of two cantilever footpaths, outside the existing parapets at an estimated cost of £4,523. Now as I pointed out to you in my letter of last week, this is in my opinion a useless expenditure of public money on a bridge that every member of our Council must be aware, cannot be made to fulfil the requirements of our town in the future, though it may have served our purpose very in- differently in the past. The gentlemen who voted for this resolution must know that it would be im- possible to carry out the condition of this resolution in opposition to the wishes of the firms interested in the navigation of the river above the bridge, unless by defending protracted litigation at • an enormous expenditure of public money, which is the I most useless of all expenditures, besides damaging ; vested interests of the Corporation in the navigation of our waterway. For in the first place the Corporation of Warrington have spelt over £5,000 of public money in• immuring fore-shore rights and building wharves on the upper reaches of Ow river from the bridge to the weir. ' I believe I arecorrect in stating that these are the only, fore-shore rights the Corporation of Warrington own on -the river Mersey and this which should have been a valuable and profitable investment to our town has been practically worthless to us,owing to the unnavigable state • of the\ riverunder the Bridge.N ow when this has been the state of things in the past, what will it be in the future ;when 1-,y the new proposed river diversion scheme, the surface of the water is raised by two feet six inches, making the depth of the river under the bridge over eleven fe.et.? Now let me say in passing

that it isto the interests of Warrington to get the depth of the river increased as much as possible and I believe if the present scheme is carried out as sug- gested it will be one of the best improvements the town has secured within the present generation. Now sir to prove my contention let me state that a steamer that could pass under the bridge with a cargo of say 250 tons with the water in the river the above mentioned depth, could not return empty, for the space between the surface of the water and the top of the arch will only be a little over fourteen feet, and yet a 250 ton cargo steamer is only a very small boat indeed. It may be pointed out,however,that the railway bridge is still 6 inahes lower than our town bridge and this is so. But the railway bridge which is already condemned and must be replaced by anew one in the near future, is a bridge of one span of about 100 feet, whereas our bridge consists of three arches and only the centre one is navigable with a span of about 50 feet. The two abutments, the centre supports of our bridge, occupyl6 feet of the river and cause the water to rush through the centre arch with such force that it is extremely dangerous to navigation. My contention is this, if we are to gain any real benefit from the new river diversion scheme both the bridges must be removed. Now if this is the navigable con- dition of the river with the bridge as it now stands what would it be if you add 20 feet to its width by the proposed cantilever footpaths? It will be still more dangerous to navigation. Now I think it is quite clear to anyone why I am opposed to what I consider useless expenditure, for though I am not one of those who are nervous about spending public or private capital where it is likely to bring in a return or be a benefit in any way, lam opposed to spending money on useless, or to put it mildly, temporary improvements, when the money is wanted for permanent and profitable works, which will increase our rateable value, and be an everlasting benefit to the community. This brings me to my contention in my last week's letter, our necessity for two new bridges, so that we can divert the traffic over them when the time comes for us to erect a new one where the old bridge now stands, besides giving us the additional connections with the Cheshire and Lancashire part of the Borough. My views on the proposed bridge at Walton, I shall have to leave over to some future occasion, and content myself with stating my intention of bringing forward a motion at the next monthly meeting of the Council to immediately erect a bridge at Howley connecting that part of the town with Latchford by means of a road through Victoria Park, the erection of which I am sure should be opposed by no one, as it would be a permanent benefit to property owners and manufac- turers alike, and at the same time find work for the large number of Unemployed who are at the present time depending on us for their existence.—Yours &c. JOHN MAGINN.

A meeting of the Warrington Union Assessment Committee was held in the Board Room at the Work- house yesterday. Mr. A. J. Jolley presided, and there were also present :—Messys. S. Davies, G. Pilling, William Houghton, J. Billinge, A.Jolley, J.C. Sutton (clerk), William Owen (valuer), and W. 11. Woodcock (assistant overseer).
