THE BROOKLANDS_ HOTEL.

Mr. H. J. IVE—sT—s;l7o-0K (on behalf of Mr. James Spencer Earle, the tenant of the Brooklands Hotel, Lovely-lane) appealed %against the assessment which stood at £1.50 gross and £127 10s. rateable. Mr. Earle, he said, was a tenant under Messrs. Greenall Whitley and Company. During the alterations which ,were still being made the rental was £4O a year, but when completed the rental would be £7O. He was not going to argue the principle, he continued, of how a tied house ought to be rated because the principle was not clearly estab- lished and the law was conflicting. Hewes prepared to admit at once that the money paid as rent was not the true rateable value of thehotel. The landlord let it at a less rent because of the extra profit he received on the beer and the 'tenant paid less rent because of the increased price he had to pay for beer. It was here where the unknown quantity come in and the question arose what additional value should be paid to represent the extra amount paid for beer in view of the reduced rental. Although the law was complicated the court of appeal had laid down a certain definite line which should beadopted forarriving at the basis of the true value of a tied public-house and it had said they must obtain the evidence of experts who would compare the house in question with similar houses, similarly situated in the same place and similarly built. The position and capability of the house for doing this trade must also be considered and the question must be asked what the premises would be reasonablyexpected to let for. The CLERK: As a free house?

Mr. WESTBROOK : Yes, as a free house. The Court of Appeal also Said that the question of the tenant's earnings or his profits ought not to be taken into con- sideration, except perhaps a,s a last resort. He did not propose to produce expert evidence. It was not that they had anything to hide but because on principle he objected to do it. He hoped to show on the evidence of the tenant that the assessment of the house at £l5O gross was unreasonable and excessive. If the assess ment were based on the supposition that the house was selling 15 barrels a week he could assure the committee that nothing approaching that quantity was sold. At the present time the Brooklands •Vas not capable of doing so large a trade, Then it must be remembered that the licensing of this house was still a source of heartburning to some of the residents in the neighbourhood and he suggested that the gross value of the house was not more than £lOO.— Mr. Earle was then called and said that under present conditions he would not like to pay more than £7O a year rent if the house werea free one. He did a good outdoor trade when he first opened but it had since fallen off. A big outdoor trade was done in the district but he was not doing it. The alterations to the house were not yet complete. The committee decided to re- duce the assessment to £l2O gross and £lO2 net until April next when it will be restored to the original figure of £l5O gross and £127 les Od rateable.

play of eloquence that evening. He agreed with every word Councillor Maginn had said with respect to the building of a new bridge at Howley. WARRINGTON BRIDGE.

TO BE WIDENED 13? CANTILEVERS.

COUNCILLOR MAGINN'S COUNTER PROPOSAL

A long discussion took place in the Council Chamber at the monthly meeting of the Town Coun- cil on Tuesday, respecting a proposal of ;;the General Purposes Committee that Warrington. Bridge be widened by means of cantilevers. The matter was brought before this Committee on the 21st of Decem- ber by Alderman Smethurst and it was then unani- mously passed. Since then Councillor Maginn has brought forward a counter-proposal in Which he suggests the construction of a new bridge across the Mersey at Howley and thereby providing a new outlet into Cheshire and giving access to Victoria Park to the inhabitants at the Howley end of the town. He further suggests another bridge across the Mersey at Walton. The matter had aroused consider- able interest and the Mayor allowed the subject to be fully debated, notwithstanding the fact that the pro- posals of Mr. Maginn were not on the agenda. Alderman SMETHURST formally moved the adoption of his scheme. He would reserve what remarks he had to make, he said, until he saw what opposition there might be to his scheme. Councillor ROGERS seconded. Councillor MAGINN observed that everyone was now pretty well aware of his feeling respecting this pro- posed outlay of £4,000 or. £5;000 on the proposed footpaths to be constructed outside the existing War- rington bridge. The original estimate of Mr. Webster for constructing a footpath ift. 6in. wide was £3;047 10s., and for 10ft. £4,523 18s. There was a big differ- ence, and he felt that it was just like throwing the money into the river. They had put some of it in I already. He proposed, in opposition to the scheme of the committee, the erection of a bridge at Howley. I No matter how good, broad or gieat the bridge might be at Bridge Foot it would not be sufficient to accom- modate the traffic between Lancashire and Cheshire. He could see nothing to prevent Warrington making greater strides than any other town in Lancashire. They had the finest natural facilities of any town in the North of England, they had the best direct rail- way system to all parts, the Ship Canal was second to none on earth, and over and above that they had a tidal river as good as the Clyde was 50 years ago. (Hear,-hear.) ''Still they had been practically at a standstill, and a great deal of the stagnation was attribu- table to their niggardly means of spending money. There were many who said they spent too much, but theyspant as a miser spent and were penny wise and pound foolish. If they wished to make the town what itshould be they should encourage its growth. The tendency of the town was to grow on the Cheshire side. The town was closed in the matter of extension, so far. as Sankey Bridges was concerned. The same remark applied to Longford and he did not think Manchester road was a natural outlet.. Then in what direction could they extend? The answer was "On the Cheshire side." There they had a rising ground and a good fall and why therefore, not spend money on providing a new outlet into Victoria Park which had already cost them a.good sum of money. The original cost of the park was £lO,OOO the interest and sinking fund cost us between £3OO and £4OO per annum. He agreed that it was a wise thing to purchase the park and provide an additional recreation ground, but at present we were making very little use of it. We received as rentals £72 lOs Od, and when the hall was let it brought in an additional sum of £6O but still there was a deficiency of £266 per annum Rhich had to be made good by the ratepayers. Were they going to-pay interest and sinking fund for the next 20 years for the benefit of a future generation? He thought it would be better if we derived a little benefit ourselves instead of leaving it to those who came after us. He proposed that they construct a bridge at Howleyand open out the park as well as making a new outlet into Cheshire. The original estimate of Mr. Webster was £17,789 10s. but with the reduction in the cost of iron and steel of £1 per ton the estimate was reduced by £2,582 odd, and he contended that the structure might be erected for £15,261. Suppose it cost £lB,OOO, well to borrow that sum and the payment of the sinking fund that would ' be necessary it would burden the rates just a penny in the £. Let them compare that cost with the advan- tage the town would get in making a new entrance into Cheshire with a circular route for their tramways besides. He would, perhaps, be criticized, and some would say that this was not a time for the town to make improvements in the direction he had named, but he contended that the time to make those improvements was when things were bad, as the work would find em- ployment for the unemployed. The greatest charity they could extend to the workingman was to give him work and money for it. That was better than acting on the degrading principle of charity. However,whether he carried his amendment or not for the erection of a bridge at Howley, he should bring it forward again a month hence, and he would do all one man could to make the town see the necessity of his motion. The MAYOR : What is your amendment?

Councillor MAGI-Ns read it as follows :—"That the minutes of the General Purposes Committee of the 21st November be approved with the excention of the minute relating to the widening of Warrington Bridge." Councillor GOUGH seconded the amendment. The time was inopportune, he said, to carry the resolution referred to in the minutes. If the Corporation were called upon to alter the bridge in consequence of the effect of the alterations to the river by the Ship Canal Company, the cost of constructing cantilevers would be thrown away. He believed they ought to carry another bridge if possible or wait until they saw what difference the Ship Canal proposals would make. Councillor JA.MES EVANS said he agreed With Corm- , cillor Gough that the time for widening the bridge was inopportune. After the tramways had been inaugurated a " Bridge " committee was appointed and Alderman Smethurst was made conveuer of it. The Council under- stood that that committee would be called together to consider the bridge question thoroughly. He thought he was quite safe in saying that the committee had never been called together, and the Council had had no report tendered to them from it. Therefore, why the question should again come before the Council in the form it did without. any reason being assigned for it, he was at a loss to understand. He might remind the Council that there was a sub-committee of the Street Improvement Committee appointed to consider the , question of the advisability of. a bridge at Howley , Quay for the purposes of opening out Victoria Park and making a highway out of Lancashire into Cheshire. The Street Improvement Com- mittee considered that by a bridge there .one- third of the traffic would be relieved from Warrington Bridge, and it would also give the advantages named by Councillor Maginn. The sub-Committee had • also con- sidered the widening of Howley-lane and negotiations were still in progress. They, had been in corres- pendence with Messrs. Rylands Bros. with the object of rounding off the corner at Howley-lane, and they were proposing to make some alterations in Egerton- street. The council would therefore see that the sub-committee had already done something towards the proposed new road into Cheshire. He quite endorsed the statement that there had been no logical reason assigned why there should not be another way across Arpley meadows when they could easily obtain the land. It might be that the Council did not know how full the hands of the street Improvement Committee were in regard to the better approach to Warrington bridge on the Latchford side. They had at present two schemes which would very materially effect the widening of the bridge, but it would not be to the interests of the town or themselves to fully explain ' them. At any rate he could assure the ratepayers and the Council that the Street Improvement Committee were doing their best to ensure a permanent andsuffici- ent approach to ,Warrington bridge from the Latchford side. Further, if the Manchester Ship Canal Company carried out the diversion of the river and materially interfered with the navigation under thp bridge, he took it that that Council would see that Warrington got their "poling of flesh." (Hear, hear.) By-and-by they expected that the tramway traffic over the level cross- ings—which everybody considered a nuisance—would increase and sooner or later they would have to deal with it. When they did that, then would be the time to consider the bridge. He was told there was another motive for springing the question upon the Council. although he would not say it was true, and that was that there were persons in the town who would oppose the proposed widening because it would facilitate the natural opportunities some of them had by • the freer and better use of the river. If there was any truth in that it was sordid eo the core. Alderman SMETHURST (excitedly) : I object. think it is wrong to insinuate anything of that kind. Councillor JAMES EVANS: If you begin to jump about like that I shall begin to think the cap fits 'some- . where. (Laughter.) I did not insinuate against any- body. Continuing, he said that if there was such a sordid thing in the mind of any member of the Council he asked him, in the interests of the common good of the town, to let it drop for ever. In deciding the question they must consider the best interests of the town only, and he would ask Alderman Smethurst to leave the question over for a while Alderman SMETHURST : No, no. ' I mean to go to the vote. Councillor 3.-AMES EVANS: Then I must ask the members of the Council, in all common sense, to vote against it Ifor the time being. Continuing, he said he had had experience of the bridge for 40 years, and during that time there had not been one serious accident on it. He maintainad that the traffic on .the bridge was only what was common in the narrow streets of the town, and in the face of the things looming' before us every- where, he advised them to bide•their time. He had had some experience of. London Bridge— Alderman PiEnromr : Do you mean the one at Stockton Heath? NOTES ON On GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Rugby Football.

By " Ovalist."

The Warrington men were idle on Saturday the frost lasting just a few hours too long to permit of play at Leigh and Warrington. A thaw set in earl* in the afternoon but the matches had been declared off by then: Monday, the 18th of April has been decided upon as the day for the replay at Leigh. -

St. Helene will fill their South West lAncashire fixture at Warrington on Easter Afonday, and this should.prove an attractive feature for the hiiday folks.

Play was possible at most places on Saturday where the ground had been protected. Salford were at home for the third time in succession on Saturday and they fairly rubbed it into the "gallant youths" from Batley. The latter put up a fair show for about ten minutes, but when Salford had scored they seemed to conclude that they might go through the remaining 60 minutes as easily as possible. The Reds had a glorious time and before the close they had piled up 41 points to nil. Lomas was allowed to do almost as he liked, with the result that he crossed the Batley line four times and kicked half-a-dozen goals. The spectacle must have been some coMpensation to. the Weaste crowd for the blow on Boxing Day. '

Oldham maintained the lead by a good win at Hull while Bradford smote the Widuesians cruelly. Park Avenue has been a sad ,QT mad. for Lancashire clubs this season. Leeds and Brought-in Itangers divided the p Ants at Eleadingly, so there was not much shuffling of places.

In the " Athletic News" on .llonday,ltr. J. H. Smith reviewed the progress of the League clubs during the first half of the season in which he said: Only five of the eighteen clubs have played exactly the same number of matches as they- had at the end of December last season, and it will, therefore, give a truer estimate of the situation if the comparison is made on the points lost. On this basis, Leeds are 18 p Ants better off than were St Helms (whose place they have taken) a year age,Hull Kingston lovers coming next with 12 to the goad. Hunslet have improved their position by 8 points, Warrington by 7, and Bradford and Oldham 3 e:ach, while. Keighley, although at the bottom of the class, are 2 points .better than Brig- house were twelve months ago. On the other hand, Swinton, in spite of their recent improvem-nat, are still eight points behind last year's record ; Halifax and Leigh have each dropped seven points, and Run- corn, Wigaß, and Batley six each. Huddersfield and Hull are three down, and Widnes two. Salford, with 23 points for 17 matches, are, .curiously enough exactly in the same position as last year.

He also contraSts the performances of clubs in opposition to others of their own county. It will surprise my readers to learn that on this reckoning Warrington are champions of Lancashire. They have only lost three points against teams from their own county. The tabulated results up to December 31 are :

THE NEW VICAR OF ST. JAMES' LATCHFORD.

institution and Induction.

The Rev. Herbert Boiseau Firth, ILA.. for over nine years senior curate at Runcorn Parish Church, was on Saturday afternoon instituted by the Bishop of Chester to the living of St. James' Latchford, rendered imcant by the death of the Rev. F. R. Pearson. There was a good congregation and the service was most impressive. Evensong to the end of the third collect was taken by the Rev. W. Scholes,the lessons being read by the Revs. W. Stanton and F. S. Thetford. The Rev. H. V. Pigot acted as chaplain to the Bishop. The service was fully choral, Mr. E. Davies presiding at the organ. In the course of anaddress the Bishop, alluding to the atten- dance of parishioners and neighbouring clergy, the work of preparation undertaken by the church wardens, and the services rendered by the organist and choir, said they were all signs and tokens of that good fellow- ship which belonged to the very idea and purpose of the Church and without which the work of the Church could not poSsibly progress Speaking to the choir, his lordship emphasised the central thought that the value of their worship and the acceptableness of their office in leading the worship of the Church must depend vitally and radically upon the fact that they must always be striv- ing to realize that they were worshipping in the presence of God, that they were speaking before God, and to God, and on behalf of God. They must remem- ber that they were not to supersede or supplant the congregation at large, even in the fmusical part of worship. Their new vicar, dontinued his lordship, had come from a good school, and had worked for a long series of years in one of the most difficult parishes of the diocese, a very populous parish so that he was quite prepared to undertake the duties of the large and growing parish of St. James'. He had been under wise and experienced guidance, and had proved himself to be thoroughly loyal and efficient and, to use the very significant words of the incumbent, a I thoroughly comfortable assistant curate. Speaking of the duties of the new Vicar, the Bishop referred to the conduct of the services of the Church. The Vicar would be ever thinking of the central thought of mae's life in God. He would pay due and reverent attention to things external, for man was body as well as soul, but he would remember that things external were quite subordinate to the higher faculties and destinies of man St. Paul taught them to view all things external, especially in their exuberance, as the rudi- mentary lessons of outward things. Therefore, a large use of outward things and rudimentary lessons belonged not to the advanced stage of religion but to the beginners in religion. Viewing the mcdter from that point of view the Vicar would learn due proportion, making much of outward things in their august simplicity but always remember- •ing that God was a Spirit and they that worshipped Him must worship Him in spifit and in truth. As a faithful and true pastor, he was sure their Vicar would lay special stress upon the care of the schools, making it his delight, as well as his duty, to be constantly in the schools, taking an interest in every branch of the work, and, of course, a very special in- terest in the central religious teaching and worship of the schools. It was impossible to lay too much strebs upon the blessed opportunity which a parish clergyman had when he was enabled to go and shepherd the little ones of the flock discreetly, wisely and affectionately in the schools more especially associated with their own Church. _

Tie—new Vicar having taken the oath of allegiance to the King, and the oath of canonical obedience to the Bishop of the dtocese, and made the declaration of assent to the thirty-nine articles of religion and the Book of Common Prayer, and also the declaration against simony, he was instituted to the cure of souls in the parish by the Bishop. The ceremony of induc- tion was then conducted by the Rural Dean of Frodsham (Canon Maitland Wood, of Runcorn), the new incumbent tolling the bell to signify to the parishioners his tak- ing possession of the Church. _

The Rev. H. B. Firth preached his first sermon as Vicar of St. James' on Sunday evening, when there was a large congregation. His text was" Jesus Christ, the same yesterday, and to-day, and for ever (Heb.xiii, 8.) When he reflected, said the preacher, that the popula- tion of the parish was 6,000, that he was single-handed, and had no clerical help, he was overwhelmed with thought of the responsibility which had been placed Upon him. He had, however, resolved, by the grace of God, to do his best. He felt that, just as in the past, the Good Shepherd had placed labourers in large and difficult parts of His vineyard, had given them strAngth to do the work, and had crowned their efforts with His blessing, so he must look in the same direction. The public were at times apt to make a mistake and to think that the clergy were the only people who were to work for God. If he was enabled to help them as parishioners, surely they ought to be able to help him, and together they might work for the promotion of God's glory during the year. The Rev. Canon Virood,writing in the" Runcorn Parish Church Magazine" says :—Mr. Firth came to us a newly ordained deacon In May, 1891. How steadfastly, conscientiously and well he has laboured until this time von blow yourselves. His very honest and conscienti- ous wish to do his duty made him the object of attack rather more than two years ago, an attack which he felt keenly,which perhaps served its purpose at the tirne,brit which you and I even now strongly resent. As is the case with most unreasonable and unjustifiable attacks of a personal nature, the result has enshrinedhim the more securely in our affectionate regard. We have trusted him, and we do trust him, and we shall feel his loss, and at the same time we congratulate the Parish of St. James', Latchford, that she will have a man, a most loyal member of the Church of England, who will have come to seek the welfare (4 its inhabitants. May the blessing of God be with him and them. In the coarse of a letter to his p,arishioners,published in this month's "Parish Magazine " the new Vicar says :—" It is at least in one respect fortunrete for me that I should take up my work amongst you at the beginning of January, for it enables me t Irough the medium of the "Parish Magazine" to wish you a Happy New Year. "A Happy New Year," if by that is meant a year's freedom front anxiety and care, is more than we can expect to have, a year blessed with a smile of God we can all have if we earnestly seek Him and strive to follow in the footsteps of His dear Son. As you are aware I was on the 2nd of January instituted to the care of souls in this parish; the presence of the Bishop of the diocese and his kind words were a great encour- agement to me in view of the responsibility which has been laid upon me and of which I run very conscious. ,At present. there are in the parish more than 11e thou- sand souls and at present I have no clerical assistance and therefore with all the earnestness I can, I ask your sympathy, zour practical asSistance and your prayers. An immense task lies before me and without the bless- ing of God and without the co-operation of many will- ing helpers the task can never be accomplished. St. James'. is a large church in a large parish; with the new year let it be our united endeavour to make the influ- ence which shall radiate from St. James' correspond- ingly large. The strain of undertaking single handed, the work of a new and large parish is to me a very real one, and for the present, qt any rate, I do not feel pre- pared to continue the week-day celebrations of the Holy Communion except on Holy-days. I run hoping, how- ever, whenever U is not an impossibility to arrange for daily Aorning Prayer at 10 o'clock.

===== 0002601_19040109_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
WELCOMING THE NEW VICAR.

Connl't in St. Jamas' Schools.

Additiorul int•m4.st w lent to a concert held on Thursday in aid of St. James' Sunday schools by the presence of the new Vic ir of the pirish (Rev. Herbert B. leirtb)lwho presided. There was' a very large attend- ance and the Vicir r3ceived a hearty welcome.

Mr. BURGESS, at. the outset, said as that was Mr. Firth's tirit appearance at a public gathering in the parish he had ben asked to sty a few worth by way of introduction. Re must confess that he accepted the invitation with considerable reluctance and much hesitation. If there was one member of the congrega- tion who ought not to hive been asked to perform thatdutylie was that one. As many of them:knew during the past five years it had fallen to his lot on some three or four occasions, either to welcome an in-corning vicar or to bid God speed to a departing one, on their behalf. He sincerely hoped he i'yould not be called upon to per- form a similar duty. Last year was an extremely try- ing one for them as a 'congregation- They lost in that year a Vicar whourt in a very short period they had learnt to love and whose death cast a gloom over the whole parish and caused a shock from which they had scarcely recovered. For nearly six months they had been sheep, as it were, without a shepherd, and naturally much loss had been occasioned to the parish in conse- quence of it. There was mach leeway to be made up. He should not like that opportunity to pass without I expressing on his own behalf, as he was sure he did on behalf of the churchwardens, and indeed of eveg3. mem- ber of the congregation, the deep debt of gratitude they owed to 11tr. Moxon, who for four months con- ducted the services in the church. His devotion and self-denial had been beyond all praise. He did not I i think t right to let that opportunity pass without expressing fully their obligation to him. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Firth had been in the parish but a very short time and so far they had seen very little of I him. But he thought he might say that Mr. Firth had I already made a favourable impression and if he (Mr. I Burgess) might venture to prophesy he would say that that impression would grow as time went on. (Ap- plause.) His belief was that they had got a Vicar, who, as their Bishop said last Saturday, would give his 1 life—by that he meant his best both of body and mind I —for the people ' of this parish. (Applause.) The parishioners were the Vicar's first care and it was to them hehad come to minister. Those who lived outside I the parish, but who were members of the congrega- tion, must not expect too much of the Vicar, indeed they could only hope to see him occasionally, Mr. Firth had come there with the fixed determination to extend Christ's kingdom in the parish, and it was the I duty of everyone who was a member of the congrega- tion to do everything he or she could to assist the loVicar. Let them !ally round him and show that what-

ever points of difference there might be between them, they were prepared to co-operate on the principle of concessien, not in the spirit of those whose fault it was to give little and take much, but in the true Christian spirit that it was better to give than to receive. (Hear, hear.) He did not think anyone would be called upon to give up any vital principle and therefore they could with perfect consistency help on any- good work the Vicar might inaugurate. The church, schools, and mission all needed their special care and attention. It was a great pleasure to those who had borne very largely the heat and. burden of the day to see some who had been lately and he said it in all charity—slack and lukewarm, re- turning to the fold. He would ask them not to let this be a mere flash in the pan, or the enthusiasm of a moment because a new Vicar had come, but to let it be a determined endeavour to take their part and do their share of the work of the parish. (Hear, hear.) Let this new year be commenced and continued with real earnest work in the parish and there would be happier times in store for them. It. only remained for him to in- troduce their new Vicar and ask them to give him a cordial reception. (Applause.) He hoped Mr. Firth's tenure of the living would be a blessed one and that he would never have any cause to regret having come to St. James' parish, Latchford. (Applause.) The Rev. H. B. FIRTH, who on rising was greeted with loud applause said he thanked them very heartily for the very kind reception which they had just given him. Their presence there that night in such large numbers was to him a very real encouragement and the words they had just listened to from one of their la. representatives was to him also a matter for great en- couragement. There was a certain number of people who imagined that to take up new work as a clergyman and to settle down in a new parish was a matter of comparative ease. But he could assure them—speaking from his experience of the last three months—that the comparative ease was absent. They little-realised what troubles and anxieties had to be ,gone through. There was, in the first place, the leaving of old friends and acquaintances who had become very dear to one, and lae could say with all truthfulness that it was a very real pain to him to sever a connection which lasted nearly ten years in his old parish. Then therewas the trouble of coming into a new house and trying to settle down las best one could. Thirdly, there was the far greater work to be undertaken, and a matter involving great anxiety and consideration, that of undertaking the I charge of a new parish. It had been to him a matter of very great anxiety indeed. Sometimes when he had been thinking of the tremendous responsibility which would rest upon his shoulders as Vicar of a large parish such at that, he had felt veryanxious indeed. He could assure them that the gathering that night and the words he had heard had been to him very encouraging. He thanked them all very heartily for, the reception given him. That gathering showed very well and plainly, the interest which they took in the Sunday schools in the parish, for he understood the proceeds of the con- cert were to be devoted partially to the prize fund for the Sunday scholars. It seemed to him that there must be a very large interest in the Sunday ochools and that in itself was a very encourag- ing thing. Mr. Burgess had exhorted them to be so good as to try and support him (the Vicar). He could assure them that he would do his very best to co-oper- ate with them. He was:certain that if they co-operated and recognised that they were in the same boat and that they had got to take a hand at pulling the oar, and if theyraade up their minds that God helping them they would do their best, then he was sure the work of the church in that parish would go forward to the mutual satisfaction of all concerned and he believed it would be crowned with the blessing of God. (Applause.) An interesting programme of vocal and instrumental music was submitted. The vocalists included some of the most prominent and accomplished local artistes and they all secured hearty receptions. Solos were sung by Mrs. A. Booth (soprano), Miss Mabel Henshall (contralto),Miss Eckersley(mezzo soprano), Mr. Arthur Griffiths (tenor) and Mr. Edward Pownall (bass). Mrs. Booth was recalled for her fine rendering of the difficult trill song" Star of the Twilight" and complieçl with"On the Banks of Allen Water." "The Chinese Love Song." was sung by Miss Mabel Henshall with a vivacity and piqueness that secured for her an encore to which she responded. Miss'Eckereley who had arranged the con- cert was also recalled for her spirited rendering of 'Angus Macdonald," which she sang by request. In response to repeated applause she saeg "Sing me to Sleep," Messrs. Griffiths and Pownall were heard to advantage in their respective solos and also sang the duet "Love and War." The instrumental portion of the programme included a violin solo by Mr Taylor, who was accompanied on the piano by Miss Taylor, and two pianoforte solos by Miss P. Houghton,. L.R.C.M. Miss Azatha Jones gave two pretty dances. and VraS encored for her graceful movements in " Taran- tella." The humour of the evening wm supplied by Mr. Hallard, whose 'sketches were a.- great success, and led to recalls. The duties of accompanist were dis- charged by Mr. A. Trow.—The proceedings terminated with a vote of thanks to the Chairman, the artistes, and Miss Eckersley for arranging the concert.

‘, PRACTICAL ENGINEER" ELECTRICA L POCKET BOOK.—We have received a copy of the fifth edition of this useful annual, Many additions and improvements have been introduced in the present issue. Amongst these may be specially mentioned the standards of electrical conductors as recommended by the Cable Makers Association, some additional facts in reference to conduit wiring, and some new types of electrical instruments. The contents have been carefully revised and brought up to date, and in short, no effort has been spared to add to its value and usefulness as an every-day work of reference for all engaged in electrical undertakings.

The Ship Canal Appeal Case. The appeal of the Manchester, Ship Canal Company against the decision of Nit% Justice Byrne with refer- ence to the dredging of the river at 'Warrington would, in all probability have been heard next week. By the sonsent of both parties the hearing has been adjourned until the third week of this month.
